贝利太太羞怯地笑了，“她像她可怜的父亲；她有她自己的主意。”米丽亚姆安然坐在潮水之中……女人是从哪里得来那种洞察人格的目光，得来那种非同寻常的预言力的？她自己一丝一毫也没有。也许是做母亲的缘故；马利亚就把这一切事存在心里，反复思想。不；姨妈们往往也有，甚至比母亲们更强；贝利太太自持得那样出色，对孩子们来说，她既是母亲，又是姨妈。她端详着那颗被岁月锐利蚀损的小脑袋，凭着她自觉的、亲口承认的意志力，昂着、笑着，浮在人们所谓的“命途多舛”的波涛之上；是的，米丽亚姆想着，还凭着她不自知的公正与慷慨，还有一种不可动摇的正义感。这么多年的苦苦撑持与左支右绌没有败坏贝利太太；她本该去做法官，而不是做冈纳先生的总管。……正义是个女人；蒙着眼；从内里观看，不被表象引偏；男人们发明伦理体系，却掂不出人格的分量；他们没有个性，只有对抽象体系的顺从或不顺从；然而，承认一个女人的力量——承认她认识的任何一个女人的力量——就不可能不沦为奴隶；除了患难之时，也不可能同女人交往；可在她这趟有意为之、走进这间小屋的远足里，她是自由的；她的全部生活远远躺在别处，晒着自由；向外再向外铺展，无边无际；每一处空间、每一个部分都是一只斟满的杯，谁的手也搭不上……那个小个子男人不过是一副古怪的外国嗓音，过不多久就会惹起她的不耐……而方才，他显得那么近。……她是在用贝利太太的眼睛看他吗？贝利太太会说：“哦，是啊，我觉得他是个很不错的小个子男人。”除了他作为殷实寄宿客的那点出众，在贝利太太眼里，他没有任何可资突出之处；她会敬重他的学问，却把学问看作某类男人才有的品质；而不知道那有几分是境遇的偶然。她会看穿它；她永远不会拜倒在它面前。

===Original===
But the distant vision of the free life was not Mrs. Bailey’s vision; there was something there she could not be made to understand, and would in any way there were words that tried to express it, certainly not approve. Yet why did it come so strongly here in her room? The sense of it was here, somewhere in their intercourse, but she was unconscious of it. … Miriam plumbed about in the clear centre—where without will or plan or any shapely endeavour in her life, she was yet so strangely accepted and indulged. Mrs. Bailey was glancing back at her from the depths of her abode, her face busy in control of the rills of laughter sparkling in her eyes and keeping, Miriam knew, as she moved, hovering, and saw the fostering light they shed upon the world, perpetual holiday; the reassuring inexhaustible substance of Mrs. Bailey’s being.

===Chinese===
可那自由生活的远景，不是贝利太太的远景；那里头有某种东西，没法叫她明白，任凭用什么言语去表达，她也一定不会赞成。可为什么它偏偏在她这间屋里来得这样强劲？它的意味就在这里，在他们这场交往的某处，只是她自己浑然不觉。……米丽亚姆在清澈的中心一带下探——在这里，她一生中不曾有过意志、计划或任何成形的努力，却这样奇异地被接纳、被纵容。贝利太太正从她居所的深处回望她，脸上忙着管住眼睛里闪闪发亮的一缕缕笑意，并且保持着——米丽亚姆在近旁盘旋时知道，也看见那笑意投向世间的滋养之光——永远的假日；贝利太太存在里那叫人安心、取之不竭的实质。

===Original===
“It’s Sissie I worry about,” said Mrs. Bailey. Miriam attended curiously. “She’s like her dear father; keeps herself to herself and goes on; she’s a splendid little woman in the house; but I feel she ought to be doing something more.”

“She’s awfully capable,” said Miriam.

“She is. There’s nothing she can’t turn her hand to. She’ll have the lock off a door and mend it and put it on again and put in a pane of glass neater than a workman and no mess or fuss.” Miriam sat astonished before the expanding accumulation of qualities.

“I don’t know how I should spare her; but she’s not satisfied here; I’ve been wondering if I couldn’t manage to put her into the typing.”

“There isn’t much prospect there,” recited Miriam, “the supply is bigger than the demand.”

===Chinese===
“我发愁的是西西，”贝利太太说。米丽亚姆好奇地听着。“她像她亲爱的父亲；独来独往，闷头做事；在家里她是个顶用的小女人；可我觉得，她该做点更大的事。”

“她能干极了，”米丽亚姆说。

“她能。没有她伸不上手的活儿。她能把门锁卸下来、修好、再装回去，还能上块玻璃，比工匠还利索，又不脏又不乱。”米丽亚姆坐在那里，对着这一层层累积起来的才干叹为观止。

“我不知道少了她可怎么办；可她在这儿不满足；我一直在琢磨，能不能想办法叫她去学打字。”

“那一行没什么前程，”米丽亚姆背诵似的说，“供大于求。”

===Original===
“That is so,” assented Mrs. Bailey; “but I see it like this; where there’s a will there’s a way and one has to make a beginning.” Mrs. Bailey had made up her mind. Quite soon Sissie would know typewriting; a marketable accomplishment; she would rank higher in the world than a dental secretary; a lady typist with a knowledge of French. That would be her status in an index. No doubt in time she would learn shorthand. She would go capably about, proud of her profession; with a home to live in, comfortably well off on fifteen shillings a week; one of the increasing army of confident illiterate young women in the city; no, Sissie would not be showy; she would bring life into some office, amongst men as illiterate as herself; as soon as she had picked up “yours to hand” she would be reliable and valuable. … Sissie, with a home, and without putting forth any particular effort, would have a place in the world. …

===Chinese===
“是这样，”贝利太太附和道；“可我是这么看的；有志者事竟成，总得有个开头。”贝利太太已经拿定了主意。用不了多久，西西就会打字了；一门可以换饭吃的本事；她在世上的地位会高过一个牙医诊所秘书；一位懂法文的女士打字员。这便是她在名册里的身份。毫无疑问，假以时日她还会学速记。她会利利落落地四处做事，为她的职业自豪；有个家可以住，一星期十五先令，过得宽宽裕裕；城里那支日益壮大的、自信而不学无术的年轻妇女大军中的一员；不，西西不会张扬；她会把生气带进某间办公室，带到同她一样不学无术的男人中间；等她一学会“贵函敬悉”那套话，她就是靠得住、顶用的人了。……西西，有个家，又不必付出什么特别的努力，便在世上占有一个位置。……

===Original===
“I’ll make some inquiries,” said Miriam cheerfully. Mrs. Bailey thanked her with weary eagerness; she was flushed and flagging; the evening’s work was being cancelled by the fascination which had allowed her to go on talking. She admitted a return of her neuralgia and Miriam, remorseful and weary, made her lie down again. She looked dreadfully ill; like someone else; she would go off to sleep looking like someone else, or lie until the morning, with plans going round and round in her head and get up, managing to be herself until breakfast was over. But all the time, she had a house to be in. She was Mrs. Bailey; a recognised centre. Miriam sat alone, the now familiar little room added to the strange collection of her inexplicable life; its lamplit walls were dear to her, with the extraordinary same dearness of all walls seen in tranquillity. She seemed to be responding to their gaze. Had she answered Mrs. Bailey’s murmur about going to bed? It seemed so long ago. She sat until the lamp began to fail and Mrs. Bailey appeared to be going to sleep. She crept out at last into the fresh still darkness of the sleeping house. On the first floor there was a glimmer of blue light. It was the street lamp shining in through Mr. Shatov’s wide-open empty room. When she reached her own room she found that it was one o’clock. *Already* he had found his way to some horrible haunt. She wrapped her evening round her, parrying the thought of him. There should be no lesson tomorrow. She would be out, having left no message.

===Chinese===
“我去打听打听，”米丽亚姆快活地说。贝利太太带着倦怠的热切谢了她；她红着脸，蔫了下来；这一晚的操劳，正被那股容得她一直说下去的迷恋抵消掉。她承认神经痛又犯了，米丽亚姆又悔又倦，让她重新躺下。她看上去病得厉害；像另外一个人；她会像另外一个人似的睡去，或者睁着眼躺到天亮，脑子里计划转了一圈又一圈，然后起床，撑着做自己，一直撑到早饭结束。可自始至终，她有一座宅子可待。她是贝利太太；一个公认的圆心。米丽亚姆独自坐着，这间如今已熟悉的小屋，又添进她那难言其所以然的生涯的奇妙收藏里；它被灯光映亮的墙壁是她所珍爱的，带着人在安宁中看见的一切墙壁所共有的那种非同寻常的珍爱。她仿佛正在回应它们的凝视。她可曾回答过贝利太太那句关于就寝的喃喃低语？仿佛已是很久以前的事了。她一直坐到灯火将尽、贝利太太似乎睡去。她终于蹑手蹑脚走出来，走进沉睡宅子那清新而静止的黑暗。二楼有一线幽蓝的微光。是街灯照进沙托夫先生那间门户大敞的空屋。回到自己房里，她发现已经一点钟了。*这么快*他就摸进了什么可怕的去处。她把这一晚裹在身上，挡开关于他的念头。明天不该有课。她会出门去，不留口信。
---

===Original===
When she came in the next evening he was in the hall. He came forward with his bearded courteous emphatically sweeping foreign bow; a foreign professor bowing to an audience he was about to address. *Bitte verzeihen Sie*, he began, his rich low tones a little breathless; the gong blared forth just behind him; he stood rooted, holding her with respectful melancholy gaze as his lips went on forming their German sentences. The clangour died down; people were coming downstairs drawing Miriam’s gaze as he moved from their pathway into the dining-room, still facing her with the end of his little speech lingering nervously on his features. He was in his frock-coat and shone richly black and white under the direct lamplight; he was even more handsome than she had thought, solidly beautiful, glowing in shapely movement as he stood still and gestureless before her, set off by the shapelessly moving, dinner drawn forms passing into the dining-room. She smiled in response to whatever he may have said and wondered, having apologised for yesterday, in what way he would announce to her the outside engagement for this evening for which he was so shiningly prepared. “Zo,” he said gravely, “if you are now free, I will almost immediately come up; we shall not wait till eight o’clock.” Miriam bowed in response to the sweeping obeisance with which he turned into the dining-room, and ran upstairs. He came up before the end of the first course, before she had had time to test in the large overmantel the shape of her hair that had seemed in the little mirror upstairs, accidentally good, quite like the hair of someone who mysteriously knew how to get good effects.

===Chinese===
第二个晚上她进门时，他正在门厅里。他迎上前来，行一个蓄着胡须的、彬彬有礼、幅度阔大的外国式鞠躬；一位即将开讲的外国教授向听众鞠躬。*请原谅*（Bitte verzeihen Sie），他开口了，那浑厚的低音微微气促；开饭的锣正好在他背后轰然响起；他扎根似地立定，用恭敬而忧郁的目光挽留着她，嘴唇继续做着德语句子的口型。锣声的余音散尽；人们陆续下楼，牵走米丽亚姆的目光，他从他们的走道让开、退向饭厅，仍旧面对着她，那篇小演说的尾巴还紧张地挂在他的眉眼间。他穿着长礼服，在直射的灯光下焕发出浓郁的黑与白；他比她想的还要俊美，结结实实地漂亮，一举一动光彩照人；他一动不动、不带手势地立在她面前，被那些身形臃肿、被晚饭牵着走进饭厅的人影一衬，愈发夺目。她微笑着，回应他不知说了些什么的话，心里琢磨着：既然已为昨天道过歉，他会以怎样的方式向她宣布今晚的外出应酬呢——为那应酬，他显然已焕然一新地准备停当。“那么（他说成‘Zo’），”他庄重地说，“如果您现在有空，我几乎立刻就上来；我们不必等到八点钟。”米丽亚姆朝他那阔大的俯身答了一礼，看他转身进了饭厅，便跑上楼去。头一道菜还没上完他就上来了，来得那么快，她还来不及在那面大壁炉镜里检验自己发型的样子——在楼上那面小镜子里，它似乎无意间弄得好好的，很像某个神秘地懂得如何弄出好效果的人的头发。

===Original===
“I have been sleeping,” he said in wide cheerful tones as he crossed the room, “all day until now. I am a little stupid; but I have very many things to say you. First I must say you,” he said more gravely and stood arrested with his coat tails in his hands, in front of the chair opposite to hers at a little table, “that your Emerson is *most*-wonderful.”

Miriam could not believe she had heard the deep-toned emphatic words. She stared stupidly at his unconscious thoughtful brow; for a strange moment feeling her own thoughts and her own outlook behind it. She felt an instant’s pang of disappointment; the fine brow had lost something, seemed familiar, almost homely. But an immense relief was surging through her. “No—Ree—ally, most-wonderful,” he reiterated with almost reproachful emphasis, sitting down with his head eagerly forward between his shoulders, waiting for her response. “Yes, *isn’t* he?” she said encouragingly and waited in a dream while he sat back and drew little volumes from his pocket, his white eyelids downcast below his frowning brow. Would he qualify his praise? Had he read enough to come upon any of the chills and contradictions? However this might be, Emerson had made upon this scholarly foreigner, groping in him with his scanty outfit of language, an overwhelming impression. Her own lonely overwhelming impression was justified. The eyes came up again, gravely earnest. “No,” he said, “I find it most difficult to express the profound impression this reading have made on me.”

===Chinese===
“我一直在睡觉，”他跨进屋来，声音开朗愉快，“睡了一整天，直到现在。我有点儿蠢；可我有许许多多话要对您说。首先我必须告诉您（他说成‘say you’），”他的声音更庄重了，两手捏着礼服后摆，在她对面那把椅子前站定，隔着一张小桌，“您的爱默生*最*——奇妙。”

米丽亚姆没法相信自己听见了这些低沉、着重的字眼。她怔怔地盯着他那种不自觉的、沉思的前额；奇异的一瞬，她仿佛感到自己的思想和自己的眼界就藏在那额头后面。她感到一阵短短的失望之痛；那美好的额头失去了点什么，显得熟悉，几乎家常了。可一股巨大的宽慰正在她周身涌动。“不——真——的呀，最奇妙了，”他又说了一遍，带着近乎责备的郑重，坐下来，头急切地探在双肩之间，等她的回应。“是啊，*可不是*吗？”她鼓励地说，随后梦一般等着，看他靠回椅背、从口袋里掏出几册小小的书，白眼睑垂在蹙起的眉头之下。他会给他的赞誉打折扣吗？他读的份量够不够撞上那些冷峭与矛盾之处？不管怎样，爱默生已给这位学养深厚的外国人留下了压倒性的印象——他揣着那套寒酸的语言行头，在爱默生身上摸索。她自己那孤独的、压倒性的印象，得到了辩护。那双眼睛又抬起来，庄重而恳切。“不，”他说，“我发觉，最难不过的，是表达这次阅读所给我的深刻印象（他说成‘have made’）。”

===Original===
“He isn’t a bit original,” said Miriam surprised by her unpremeditated conclusion, “when you read him you feel as if you were following your own thoughts.”

“That is so; he is not himself *philosophe*; I would call him rather, *poète*; a most remarkable quality of English, *great* dignity and with at the same time a most perfect simplicity.”

“He understands everything; since I have had that book I have not wanted to read anything else … except Maeterlinck,” she murmured in afterthought, “and in a way he is the same.”

“I do not know this writer,” said Mr. Shatov, “and what you say is perhaps not quite good. But in a manner I can have some sympa-thaytic apprysiacion with this remark. I have read yesterday the whole day; on different omnibuses. Ah. It was for me *most*-wonderful.”

“Well, I always feel, all the time, all day, that if people would only read Emerson they would understand, and not be like they are, and that the only way to make them understand what one means would be reading pieces of Emerson.”

===Chinese===
“他一点儿也不原创，”米丽亚姆说，被自己这脱口而出的结论吓了一跳，“读他的时候，你仿佛是在跟着自己的思想走。”

“正是如此；他自己并非*哲人*（philosophe）；我宁愿称他为*诗人*（poète）；一种极其出色的英语品质，*极大*的庄严，同时又有一种最完美的质朴。”

“他什么都懂；自从有了那本书，我就再不想读别的了……除了梅特克林，”她事后想起似的低声补了一句，“某种意义上，他是一路的。”

“我不认识这位作家，”沙托夫先生说，“您说的话也许不很对。不过在某种意义上，我对这话能有一点同感的领会（他把法语的 sympa-thaytic apprysiacion 念得走了调）。我昨天读了整整一天；在好几辆不同的公共马车上。啊。它对我是*最*——奇妙的。”

“唉，我总是觉得，时时刻刻，一整天，只要人们肯读爱默生，他们就会明白，就不会是现在这个样子；而要让他们明白我的意思，唯一的办法就是读几段爱默生。”
===Original===
“That is true; why should you not do it?”

“Quotations are feeble; you always regret making them.”

“No; I do not agree,” said Mr. Shatov devoutly smiling, “you are wrong.”

“Oh, but think of the awful people who quote Shakespeare.”

“Ach-ma. People are, in general, silly. But I must tell you you should not cease to read until you shall have read at least some Russian writers. If you possess sensibility for language you shall find that Russian is *most*-beautiful; it is perhaps the most beautiful European language; it is, indubitably, the most rich.”

“It can’t be richer than English.”

“Certainly, it is richer than English. I shall prove this to you, even with dictionary. You shall find that it occur, over and over, that where in English is one word, in Russian is six or seven different, all synonyms, but all with *most* delicate individual shades of nuance … the abstractive expression is there, as in all civilised European languages, but there is also in Russian the most immense *variety* of natural expressions, coming forth from the strong feeling of the Russian nature to all these surrounding influences; each word opens to a whole *aperçu* in this sort … and what is most significant is, the great richness, in Russia, of the people-language; there is no other people-language similar; there is in no one language so immense a variety of tender diminutives and intimate expressions of all natural things. None is so rich in sound or so marvellously powerfully colourful. … *That* is Russian. Part of the reason is no doubt to find in the immense paysage; Russia is zo *vast*; it is inconceivable for any non-Russian. There is also the ethnological explanation, the immense vigour of the people.”

===Chinese===
“这话不假；您为什么不做呢？”

“引文是软弱无力的；引了总要后悔。”

“不；我不同意，”沙托夫先生说，虔敬地微笑着，“您错了。”

“哦，可想想那些引用莎士比亚的可怕的人吧。”

“哎呀嘛。人们，总的来说，是愚蠢的。可我必须告诉您，在您至少读过一些俄国作家之前，不应停止阅读。倘若您对语言有感受力，您将会发现，俄语是*最*——美的；它也许是欧洲最美的语言；它无疑是最丰富的。”

“它不可能比英语丰富。”

“当然，它比英语丰富。我要向您证明这一点，哪怕用字典来证明。您将会发现，这样的情况一而再、再而三地出现：英语里一个词的地方，俄语里有六七个不同的词，全是同义词，却个个都有*最*精微的个别色调差异……抽象的表达在那里，正如在一切文明的欧洲语言里那样；可俄语里还有最浩繁的*种种*天然表达，从俄罗斯天性对周遭一切影响的强烈感受里涌出来；这样一个个的词，各自洞开成一整个*观照*（aperçu）……而最有意味的是，俄国民间语言的极大丰富；再没有别的民间语言与之相似；任何一种语言里，都没有如此浩繁的亲昵小称，没有对一切自然事物如此亲密的表达。没有哪一种语言在声音上如此丰富，在色彩上如此奇异地浓烈。……*那*才是俄语。部分原因，无疑要到那浩大的风土里去找；俄国如此*辽阔*（他把 so vast 念成‘zo vast’）；对任何非俄罗斯人来说，这都是难以想象的。还有人种学上的解释：那人民精力之旺盛。”
===Original===
Miriam went forward in a dream. As Mr. Shatov’s voice went on, she forgot everything but the need to struggle to the uttermost against the quiet strange attack upon English; the double line of evidence seemed so convincing and was for the present unanswerable from any part of her small store of knowledge; but there must be an answer; meantime the suggestion that the immense range of English was partly due to its unrivalled collection of technical terms, derived from English science, commerce, sports, “all the practical life-manoeuvres” promised vibrating reflection, later.

===Chinese===
米丽亚姆梦一般向前走着。沙托夫先生的声音滔滔不绝，她忘了一切，只记得必须竭尽全力，抵挡这场对英语的安静的奇袭；那双重论证听来如此有力，此刻凭她那点小小的知识库存，无处可以应战；但必定有答案；与此同时，另一个念头——英语的浩瀚幅员，部分源于它那无可匹敌的技术词汇库，来自英国的科学、商业、体育，“一切实际的生活运作”——则许诺了日后一阵震颤的思索。

===Original===
But somewhere outside her resentful indignation, she found herself reaching forward unresentfully towards something very far-off, and as the voice went on, she felt the touch of a new strange presence in her Europe. She listened, watching intently, far-off, hearing now only a voice, moving on, without connected meaning. … The strange thing that had touched her was somewhere within the voice; the sound of Russia. So much more strange, so much wider and deeper than the sound of German or French or any of the many tongues she had heard in this house, the inpouring impression was yet not alien. It was not *foreign*. There was no barrier between the life in it and the sense of life that came from within. It expressed that sense; in the rich, deep various sound and colour of its inflections, in the strange abruptly controlled shapeliness of the phrases of tone carrying the whole along, the voice *was* the very quality he had described, here, alive: about her in the room. It was, she now suddenly heard, the disarming, unforeign thing in the voice of kind commercial little Mr. Rodkin. Then there was an answer. There was something in common between English and this strange language that stood alone in Europe. She came back and awoke to the moment, weary. Mr. Shatov had not noticed her absence. He was talking about Russia. Unwillingly she gave her flagging attention to the Russia already in her mind; a strip of silent sunlit snow, just below Finland, St. Petersburg in the midst of it, rounded squat square white architecture piled solidly beneath a brilliant sky, low sledges smoothly gliding, drawn by three horses, bell-spanned, running wildly abreast, along the silent streets or out into the deeper silence of dark, snow-clad wolf-haunted forests that stretched indefinitely down the map; and listened as he drew swift pictures, now north, now south. Vast outlines emerged faintly, and here and there a patch remained, vivid. She saw the white nights of the northern winter, felt the breaking through of spring in a single day. Whilst she lingered at Easter festivals in churches, all rich deep colour blazing softly through clouds of incense, and imagined the mighty sound of Russian singing, she was carried away to villages scattered amongst great tracts of forest, unimaginable distances of forest, the vast forests of Germany small and homely … each village a brilliant miniature of Russia, in every hut a holy image; brilliant colouring of stained carved wood, each peasant a striking picture, filling the eye in the clear light, many “most-dignified”; their garments coloured with natural dyes, “the most pure plant-stain colours,” deep and intense. She saw the colours, mat and sheenless, yet full of light, taking the light in and in, richly, and turned grievously to the poor cheap tones in all the western shops, clever shining chemical dyes, endless teasing variety, without depth or feeling, cheating the eye of life; and back again homesick to the rich tones of reality. … She passed down the winding sweep of the Volga, a consumptive seeking health, and out into the southern plains where wild horses roamed at large, and stayed at a lodge facing towards miles and miles of shallow salt water, seagull haunted, and dotted with floating islands of reeds, so matted and interwoven that one could get out from the little shallow leaky fishing-boat and walk upon them; and over all a crystal air so life-giving that one recovered. She heard the peasants in the south singing in strong deep voices, dancing by torchlight a wild dance with a name that described the dance. …

===Chinese===
可在她那愤愤不平的义愤之外的某处，她发觉自己毫无怨怼地向前探身，迎向一样非常遥远的东西；那声音继续着，她感到一个崭新而奇异的临在，触到了她的欧洲。她听着，凝神望着，远远的，此刻只听见一个声音，向前流去，没有连贯的意义。……那触到她的奇异之物，就在那声音之中的某处；俄国的声音。比德语或法语的声音、比她在这宅子里听过的许多语言里的任何一种，都更奇异、更宽、更深，这倾注进来的印象却并不异己。它不是*外来的*。它内里的生活，与从她内里升起的对生活的感觉之间，没有藩篱。它表达了那份感觉；用它语调的丰饶、深沉、多变的声与色，用它那些音句奇异而骤然受控的成形，载着整体向前——这声音*就是*他所描述的那种品质，就在这里，活着：在她四周的这间屋里。她此刻突然听出来，它正是那位和气、做小本生意的罗德金先生嗓音里那件解除武装的、非外来的东西。那么就有答案了。英语同这门在欧洲孤悬的奇异语言之间，有某种共通的东西。她回过神来，倦倦地醒到此刻。沙托夫先生没发觉她方才的缺席。他在谈俄国。她不情愿地把疲沓的注意力交给那已在她心里的俄国；芬兰（Finland）之下一狭条寂静的、阳光普照的雪原，圣彼得堡卧在正中，浑圆敦实的白色方形建筑，结结实实垒在明亮的天空底下，低低的雪橇平滑地滑行，三匹马拉着，铃铛横挂，并辔狂奔，沿寂静的街道，或奔进黑暗的、积雪的、狼群出没的森林那更深的寂静——森林顺着地图无边无际地延伸下去；她一面听他飞快地画出一幅幅图景，忽而北，忽而南。浩大的轮廓淡淡浮现，四处有一小块一小块地方留了下来，鲜艳。她看见了北方冬季的白夜，觉出春天在一天之内破土而来。她还在教堂的复活节庆典里流连，满堂浓丽的色彩透过香雾柔柔燃烧，想象着俄国歌咏的宏大声浪，人已被带到散落在茫茫林海间的村庄——那森林之广袤不可思议，德国的大森林相形之下又小又家常……每座村庄都是俄国的一幅鲜亮缩影，每座木屋里都有圣像；染色木雕的绚丽色彩，每个农人都是一幅夺目的画，在清亮的光里充满眼界，许多位“最尊严”；他们的衣裳用天然染料着色，“最纯粹的植物染色”，深沉而浓烈。她看见那些颜色，无光的哑色，却满含光，把光一寸寸收进去，丰丰润润；她痛心地转念想到西方一切商店里那些可怜的廉价色调，聪明闪亮的化学染料，无穷无尽撩人的花样，没有深度，没有感情，骗走眼睛里的生活；又思乡似的回到现实那丰润的色调里。……她顺伏尔加河（the Volga）蜿蜒的浩荡河身而下，一个寻求疗养的痨病人，漂出至南方的平原，野马成群漫游；她住进一间面向绵延数英里浅盐湖的旅舍，鸥鸟盘绕，湖上星星点点浮着芦苇岛，盘结交织得密密实实，人可以从那条小小的、浅浅的、漏水的渔船上下来，在岛上行走；笼罩这一切的空气水晶般澄澈，予人生命，叫人痊愈。她听见南方的农人们用高亢深沉的嗓音歌唱，在火把光里跳一种狂野的舞蹈，舞名便道出了那舞本身。……

===Original===
Throughout the recital were vivid words, each a picture of the thing it expressed. She would never forget them. Russia was *recognisable*. So was every language … but no foreign sound had brought her such an effect of strength and musical beauty and expressiveness combined. That was it. It was the strange number of things that were together in Russian that was so wonderful. In the end, back again in England, sitting in the cold dilapidated room before the table of little books, weary, opposite Mr. Shatov comfortably groaning and stretching, his eyes already brooding in pursuit of something that would presently turn into speech, she struggled feebly with a mournful uneasiness that had haunted the whole of the irrevocable expansion of her consciousness. A *German*, not a Russian ethnologist, and therefore without prejudice, had declared that the Russians were the strongest kinetic force in Europe. He proved himself disinterested by saying that the English came next. The English were “simple and fundamentally sound.” Not intelligent; but healthy in will, which the Russians were not. Then why were the Russians more forceful? What was kinetic force? And … mystery … the Russians themselves knew what they were like. “There is in Russia except in the governing and bourgeois classes almost no hypocrisy.” What was kinetic. … And religion was an “actual force” in Russia! “What is ki—”

===Chinese===
整场叙述里满是鲜活的词，每一个都是它所表达之物的图画。她永远不会忘记它们。俄国是*可以辨认的*。每种语言都是……可从来没有哪一种外国的声音，给过她这样一种力量、音乐之美与表现力合而为一的效果。就是这个。是俄语里汇聚在一起的事物那奇异的数目，才那样奇妙。到末了，又回到英国，坐在那寒冷破败的房间里、摆在小小书本的桌前，倦倦的，对面是舒舒服服呻吟着、伸展着四肢的沙托夫先生，他的眼睛已在出神地追寻着什么，那东西转眼就要化成言语——她软软地同一阵悒悒不安搏斗，那不安一路纠缠着她意识那不可逆的扩展。一位*德国*人种学家——不是俄国人，因而不带偏见——宣称俄国人是欧洲最强的动力。他说英国人位居第二，以此证明自己不偏不倚。英国人“单纯而根本健全”。不聪明；但意志健康，俄国人则不然。那俄国人为什么更有力量？动力是什么？还有……谜……俄国人自己知道他们是什么样子。“在俄国，除了统治阶层和资产阶级，几乎没有虚伪。”什么是动力。……还有，宗教在俄国竟是一股“现实的力”！“什么是动——”

===Original===
“Ah but you shall at least read some of our great Russian authors … at least Tourgainyeff and Tolstoy.”

“Of course I have heard of Tolstoy.”

“Ah, but you shall read. He has a most profound knowledge of human psychology; the most marvellous touches. In that he rises to universality. Tourgainyeff is more pure Russian, less to understand outside Russia; more academical; but he shall reveal you most admirably the Russian aristocrat. He is cynic satirical.”

“Then he can’t reveal *anything*,” said Miriam. Here it was again; Mr. Shatov, too, took satire quite unquestioningly; thought it a sort of achievement, worthy of admiration. Perhaps if she could restrain her anger, she would hear at least in some wonderful explanatory continental phrase, what satire really was, and be able to settle with herself why she knew it was in the long run, waste of time; *why* the word satirist suggested someone with handsome horns and an evil clever eye and thin cold fingers. Thin. Swift was probably fearfully thin. Mr. Shatov was smiling incredulously. If he went on to explain she would miss the more important worrying thing. Novels. It was extraordinary that he should. …

===Chinese===
“啊，可您至少要读几位我们伟大的俄国作家……至少读屠格涅夫（他把 Turgenev 念成‘Tourgainyeff’）和托尔斯泰（Tolstoy）。”

“托尔斯泰我当然听说过。”

“啊，可您要去读。他对人类心理有最深湛的认识；有最神妙的笔触。在这一点上，他升入了普遍。屠格涅夫更纯粹是俄国的，在俄国之外较难领会；更学院气；可他会最出色地向您揭示俄国的贵族。他是个愤世的讽刺家。”

“那他就什么也揭示不了，”米丽亚姆说。又是这一套；沙托夫先生也全盘照收讽刺；把它当成一种成就，值得钦佩。也许，只要她按得住火气，她至少能从某个绝妙的、解说性的大陆短语里，听到讽刺究竟是什么，从而跟她自己把账算清：为什么她知道它到头来终是浪费时间；为什么“讽刺家”这个词叫人想起一个长着漂亮犄角、一只恶毒的聪明眼睛和冰冷瘦手指的人。瘦。斯威夫特（Swift）恐怕瘦得吓人。沙托夫先生正微笑着，一脸不敢相信。他若接着解释下去，她就要错过那更要紧的烦心事了。小说。他竟然会——这真是非同寻常。……

===Original===
“I don’t care for novels. … I can’t see what they are about. They seem to be an endless fuss about nothing.”

“That may apply in certain cases. But it is a too extreme statement.”

“It is extreme. Why not? How can a statement be too extreme if it is true?”

“I cannot express an opinion on English novelistic writings. But of Tolstoy it is certainly not true. *No*; it is not in general true that in fictional representations there is no actuality. I have read with my first English teacher in Moscow a story of your Myne-Reade. There was in this story a Scotch captain who remained for me most typical British. He was *very* fine this chap. This presentation here made me the more want what I have want always since a boy; to come to England.” Was Mayne Reade a novelist? Those boys’ stories were glorious. But they were about the sea; and the fifth form … “a noble three-bladed knife, minus the blades.”

===Chinese===
“我不喜欢小说。……我看不出它们讲的是什么。它们仿佛是无休无止、无事生非。”

“这话在某些情形下或许成立。可这说法太极端了。”

“是极端。又怎样？一句话若是真的，怎么会太极端？”

“对英国的小说写作，我无从表达意见。但对托尔斯泰，这当然不成立。*不*；说虚构作品中没有实在，这普遍地不成立。我当年在莫斯科，同我的第一位英文老师一起读过你们梅恩·里德（Mayne Reade，他念成‘Myne-Reade’）的一个故事。那故事里有位苏格兰船长，在我心里始终是最典型的英国人。这家伙*非常*出色。这里的这一番话，更叫我想要那件我从小男孩时起就一直想要的东西；到英国来。”梅恩·里德算小说家吗？那些男孩子的故事何等辉煌。可它们讲的是海；还有五年级……“一把高贵的三开折刀，刀片全没了。”
===Original===
“There’s a thing called the Ebb-Tide,” she began, wondering how she could convey her impression of the tropical shore; but Mr. Shatov’s attention, though polite, was wandering, “I’ve read some of Gorki’s short stories,” she finished briskly. They were not novels; they were alive in some way English books were not. Perhaps all Russian books were. …

“Ah *Gorrrki*. He is come out direct from the peasantry; very powerfully strange and rough presentations. He may be called the apostle of *misère*.”

===Chinese===
“有一本书叫《退潮》（The Ebb-Tide），”她开口道，想着怎样才能把自己对那片热带海岸的印象传达出去；可沙托夫先生的注意力虽然客气，却已经游移了，“我读过几篇高尔基（Gorki）的短篇，”她干脆地收了尾。那不是小说；它们以某种英国书所没有的方式活着。也许所有的俄国书都是。……

“啊，*高尔基（他念成‘Gorrrki’）*。他直接从农民中走了出来；非常强悍、奇异而粗粝的呈现。他可以称作苦难（misère）的使徒。”

===Original===
… the bakery and the yard; the fighting eagles, the old man at the prow of the boat with his daughter-in-law. … All *teaching* something. How did people find it out?

“But really I must tell you of yesterday,” said Mr. Shatov warmly. “I have made a *Schach-Partei*. That was for me *very good*. It include also a certain exploration of London. That is for me I need not say most *fascinatink*.” Miriam listened eagerly. The time was getting on; they had done no work. She had not once corrected him and he was plunging into his preliminary story as if their hour had not yet begun. She was to share. …

===Chinese===
……面包房和院子；搏斗的鹰，船头那位带着儿媳的老头。……全都在*教*人什么。人们是怎么发现的呢？

“可我真的必须跟您讲讲昨天了，”沙托夫先生热切地说。“我下了一盘*棋*（Schach-Partei，德语：一局棋）。那对我来说*非常好*。其中还包括了一番对伦敦的探索。那对我，不用说，是最*迷人的（他念成‘fascinatink’）*。”米丽亚姆热切地听着。时间越来越晚；他们还没做一点功课。她一次也没纠正过他，而他一头扎进他的开场故事，仿佛他们的钟点还没有开始。她将有份分享。……

===Original===
“There was on one of these many omnibuses a gentleman who tell me where in London I shall obtain a genuine coffee. Probably you know it is at this Vienna Café, in Holeborne. You do not know this place? *Strange.* It is quite near to you all the time. Almost at your British Museum. Ah; this gentleman has told me too a most funny story of a German who go there proudly talking English. He was waiting; ach they are very slow in this place, and at last he shouts for everyone to hear, Vaiter! Venn shall I become a cup of coffee?”

Miriam laughed her delight apprehensively. “Ah, I like very much these stories,” he was saying, his eyes dreamily absent, she feared, on a memory-vista of similar anecdotes. But in a moment he was alive again in his adventure. “It was at London Bridge. I have come all the way, walkingly, to this Café. It is a strange place. Really *glahnend*; Viennese; very dirrty. But coffee most excellent; just as on the Continent. You shall go there; you will see. Upstairs it is most dreadful. More dirrty; and in an intense gloom of smoke, very many men, ah they are dreadful, I could not describe to you. Like monkeys; but all in *Schach-parteis*. That shall be very good for me. I am most enthusiastic with this game since a boy.”

===Chinese===
“在那许多公共马车的一辆上，有一位先生告诉了我，在伦敦哪里能喝到地道的咖啡。多半您知道，是在那家维也纳咖啡馆（Vienna Café），在霍尔本（他说成‘Holeborne’，即 Holborn）。您不知道这地方？*奇怪。*它其实一直就在您近旁。几乎就在你们的大英博物馆旁边。啊；这位先生还给我讲了一个最好笑的故事，说有个德国人到那里，得意洋洋地讲英语。他等着；哎呀，那地方上东西非常慢，最后他大声喊出来，好让每个人都听见：‘服务员（Vaiter）！我什么时候才能得到一杯咖啡（Venn shall I become a cup of coffee）？’”

米丽亚姆提心吊胆又满心欢喜地笑了。“啊，我非常喜欢这些故事，”他正说着，眼睛梦一般放空了——她担心，是落在了一条同类轶事的记忆长廊上。可一转眼，他又活在了他的历险里。“那是在伦敦桥。我一路走着去的，一直走到那家咖啡馆。那是个奇特的地方。真是*晃眼*（glahnend，他夹了句德语）；维也纳派头；非常脏（他说成‘dirrty’）。可咖啡好极了；同大陆上的一模一样。您该去一趟；您会看到的。楼上就非常可怕了。更脏；还有浓得化不开的烟雾，许许多多男人，哎呀他们都可怕得很，我没法向您形容。像猴子；可全都在下*棋*（Schach-parteis）。那对我非常有好处。这游戏我从男孩时起就最热心了。”

===Original===
“Billiards?”

Why should he look so astonished and impatiently explain so reproachfully and indulgently? She grasped the meaning of the movements of his hands. He was a chess-player “a game much older—*uralt*—and the most mental, the only true abstractive game.” How differently an English chess-player would have spoken. She regarded his eager contained liveliness. Russian chess players remained alive. Was chess mental? Pure tactics. Should she declare that chess was a dreadful boring indulgence, leading nowhere? Perhaps he would be able to show her that this was not so. … Why do the Germans call two people playing chess a chess-party? “I have met there a man, a Polish doctor. We have made party and have play until the Café close, when we go to his room and continue there to play till the morning. Ah, it was most-beautiful.”

“Had you met him before?”

“Oh no. He is in London; stewdye-ink medicine.”

===Chinese===
“台球？”

他为什么要露出那么惊讶的神情，又那样又责备又纵容地急急解释？她领会了他双手动作的意思。他是位下国际象棋的人——“一种古老得多的游戏——*uralt*（德语：太古）——而且最需心智，是唯一真正的抽象游戏。”英国的棋手会说得多么不一样啊。她端详着他那急切而收束的活力。俄国的棋手始终是活的。下棋需要心智吗？纯粹的战术。她该不该宣布，下棋是一种可怕的、乏味的放纵，哪儿也通不到？也许他会有本事向她证明并非如此。……为什么德国人把两个人下棋叫作一“局”棋（Schach-Partei）？“我在那儿遇到了一个人，一位波兰医生。我们下了几局，一直下到咖啡馆打烊，然后我们到他房间里接着下，下到天亮。啊，那真是最美不过。”

“你们以前认识吗？”

“哦，不。他在伦敦；研读医学（他说成‘stewdye-ink’）。”

===Original===
“St*uddy*ing,” said Miriam impatiently, lost in incredulous contemplation. It could not be true that he had sat all night playing chess with a stranger. If it were true, they must both be quite insane … the door was opening. Sissie’s voice, and Mr. Shatov getting up with an eager polite smile. Footsteps crossing the room behind her; Mr. Shatov and a tall man shaking hands on the hearthrug; two inextricable voices; Mr. Shatov’s presently emerging towards her, deferentially, “I present you Dr. Veslovski.” The Polish doctor, gracefully bowing from a cold narrow height, Mr. Shatov, short, dumpy, deeply-radiant little friend, between them; making a little speech, turning from one to the other. The Polish head was reared again on its still cold grey height; undisturbed. … Perfect. Miriam had never seen anything so perfectly beautiful. Every line of the head and face harmonious; the pointed beard finishing the lines with an expressiveness that made it also a feature, one with the rest. Even the curious long narrow capless flatly lying foreign boots, furrowed with mud-stiffened cracks, and the narrowly cut, thin, shabby grey suit shared the distinction of the motionless reined-in head. Polish beauty. If that were Polish beauty the Poles were the most beautiful people in Europe. Polish; the word suggested the effect, its smooth liquid sheen, sinuous and graceful without weakness … the whole word was at home in the eyes; horribly beautiful, abysses of fathomless foreign … any kind of known happenings were unthinkable behind those eyes … yet he was here; come to play chess with Mr. Shatov who had not expected him until Sunday, but would go now immediately with her permission, to fetch his set from upstairs. She lingered as he hurried away, glancing at the little books on the table. The Emerson was not among them. The invisible motionless figure on the hearthrug had brought her a message she had forgotten in her annoyance at his intrusion. Going from the room towards his dim reflection in the mirror near the door she approached the waiting thought—Mr. Shatov’s voice broke in, talking eagerly to Mrs. Bailey on the floor below. From the landing she heard him beg that it might be some *large* vessel, quite *voll* tea; some drapery to enfold it, and that the gazz might be left alight. They were going to play chess, through the night, in that cold room … but the thought was gladly there. The Polish doctor’s presence had confirmed Mr. Shatov’s story. It had not been a young man’s tale to cover an escapade.

===Chinese===
“是‘研*究*（studying）’，”米丽亚姆不耐烦地说，沉浸在难以置信的注视里。他同一位陌生人坐了一整夜下棋，这不可能是真的。若是真的，他们俩准都疯了……门开了。西西的声音，还有沙托夫先生带着热切的礼貌笑容起身。脚步声在她背后穿过房间；沙托夫先生同一个高个子男人在炉前地毯上握手；两副缠在一起的嗓音；沙托夫先生的那副随即朝她浮出来，恭敬地，“我向您介绍，维斯洛夫斯基医生（Dr. Veslovski）。”波兰医生从他清冷、窄高的身量上优雅地俯身行礼，沙托夫先生，那矮矮壮壮、光华深沉的小朋友，站在两人中间；作着小小的致辞，从这个转向那个。那颗波兰的头颅又昂在它静止、清冷的灰色高度上；纹丝不动。……完美。米丽亚姆从未见过这样完美漂亮的东西。头颅与面容的每一根线条都和谐；尖尖的山羊胡给线条收了尾，收得那么富有表情，竟也成了一处五官，与其余浑然一体。连那双古怪的长而窄、无靴帮、平塌塌躺着的外国靴子——靴面皲裂着干硬的泥沟——和那身裁得窄窄的、单薄的寒酸灰西装，也分享了那颗静止、受勒的头颅的气度。波兰之美。若那就是波兰之美，波兰人便是欧洲最美的人。波兰的；这个词本身就暗合了那效果，它光滑流润的光泽，蜿蜒优雅而不失筋骨……整个这个词，在那双眼睛里安了家；美得骇人，一口无底的外国的深渊……一切已知的世事，在那双眼睛后面都不可想象……可他就在这儿；来同沙托夫先生下棋——沙托夫先生原以为他要星期天才来——但若蒙她俯允，他这就立刻上楼去取棋具。他匆匆离去时，她留了片刻，瞥了一眼桌上的小书。爱默生不在其中。炉前地毯上那个看不见的、一动不动的身影，给她带来了一样东西——方才因他闯入而生的恼怒里，她忘了它。她朝门边镜子里他那朦胧的映像走去，走近那个等候着的念头——沙托夫先生的声音插了进来，在楼下同贝利太太热切地交谈。从楼梯口，她听见他央求：要一只*大*些的器皿，茶要*满满*的（voll）；要一点布帛把它裹起来；还有，煤气灯（gazz）要让它亮着。他们要下棋，下通宵，在那间冰冷的屋里……可那个念头已在，欢喜地在。波兰医生的到场，证实了沙托夫先生的故事。那不是一个年轻人用来遮掩一场胡闹的编造。

## II / II

===Original===
She hurried through her Saturday morning’s work, trying to keep warm. Perhaps it was nervousness and excitement about the afternoon’s appointment that made her seem so cold. At the end of her hour’s finicking work in Mr. Hancock’s empty fireless room, amongst cold instruments and chilly bottles of chemicals she was cold through. There was no one in the house but Mr. Leyton and the cousin; nothing to support her against the coming ordeal. Mr. Leyton had had an empty morning and spent it busily scrubbing and polishing instruments in his warm little room; retiring towards lunch time to the den fire with a newspaper. Shivering over her ledgers in the cold window space, she bitterly resented her inability to go out and get warm in an A.B.C. before meeting Mr. Shatov in the open. Impossible. It could not be afforded; though this morning all the absolutely essential work could be finished by one o’clock. It was altogether horrible. She was not sure that she was even supposed to stay for lunch on Saturday. The day ended at one o’clock; unless she were kept by some urgent business, there was no excuse. Today she must have finished everything before lunch to keep her appointment. It could not be helped; and at least there was no embarrassment in the presence of Mr. Leyton and the boy. She would even lock up and put on her outdoor things and go down in them. It would not occur to them that she need not have stayed to lunch﻿ ﻿… her spirits rose as she moved about putting things in the safe. She dressed in Mr. Leyton’s warm room, washing her hands in very hot water, thawing, getting warm﻿ ﻿… the toque looked nice in his large mirror, quite stylish, not so home made﻿ ﻿… worldly people always had lunch in their outdoor things, even when they were staying in a house. Sarah said people ought always to wear hats, especially with evening dress﻿ ﻿… picture hats, with evening dress, made pictures. It was true, they would, when you thought of it. But Sarah had found it out for herself; without opportunities; it came, out of her mind through her artistic eyes. Miriam recalled smart middle-aged women at the Corries, appearing at lunch in extraordinary large hats, when they had not been out; that was the reason. It helped them to carry things off; made them talk well and quickly, with the suggestion that they had just rushed in from somewhere or were just going to rush off.﻿ ﻿… She surveyed herself once more. It was true; lunch even with Mr. Leyton and the cousin would be easier with the toque and her black coat open showing the white neckerchief. It gave an impression of hurry and gaiety. She was quite ready and looked about for entertainment for the remaining moments. Actually; a book lying open on Mr. Leyton’s table, a military drill-book of course. No. *What* was this. Wondrous Woman, by J. B. G. Smithson. Why so many similar English initials? Jim, Bill, George, a superfluity of mannishness﻿ ﻿… an attack of course; she scanned pages and headings; chapter upon chapter of peevish facetiousness; the whole book written deliberately against women. Her heart beat angrily. What was Mr. Leyton doing with such a book? Where had it come from? She read swiftly, grasping the argument. The usual sort of thing; worse, because it was colloquial, rushing along in modern everyday language and in some curious way not badly written.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
她匆匆赶完星期六上午的活计，一边想法子保暖。也许是想到下午的约定而起的紧张与兴奋，才让她显得这样冷。在汉考克先生那间空荡无火的房间里做满一个钟头琐细费神的活计，身边是冰冷的器械和寒凉的化学药剂瓶，她整个人都凉透了。屋里除了莱顿先生和那位表弟再无他人；没有什么能支撑她去面对即将到来的煎熬。莱顿先生上午没有病人，一直在他那暖和的小房间里忙着擦洗、抛光器械；快到午饭时分，便揣着一份报纸退到他那窝里的炉火边去了。她在窗口寒地里对着账本打颤，恨恨地怨自己没法出去、没法在赴约之前先到 ABC 茶馆（A.B.C.）里暖暖身子。不可能。这钱花不起；尽管今天上午所有绝对必要的工作一点钟前都能做完。这真是糟透了。她甚至拿不准星期六自己是不是该留下来吃午饭。一天到一点钟就结束了；除非被什么急事留住，否则没有借口。今天她必须在午饭前把一切做完，好去赴约。没法子；好在当着莱顿先生和那男孩的面倒不至于尴尬。她甚至可以先锁门、穿戴好外出的衣帽，就穿着它们下楼吃饭。他们不会想到她本可以不必留下吃饭……她在屋里走动、把东西收进保险柜时，情绪高涨起来。她在莱顿先生暖和的房间里换装，用很热的水洗手，回暖，暖和过来……软帽（toque）在他那面大镜子里显得好看，相当俏丽，不那么像自家手工……世上体面人吃午饭总是穿着出门的衣装，哪怕当时正住在人家家里。萨拉说人就该永远戴着帽子，尤其配晚礼服……宽檐画帽（picture hats），配上晚礼服，便成一幅幅画。是啊，想想还真是，确实会成画。可萨拉是无师自通；并没有什么机会；这见识从她心里生出，经由她那双艺术家的眼睛。米丽亚姆想起科里家（the Corries）那些时髦的中年妇人，明明没出门，午饭时却戴着大得出奇的帽子；原来如此。这帮她们撑住场面；让她们谈吐爽利快捷，显得仿佛刚从什么地方匆匆赶来、或是正要匆匆离去。……她又把自己打量了一遍。果然；哪怕同莱顿先生和表弟一起吃饭，戴着这顶软帽、黑外套敞开露出白色小方巾，也会轻松些。它给人一种行色匆匆、兴高采烈的印象。她一切就绪，环顾四周，想找点消遣打发剩下的片刻。还真有；一本书摊开在莱顿先生桌上，当然是一本军事操典。不对。*这*是什么。《奇女子》（Wondrous Woman），J. B. G. 史密森著。怎么这么多雷同的英文首字母？吉姆、比尔、乔治，一股子过剩的男人气……自然是攻击之作；她扫视着一页页和小标题；一章接一章乖戾的插科打诨；整本书处心积虑同女人作对。她的心愤怒地跳着。莱顿先生要这样一本书做什么？它从哪儿来的？她飞快地读着，抓住书中的论点。老一套的东西；更糟，因为它用的是口语，用现代日常的语言一路奔涌，而且以某种古怪的方式写得还不坏。……

===Original===
Because some women had corns, feminine beauty was a myth; because the world could do without Mrs. Hemans’ poetry, women should confine their attention to puddings and babies. The infernal complacent cheek of it. This was the kind of thing middle-class men read. Unable to criticise it, they thought it witty and unanswerable. That was the worst of it. Books of this sort were read without anyone there to point things out.﻿ ﻿… It ought to be illegal to publish a book by a man without first giving it to a woman to annotate. But what was the answer to men who called women inferior because they had not invented or achieved in science or art? On whose authority had men decided that science and art were greater than anything else? The world could not go on until this question had been answered. Until then, until it had been clearly explained that men were always and always partly wrong in all their ideas, life would be full of poison and secret bitterness.﻿ ﻿… Men fight about their philosophies and religions, there is no certainty in them; but their contempt for women is flawless and unanimous. Even Emerson﻿ ﻿… positive and negative, north and south, male and female﻿ ﻿… why *negative*? Maeterlinck gets nearest in knowing that women can live, hardly at all, with men, and wait, have always been waiting, for men to come to life. How can men come to life; always fussing? How could the man who wrote this book? Even if it were publicly burned and he were made to apologise; he would still go about asquint﻿ ﻿… lunch was going to be late, just today, of course.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
因为有些女人长鸡眼，女性的美便成了神话；因为这世界缺了海曼斯太太的诗也照样转，女人们就该把心思收窄到布丁和婴孩上。这份狂妄自得的无耻。中产阶级男人读的就是这种东西。他们无力批评，便以为它机智、无可辩驳。这才是最糟的。这类书读起来，身边没有一个人替你点破。……应当立一条法：男人出的书，先交一个女人去评注，才准印行。可是对那些因为女人没有在科学或艺术上发明创造过什么、便称女人低男人一等的男人，该拿什么回答？凭什么权威，男人断定了科学与艺术高于其他一切？这个问题不回答，世界就没法往前走。在那之前，在还没能把男人在他们一切想法里永远、永远都有一部分是错的这一点讲个明白之前，生活将充满毒害与暗地的怨苦。……男人们为他们的哲学和宗教打得不可开交，那些东西里没有半点确定；可他们对女人的轻蔑却无懈可击、众口一词。连爱默生（Emerson）也一样……正与负，北与南，雄与雌……为什么雌是*负*？梅特克林（Maeterlinck）算最接近的了：他知道女人几乎没法同男人一起生活，只能等待，一直等待着男人们活转过来。男人们怎么活转过来？一天到晚穷折腾。写这本书的那个男人又怎么可能？哪怕把书当众烧掉、逼他登门道歉；他也照样会斜着眼到处走……午饭要迟了，偏偏是今天，当然。……

===Original===
“I *say*.”

“*What* do you say,” responded Miriam without looking up from her soup. Mr. Leyton had a topic; she could keep it going with half her attention and go restfully on, fortifying herself for the afternoon. She would attack him about the book one day next week.﻿ ﻿…

“I *say*. What say you George?”

“Me? All right. I say, I say, I say, anything you like m’lord.”

Miriam looked up. Mr. Leyton was gazing and grinning.

“What’s the matter?” she snapped. His eyes were on her toque.

“*Where* did you get that hat? Where *did* you get that tile,” sang the cousin absently, busy with his lunch.

“I made it if you *must* know,” said Miriam. The cousin looked across; large expressionless opinionless eyes.

“Going out in it?” What *was* the matter; Mr. Leyton had never noticed anything of hers before; either it was too awful, or really rather effective and he unconsciously resented the fact of her going about in an effect.

“Why not?”

“Well; looks rather like a musical comedy.”

“Cheek,” observed the cousin; “I do call that cool cheek; you’re balmy, Leyton.” Mr. Leyton looked no more; that was his genuine brotherly opinion; he thought the toque showy. It was the two wings, meeting in the middle of the front; he meant pantomime; he did not know the wings were cheap; he was shocked by the effectiveness; it *was* effective; cheap and hateful; but it suited her; pantomime effects were becoming. Where was the objection?

“That’s all right. I’m glad. I like musical comedies.”

“Oh; if you’re satisfied. If you don’t mind looking risky.”

“I say, look here old man, steady on,” blushed the cousin.

“Well. What do you think yourself? Come on.”

“I think it’s jolly pretty.”

“*I* think it’s jolly *fast*.”

Miriam was quite satisfied. The cousin’s opinion went for nothing; a boy *would* like pantomime effects. But the hat was neither ugly nor dowdy. She would be able to tear down Oxford Street, no matter how ugly the cold made her feel, looking *fast*. It would help her to carry off meeting Mr. Shatov. *He* would not notice hats. But the extraordinary, rather touching thing was that Mr. Leyton should trouble at all. As if she belonged to his world and he were in some way responsible.

===Chinese===
“我*说*。”

“*你*说什么，”米丽亚姆应道，眼睛没离开汤。莱顿先生有了话题；她可以用半个心思把它维持下去，自己安安稳稳地继续歇着，为下午养精蓄锐。下星期哪天，她要为那本书向他发难。……

“我*说*。你说呢，乔治？”

“我？行啊。我说，我说，我说，随您吩咐，大人。”

米丽亚姆抬起头。莱顿先生正咧嘴笑着盯着看。

“怎么了？”她没好气地问。他的眼睛落在她的软帽上。

“*哪儿*弄来那顶帽子的？*到底*哪儿弄来那块‘瓦片’啊，”表弟心不在焉地唱着，忙着对付他的午饭。

“你要是*非*要知道，那是我自己做的，”米丽亚姆说。表弟望过来；一双大眼睛毫无表情、不含主见。

“戴着它出门？”*这是*怎么了；莱顿先生以前从没留意过她的任何东西；要么它糟得吓人，要么当真相当出彩，而他不知不觉地对“她竟戴着一副出彩的样子四处走动”生出不忿。

“怎么不？”

“呃；看着有点像音乐喜剧。”

“好厚的脸皮，”表弟评论道；“这可真是若无其事的无耻；你昏了头，莱顿。”莱顿先生不再看了；那是他由衷的兄弟之见；他觉得这软帽俗艳。是那两片翅膀，在前额正中会合；他指的是童话哑剧；他不知道那两片翅膀多廉价；他被那效果震住了；它*确实*出彩；廉价又可恨；可它衬她；哑剧式的效果就是衬她。异议又在哪里？

“那就好。我很高兴。我喜欢音乐喜剧。”

“哦；你满意就好。只要你不怕显得轻佻。”

“喂，我说，老兄，嘴下留情啊，”表弟红着脸说。

“那你说呢？你自己觉得怎么样？直说。”

“我觉得它漂亮极了。”

“*我*觉得它妖艳得很。”

米丽亚姆相当满意。表弟的意见一文不值；男孩子*就是*会喜欢哑剧效果。可这帽子既不丑也不土。这样她就能一路飞奔下牛津街，不管冻得多难看，看起来一副*妖艳*模样。这能帮她撑住同沙托夫先生的会面。*他*是不会留意帽子的。可那件不寻常、甚至动人的事是：莱顿先生竟然会在意。仿佛她属于他的世界，仿佛他对她负着某种责任。

===Original===
“All right Mr. Leyton; it’s fast; whatever that may mean.”

“Old Leyton thinks hats ought to be slow.”

“Look here young fellow me lad, you teach your﻿—”

“Great-grandfather not to be rude.”

“I fail to see the rudeness; I’ve merely expressed an opinion and I believe Miss Henderson agrees with it.”

“Oh absolutely; ab‑so*lute*‑ly;” chanted Miriam scornfully. “Pray don’t worry about the pace of my millinery Mr. Leyton.” That was quite good, like a society novel.﻿ ﻿…

“Well as I say if you’re *satisfied*.”

“Ah. That’s another matter. The next time I want a hat I’ll go to Bond Street. So easy and simple.”

“Seen the paper today, George?”

“Paper? Noospaper? No time.”

“Seen the B.M.J.?”

“No sir.”

“And you an aspiring medico.”

“Should be an expiring medico,” yodelled George, “if I read all those effusions.”

“Well. More disclosures from Schenck.”

“Who’s he, when he’s at home?”

“You know. Schenck, man; *Schenk*. *You* know.”

“Oh, sorry; all right. What’s he babbling about now?”

“Same thing; only more of it,” giggled Mr. Leyton.

“If it’s half past I must go,” announced Miriam peremptorily. Two watches came out.

“Then I advise you to hook it pretty sharp; it’s twenty to. You’d better *read* that article my son.” Miriam folded her serviette.

“Righto. Don’t worry.”

“*Why* all this mystery? Good morning,” said Miriam departing.

“Good morning,” said the two voices. Mr. Leyton held the door open and raised his voice to follow her up the stairs. “We’re discussing matters somewhat beyond your ken.”

===Chinese===
“好吧莱顿先生；它就是妖艳；随便那是什么意思。”

“老莱顿认为帽子就该慢悠悠。”

“听着，小老弟，你竟敢教你的——”

“教你的太爷爷懂规矩。”

“我看不出有什么失礼；我不过发表了一点见解，而且我相信亨德森小姐也同意。”

“哦那是自然；绝——*对*——是——的；”米丽亚姆讥诮地唱道。“请不必为敝人帽子的‘速度’费心，莱顿先生。”这句相当漂亮，像社会小说里的对白。……

“那么，我说，只要你*满意*就好。”

“啊。那是另一回事。下回我要买帽子，就去邦德街。多省事，多简单。”

“今天看报了吗，乔治？”

“报纸？报纸么？没工夫。”

“看《英国医学杂志》（B.M.J.）了吗？”

“没看过，长官。”

“亏你还是个有志从医的。”

“该是个‘断气’的从医者了，”乔治打了个呼哨般的调子喊道，“要是那些连篇大作我全都读完的话。”

“嗯。舍恩克（Schenck）又有新爆料了。”

“他是谁？在家的时候是干什么的？”

“你知道的。舍恩克，老兄；*舍恩克*。*你*知道的。”

“哦，抱歉；行吧。他现在又在咕哝什么？”

“还是那一套；只是更多了，”莱顿先生咯咯笑道。

“要是已经半点了我可得走了，”米丽亚姆斩钉截铁地宣布。两块怀表同时掏了出来。

“那我劝你麻利点儿开溜——已经差二十分了。你最好把那篇文章*好好读读*，小子。”米丽亚姆叠好餐巾。

“好嘞。不劳费心。”

“*搞*什么名堂？再见，”米丽亚姆说着离席。

“再见，”两个声音齐声道。莱顿先生替她拉住门，提高嗓门追着她上楼梯：“我们谈论的事嘛，可有点超出你的见识范围喽。”

===Original===
She could not stay. She could not have tackled him if she had stayed. Anger was perhaps as funny as embarrassment. He would have been shocked at the idea of her quietly considering the results of Schenck’s theory, if it proved to be true; beyond her ken, indeed. It was hateful to have to leave that; he *ought* to be robbed of the one thing that he imagined gave him an advantage in the presence of women. The women in his world *would* be embarrassed by the discussion of anything to do with the reproduction of the race. *Why?* Why were the women embarrassed and the men always suggestive and facetious? If only the men could realise what they admitted by their tone; what attitude towards life.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
她走不了，也留不住。倘若留下，她也拿他没办法。愤怒也许和难堪一样可笑。倘若她安安静静地琢磨起舍恩克理论的种种后果——万一它属实——他准会吓一跳；还说什么超出她的见识范围，可不是嘛。不得不撇下这个话头，真可恨；他自以为能让他在女人面前占上风的全副凭据就这一样，*就该*把它从他手里夺走。他的世界里那些女人，一谈到任何与种族繁衍沾边的事*必定*会难堪。*为什么？*为什么难堪的总是女人，而男人总是语带狎昵、插科打诨？男人们要是能明白他们的腔调招认了什么、招认了怎样一种人生态度，那该多好。……

===Original===
It was a bitter east-wind; the worst kind of day there was. All along Oxford Street were women in furs, serene, with smooth warm faces untroubled by the bleak black wind, perhaps even enjoying the cold. Miriam struggled along, towards the cruel east, shivering, her face shrivelled and frozen and burning, her brain congealed. If she were free she could at least have a cup of coffee and get warm and go into the Museum and be warm all the afternoon. To meet a stranger and have to be active and sociable when she was at her worst. He would be wrapped in the advantage of a fur-lined coat, or at least astrachan, and be able to think and speak. He would wonder what was the matter; even his careless foreign friendliness would not survive her frightful appearance. Yet when a clock told her the appointed time was past, the torment of the wind grew sharper in the thought that she might miss him. There was the Holborn Library, as he had described. There was no one there, the pavement was empty; he had given her up and gone; had perhaps never come. She was relieved. She had done her best. Fate had saved her; her afternoon was her own. But she must show herself, perhaps he might be sheltering just inside the door. The doorway was empty. There was a man leaning against the lamppost. She scanned him unwillingly, lest he should turn into Mr. Shatov; but he produced only the details of the impression she had taken before she glanced, a shabby, sinister-looking Tottenham Court Road foreign loafer, in yellow boots, an overcoat of an evil shade of brown and a waiter’s black-banded grey felt hat; but she had paused and glanced and of course his eye was immediately upon her and his lounging figure upright as she swept across the pavement to gain the road and flee the displeasing contact. He almost ran into her; trotting.﻿ ﻿… *ah*, I am glad﻿ ﻿… it *was* Mr. Shatov.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
是一阵苦寒的东风；天底下最要命的日子。牛津街沿线尽是裹在裘皮里的女人，安详，面庞光润温暖，不为那阴冷的黑风所扰，说不定还挺享受这寒冷。米丽亚姆挣扎着迎风向东而行，向着那残忍的东方，打着颤，脸皱缩着、冻僵着又火辣辣地发烫，脑子凝住了。她若是自由的，至少能去喝杯咖啡暖和一下，然后进博物馆，暖暖和和过一下午。偏要在自己状态最糟的时候去见一个陌生人，还得活跃、还得应酬。他会裹在皮里子大衣的优势里，至少也是一身阿斯特拉罕羔皮，能思考、能说话。他会纳闷这是怎么了；连他那毫不见外的外国人式的友好，也熬不过她这副吓人的模样。然而当一座钟告诉她约定时刻已过，风的折磨反而更狠了，她怕自己错过了他。霍尔本图书馆就在那儿，正如他描述过的。那里没有人，人行道空空的；他已经死了心走掉了；或许压根儿没来过。她松了口气。她尽了力。命运救了她；下午是她自己的了。可她总得露个面，说不定他正躲在门里侧避风。门廊里空空的。有个人倚着灯柱。她不情愿地打量他，生怕他变成沙托夫先生；可他只贡献出她扫视之前便已取到的那些细节印象：一个寒酸、模样阴险的托特纳姆宫路式外国闲汉，黄皮鞋，一件色调邪恶的棕色大衣，一顶跑堂用的黑箍灰毡帽；可她已经停步又扫了一眼，他的目光自然立刻黏上了她，他那懒散的身形也随着她掠过人行道、走向街心好摆脱这不快的照面而猛然挺直。他几乎是撞上她；一路小跑着。……*啊*，真好……那*正*是沙托夫先生。……

===Original===
Looking like that, she was now to take him in amongst the British Museum officials, and the readers she knew by sight and who knew her; introduce him to the librarian. She scanned him as he eagerly talked, looking in vain for the presence she had sat with in the drawing-room; the eyes had come back; but that was all, and she could not forget how brooding, almost evil, they had looked just now. They gleamed again with intelligence; but their brilliant beauty shone from a face that looked almost dingy, in the hard light; and yellowish under the frightful hat peaked down, cutting off his forehead. He was gloveless and in his hands, grimed with walking in the winter streets, he held a paper bag of grapes which he ate as he talked, expelling the skins and flinging them from him as he walked﻿ ﻿… he looked just simply disreputable. Even his voice had gone; raised against the traffic it was narrow and squeaky; a disreputable foreigner, plunging carelessly along, piercing her ear with mean broken English. She shouted vague replies in French; in yelled French his voice was even more squeaky; but the foreign tongue gave a refuge and a shape to their grouping; she became a sort of guide; anyone could be that to any sort of foreigner.

===Chinese===
就这副模样，她如今要领他走进大英博物馆的官员们中间，走进那些她认得面容、对方也认得她的读者们中间；把他引荐给管理员。他热切地说着话，她一路打量，徒劳地寻找那个曾在客厅里同她对坐的身影；那双眼睛回来了；但也仅此而已，而她忘不了方才它们看起来多么阴郁、几乎凶恶。此刻它们又闪着智慧的光；可那夺目的美，是从一张在硬光线下显得几乎暗淡的脸上照出来的；在那顶压得低低的可怕帽子底下泛黄，帽檐截去了他的额头。他不戴手套，一双走了冬天街路而脏污的手里，拎着一纸袋葡萄，边说边吃，皮吐出来随手一扬，走一路撒一路……他看上去简直不像话。连他的嗓音也没了；顶着车流提高起来，又窄又尖；一个不像话的外国人，横冲直撞地随便走，用蹩脚破碎的英语刺她的耳朵。她用法语含混地应答；他一喊法语，嗓音更尖了；可这门外国语给他们的同行添了个庇护、给了个形状；她成了一种向导；随便什么人，在随便哪种外国人面前，都当得了这个。

===Original===
In the cloak room were the usual ladies comfortably eating lunch from sandwich tins and talking, talking, talking to the staff, moving endlessly to and fro amongst the cages of hanging garments; answering unconsciously. The mysterious everlasting work of the lunching ladies, giving them the privilege of being all day at the museum, always in the same seats, accepted and approved, seemed to make no mark upon them; they bore themselves just as they would have done anywhere, the same mysteriously unfailing flow of talk, the mysterious basis of agreement with other women, the same enthusiastic discussions of the weather, the cases in the newspapers, their way of doing this and that, their opinions of places and people﻿ ﻿… they seemed to have no sense of the place they were in, and yet were so extraordinarily at home there, and most wonderful of all, serene, with untroubled eyes and hands in the thin stuffy heat of the cloakroom.

===Chinese===
衣帽间里照例是那些舒舒服服吃着午饭的女人们，三明治盒打开，对着工作人员说个不停，说呀，说呀，说呀，在挂满衣物的笼架间无休无止地来来回回；不假思索地应答着。这些用午饭的女人们那神秘的、永不停歇的功课，给了她们整日待在博物馆里、永远坐同一些座位的特权，被接受、被认可，却似乎没在她们身上留下任何印记；她们的举止同在任何地方都会有的举止一模一样，同样那股永不枯竭的谈话之流，同别的女人之间那神秘的默契，同样热烈的天气讨论、报上的案子、做这做那的门道、对地方和人物的种种见解……她们仿佛对自己身在何处毫无感觉，却又在这地方自在得出奇，而最奇妙的是那份安详，在衣帽间稀薄闷热的水汽里，眼睛和手都波澜不惊。

===Original===
These thoughts came every time; the sense of Mr. Shatov, busy, she hoped, washing his face and hands down beyond the stairs leading to the unknown privacies at the other end of the corridor, could not banish them; the bearing of these ladies was the most mysterious thing in the museum. In this room she was always on her guard. It was jolly after roaming slowly across the courtyard towards the unfailing unchanging beauty of the great grey pillars, pigeon-garlanded, to wander through the outbranching hall to where the lame commissionaire held open the magic door, and fly along the passage and break in here, permitted, cold and grimy and ruffled from the street, and emerge washed and hatless and rested, to saunter down the corridor and see ahead, before becoming one of them, the dim various forms sitting in little circles of soft yellow light under the high mysterious dome. But in one unguarded moment in this room, all these women would turn into acquaintances, and the spell of the museum, springing forth perhaps for a while, intensified, would disappear forever. They would turn it into themselves, varying and always in the end, in silence, the same. In solitude it remained unvarying yet never twice alike, casting its large increasing charm upon them as they moved distant and unknown.

===Chinese===
这些念头每次都来；沙托夫先生的存在感——但愿他正在楼梯那头、走廊另一端那些不为人知的私密处，忙着洗脸洗手——也驱不散它们；这些女人的仪态是博物馆里最神秘的事物。在这个房间里她总是存着戒心。慢慢地漫游着穿过庭院，走向那几根灰色巨柱永不失效、永不改变的美，柱头缀着鸽群如花环，再蜿蜒穿过枝杈般岔开的门厅，走到跛脚门卫替你拉开的那扇魔法之门前，飞奔过走廊闯到这里，被恩准着，带着街上的寒气、尘土和凌乱，再洗净了、脱了帽、歇足了现身，信步走下走廊，赶在变成她们中一员之前，望见高深穹顶下那一圈圈柔和的黄光里坐着影影绰绰各色人影——这多快活。可是在这个房间里，一个不留神的瞬间，所有这些女人都会变成熟人，而博物馆的魔咒——或许会一时迸发得更浓——将永远消失。她们会把它变成她们自己，变化多端，而到头来，在沉默中，千篇一律。在孤独中它恒常不变却又从不重复，随着她们远远地、互不相识地走动，把它那不断增长的巨大魅力投在她们身上。

===Original===
In the lower cloakroom there was always escape; no sofas, no grouped forms. Today it stood bare, its long row of basins unoccupied. She turned taps joyously; icy cold and steaming hot water rushing to cleanse her basin from its revealing relics. They were all the same, and all the soaps, save one she secured from a distant corner, sloppy. Surveying, she felt with irritated repugnance, the quality, slapdash and unaware, of the interchange accompanying and matching the ablutions. A woman came out of a lavatory and stood at her side, also swiftly restoring a basin. It was *she*.﻿ ﻿… Miriam envied the basin.﻿ ﻿… Freely watching the peaceful face in the mirror, she washed with an intense sense of sheltering companionship. Far in behind the peaceful face serene thoughts moved, not to and fro, but outward and forward from some sure centre. Perfectly screened, unknowing and unknown, she went about within the charmed world of her inheritance. It was difficult to imagine what work she might be doing, always here, and always moving about as if unseeing and unseen. Round about her serenity any kind of life could group, leaving it, as the foggy grime and the dusty swelter of London left her, unsullied and untouched. But for the present she was here, as if she moved, emerging from a spacious many-windowed sunlight flooded house whose happy days were in her quiet hands, in clear light about the spaces of a wide garden. Yet she was aware of the world about her. It was not a matter of life and death to her that she should be free to wander here in solitude. For those women she would have a quiet unarmed confronting manner, at their service, but holding them off without discourtesy, passing on with cup unspilled. Nothing but music reached her ears, everything she saw melted into a background of garden sunlight.

===Chinese===
楼下衣帽间总有退路；没有沙发，没有扎堆的人影。今天它空荡荡的，长长一排水槽无人占用。她欢快地拧开水龙头；冰水和滚烫的热水奔涌着，洗去她那水槽里泄露隐私的残迹。水槽全是一个样，肥皂也全是，只有一块——她从远处角落里抢到手——还算坚挺。她扫视四周，带着愠怒的嫌恶，感受着伴随这些洗漱、与之相配的那份敷衍而浑然不觉的质素。一个女人从盥洗间里出来，站到她旁边，也在飞快地恢复一只水槽的清白。正是*她*。……米丽亚姆羡慕起那只水槽。……她自在地望着镜中那张安详的脸，洗着，心头涌起一种被庇护的伴侣之情。在那张安详的脸庞深处，幽远的后面，安详的思绪在移动，不是来回移动，而是从某个笃定的中心向外、向前。她被完美地遮蔽着，不知人也不为人知，在她承袭而来的迷人世界里起居。很难想象她可能做着什么样的工作，永远在这里，永远这样走动着，仿佛视而不见、无人看见。任何一种生活都能在她的安详周遭聚拢，就像伦敦雾蒙蒙的烟尘和闷热的尘垢沾不上她一样，留她完好无损、纤尘不染。可眼下她在这里，仿佛正从一座轩敞多窗、阳光泼洒的宅子里走出来，那宅子的幸福时日就握在她安静的双手中，在明亮的光里，在一片宽阔园林的空间之间。然而她并非不觉察身外的世界。能不能独自一人到这里漫游，对她并不是生死攸关的事。对那些女人，她自会拿出一种安静的、不设防的应对姿态，听凭使唤，却又不失礼数地把她们挡开，端着满杯走过、点滴不洒。唯有音乐进得了她的耳朵，她目之所见，一切都融进一片园林阳光的背景里。

===Original===
She was out of sight, drying her hands, lighting up the corner of the room where the towels hung.﻿ ﻿… If Mr. Shatov were on *her* hands, she would not be regretting that the afternoon could hold no solitary wanderings. She made no calculations; for she could not be robbed. That was strength. She was gone. Miriam finished her operations as though she remained, drying her hands unhurriedly, standing where she had stood, trying to survey the unforetellable afternoon with something of her sustained tranquillity.

===Chinese===
她看不见了，正在擦手，照亮了挂毛巾的那个房间角落。……若是沙托夫先生要*她*操心照应，她就不会惋惜这下午容不下独自的漫游了。她不作盘算；因为她不会被人抢走什么。那便是力量。她走了。米丽亚姆继续做完自己的功课，仿佛她还在，不慌不忙地擦着手，站在她站过的地方，努力借她那份绵延的安宁，来打量这个无法预料的下午。

===Original===
He would probably be plunging up and down the corridor with a growing impatience.﻿ ﻿… There he was, unconcernedly waiting; his singing determined child’s head reared hatless above the dreadful overcoat, the clear light of the corridor upon its modest thought-moulded dignity﻿ ﻿… distinguished﻿ ﻿… that was what he was. She felt unworthy, helplessly inadequate, coming up the corridor to claim him. She was amongst the people passing about him before he saw her; and she caught again the look of profound reproachful brooding melancholy seated in his eyes, so strangely contradicting his whole happy look of a child standing at a party, gazing, everything pouring into its wide eyes; dancing and singing within itself, unconscious of its motionless body.

===Chinese===
他多半正在走廊里来来回回地蹿，火气越蹿越大。……他就在那儿，好整以暇地等着；他那颗唱着歌的、果决的孩童头颅，不戴帽子，昂然竖在那件可怕的大衣之上，走廊清亮的光照在它那谦抑的、被思想塑出棱角的庄严上……卓然出众……他就是这样的人。她觉得自己配不上，无能为力，沿着走廊走过去认领他。她在围着他走来走去的人群里，还没被他看见；她又捕捉到那缕深深的、含怨的、郁郁沉思的神色，盘踞在他眼中，同他整个幸福的模样——一个站在宴会中的孩子，凝望着，万物涌进他睁大的眼睛；身体纹丝不动，内里又唱又跳——奇怪地自相矛盾着。

===Original===
“Here we are,” she said avertedly as he came eagerly forward.

“Let us quickly to this official,” he urged in his indoor voice.

“All right; this way.” He hurried along at her side, beard forward, his yellow boots plunging in long rapid strides beneath his voluminously floating overcoat.

===Chinese===
“我们来了，”他热切地迎上来时，她偏着头说。

“让我们赶紧去见这位官员，”他用他室内的嗓音催促道。

“好；这边走。”他在她身边疾行，胡子朝前，黄色皮鞋在飘飘荡荡的大衣底下迈着大步急急踏进。

===Original===
She resented the librarian’s official manner; the appearance of the visitor, the little card he promptly produced, should have been enough. Stud. Schtu*dent*, how much more expressive than *stew*dent﻿ ﻿… to be able to go about the world for years, so-and-so, stud﻿ ﻿… all doors open and committed to nothing. She asserted herself by making suggestions in French. Mr. Shatov responded politely, also in French, and she felt the absurdity of her eager interference, holding him a prisoner, hiding his studious command of English, in order to flourish forth her knowledge. “We are not afraid even of Russian, if Mr. Shatov prefers to use his own tongue,” said the librarian. Miriam flashed a suspicious glance. He was smiling a self-conscious superior English smile. It soured into embarrassment under her eye.

===Chinese===
她不满管理员的官派做派；这位访客的仪表，加上他利落掏出的那张小卡片，本该足够了。Stud.（学生）……Schtudent（施图登特），比 stewdent（斯图登特）有表现力多了……能顶着“某某学生”的名头在世界各地走动多年……所有的门都敞开，又不担任何干系。她改用法语提建议，借此伸张自己。沙托夫先生礼貌地也用法语回应，她这才觉出自己急切的多管闲事有多可笑：把他当俘虏扣着，藏起他勤学的英语功夫，只为炫耀她自己的那点学问。“即便用俄语我们也不怕，要是沙托夫先生更愿意用自己的语言的话，”管理员说。米丽亚姆狐疑地扫了一眼。他正堆着一个自觉高人一等的英国式微笑。在她的注视下，那笑容讪讪地垮成了尴尬。

===Original===
“It is no matter,” said Mr. Shatov gently, “you shall immediately say me the requisite formules which I shall at once write.” He stood beautiful, the gentle unconsciously reproachful prey of English people unable to resist their desire to be effective. They stood conquered, competing in silent appreciation, as he bent writing his way into their forgotten library.

===Chinese===
“这没有关系，”沙托夫先生温和地说，“您马上把必要的 formules（套语）说给我听，我立刻记下来。”他站在那儿，风姿俊美，是英国人那种按捺不住的想要办事见效的欲望手下、无意识的含着责备意味的猎物。他们服服帖帖站着，暗暗较着劲地欣赏，看他伏身书写，一路写进他们那被遗忘的图书馆。

===Original===
“Now I am pairfectly happy,” he said as he passed through the swing doors of the reading-room. His head was up radiantly singing, he was rushing trustfully forward, looking at nothing, carrying her on, close at his side, till they reached the barrier of the outmost catalogue desk. He pulled up facing her, with wide wild eyes looking at nothing. “We shall at once take Anaka*ray*ninna in *English*” he shouted in an enthusiastic whisper.

===Chinese===
“这下我完全（pairfectly）幸福了，”穿过阅览室的弹簧门时他说。他的头颅昂扬，放着光，唱着歌，一股脑儿信赖地往前冲，什么也不看，把她带在身边，紧紧的，直到最外侧目录台构成的关卡前才收住。他在她面前急停，睁大的狂野的眼睛望着虚空。“我们这就马上用英文来读安纳卡雷尼娜（Anakarayninna，他把重音落在‘雷’上）”他热情地耳语着喊道。

===Original===
“We must choose seats before we get books,” murmured Miriam. There was plenty to do and explain; the revelation of her meagre attack on the riches of the library need not yet come. Were they to read together? Had he reached his goal “midst all those literatures” to spend his time in showing her Tolstoy? He followed her absently about as she filled in the time while they waited for their book, by showing all she knew of the routine of the library. “There shall of course,” he said in a gruff explanatory tone, arresting her near the entrance to the central enclosure, “be a quite exhaustive system of catalogue, but I find there is too much *formalities*; with all these little baskets.” “Ssh,” begged Miriam leading him away. She drifted to the bookshelves, showing him the one shelf she knew on the south side; there was a reader on a ladder at the very shelf. “Carlyle’s French Revolution is up there,” she said confidently. “Na, na,” he growled reproachfully, “this is a most purely unreliable fictional history, a tour de force from special individual prejudices. You should take rather Thiers.” She piloted him across to her shelf on the north side to point out the *Revue des deux Mondes* and the *North American Review*. He paused, searching along the shelves. “*Ah. Here* is books.” He drew out and flung open a heavy beautifully printed volume with wide margins on the pages; she would show him the clever little folding arrangements to hold heavy volumes; “You do not know these?” he demanded of her silence; “ah that is a great pity; it is the complete *discours de l’Académie française*; you shall immediately read them; ah, they are the most *perfect* *modèles*.” She glanced at the open page beginning “*Messieurs! Le sentiment de fierté avec laquelle je vous*”; it was a voice; exactly like the voice of Mr. Shatov. He stood with the heavy open volume, insisting in his dreadfully audible whisper on wonderful French names prefixed to the titles of addresses, fascinating subjects, one of them Mr. *Gladstone*! He looked French as he spoke; a brilliantly polished Frenchman. Why had he not gone to France? He was German too, with a German education and yet with some impatiently unexplained understanding and contempt﻿—for Germany. Why was he drawn towards England? That was the mysterious thing. What was the secret of the reverence in this man towards England and the English? He was not an anarchist. There he stood, Russian, come from all that faraway beauty, with German and French culture in his mind, longingly to England, coming to Tansley Street; unconsciously bringing her her share in his longed-for arrival and its fulfilments. She watched as he talked, marvelling at the undeserved wealth offered to her in the little figure discoursing so eagerly over the cumbrous volume, and at this moment the strange Russian book was probably waiting for them.

===Chinese===
“拿书之前得先选座位，”米丽亚姆低声说。要做的事、要交代的事多着呢；她对自己那份对图书馆宝藏的单薄攻略，还不必和盘托出。他们是要一起读吗？他“在所有那些文学中间”抵达了他的目标，难道是为了把时间花在领她看托尔斯泰上？等书的时候，他心不在焉地跟着她，她便把自己知道的馆内规程一一指点，借此填充时间。“当然会有，”他用一种粗声的解释腔调说，在通往中央围地的入口处拦住她，“一套十分详尽的目录体系，可我发现这里*形式*（formalities）太多了；用这些小筐子。”“嘘，”米丽亚姆恳求着把他领开。她飘到书架前，指给他南侧她唯一熟悉的那一格；正好有个读者在梯子上，就在那一格。“卡莱尔的《法国革命》就在上头，”她笃定地说。“不，不，”他不以为然地低吼，“这是一部最纯粹的不可靠的虚构历史，一件出于特殊个人偏见的炫技之作（tour de force）。您不如去读梯也尔（Thiers）。”她引他到北侧她的书架，指给他看《两世界评论》（Revue des deux Mondes）和《北美评论》（North American Review）。他停下来，沿着书架搜寻。“*啊。这儿*才是书。”他抽出一本沉甸甸印制精美的卷册，书页留着宽阔的空白；她正要指给他看托住重册的那些灵巧的小折架；“您不认识这些吗？”他冲着她的沉默发问；“啊，那太遗憾了；这是法兰西学院演说（discours de l'Académie française）全集；您得马上读一读；啊，它们是最完美的*范本*（modèles）。”她瞥了一眼摊开的那页，开头是“Messieurs! Le sentiment de fierté avec laquelle je vous（诸位先生！我借此向您们表达的那种自豪之情）”；那是一个声音；同沙托夫先生的嗓音一模一样。他抱着那本摊开的厚重书卷，用他那可怕的听得见的耳语，坚持讲着冠在演讲标题前的那些美妙的法国名字，那些迷人的题目，其中一位竟是*格莱斯顿*（Gladstone）先生！他说话时看上去像个法国人；一位光彩照人的、打磨光亮的法国人。他为什么没去法国？他也是个德国人，受的是德国教育，却又怀着某种急不可耐、无从解释的对德国的了解与蔑视——。他为什么被英国吸引？这才是神秘之处。这个人对英国和英国人的敬意，秘密何在？他不是无政府主义者。他站在那里，俄罗斯人，从那全部的遥远的美里走来，头脑里装着德国和法国的文化，渴望地奔向英国，来到坦斯利街；浑然不觉地，把他渴望已久的抵达及其种种圆满中属于她的那一份，带给了她。她望着他谈讲，惊叹于这个小小的身影抱着笨重的书卷热切讲论时向她捧出的这份非分之财，而此刻，那本神秘的俄国书多半正等着他们。

===Original===
It was a big thick book. Miriam sat down before it. The lights had come on. The book lay in a pool of sharp yellow light; Tolstoy, surrounded by a waiting gloom; the secret of Tolstoy standing at her side, rapidly taking off his overcoat. He drew up the chair from the next place and sat close, flattening out the book at the first chapter and beginning to read at once, bent low over the book. She bent too, stretching her hands out beyond her knees to make herself narrow, and fastening on the title. Her anticipations fell dead. It was the name of a woman﻿ ﻿… Anna; of all names. Karenine. The story of a woman told by a man with a man’s ideas about people. But Anna Karenine was not what Tolstoy had written. Behind the ugly feebleness of the substituted word was something quite different, strong and beautiful; a whole legend in itself. Why had the translator altered the surname? Anna Kar*ay*ninna was a line of Russian poetry. His word was nothing, neither English nor French, and sounded like a face-cream. She scanned sceptically up and down the pages of English words, chilled by the fear of detecting the trail of the translator.

===Chinese===
那是又大又厚的一本书。米丽亚姆在它面前坐下。灯亮起来了。书躺在一汪锐利的黄光里；托尔斯泰，被一圈等待着的幽暗环抱；托尔斯泰的秘密立在她身旁，正飞快脱下大衣。他把邻座的椅子拖过来紧挨着坐下，把书在第一章摊平，立刻开始朗读，身子低低地俯在书上。她也俯下身，双手伸到膝盖外面收窄自己，盯住书名。她的期待轰然倒毙。竟是一个女人的名字……安娜；千万个名字里偏偏是它。卡列宁（Karenine）。一个男人用他关于人的男式观念讲述的一个女人的故事。可安·卡列尼娜并不是托尔斯泰写下的原词。在那个被替换的词丑陋的疲软背后，是全然不同的东西，强壮而美丽；本身便是一整个传奇。译者为什么要改动这个姓氏？安纳·卡雷尼娜（Anna Karayninna）是一行俄国诗歌。他用的那个词什么也不是，既非英语也非法语，听着像一种面霜的名字。她怀疑地上下扫视那一页页英文字，生怕嗅出译者留下的踪迹，心里发冷。

===Original===
Mr. Shatov read steadily, breathing his enthusiasm in gusts, pausing as each fresh name appeared, to pronounce it in Russian and to explain the three names belonging to each character. They were all expressive; easy to remember because of their expressiveness. The threefold name, giving each character three faces, each turned towards a different part of his world, was fascinating.﻿ ﻿… Conversation began almost at once and kept breaking out; strange abrupt conversation different to any she had read elsewhere.﻿ ﻿… What was it? She wanted to hold the pages and find out; but Mr. Shatov read on and on, steadily turning the leaves. She skipped, fastening upon the patches of dialogue on her side of the open page, reading them backwards and forwards, glancing at the solid intervening portions to snatch an idea of the background. What was the mysterious difference? Why did she feel she could hear the tone of the voices and the pauses between the talk; the curious feeling of things moving and changing in the air that is always there in all conversations? Her excitement grew, drawing her upright to stare her question into the gloom beyond the lamp.

===Chinese===
沙托夫先生平稳地读着，一阵一阵地呼吸出他的热忱，每逢新名字出现便停下来，用俄语念一遍，再把每个人物名下的三个名字讲解一番。这些名字个个传神；正因为传神所以好记。三重名字，给每个人物三张面孔，各自朝向他世界的一个不同部分，真令人着迷。……交谈几乎立刻就开始了，不断迸发；奇特的、突兀的交谈，同她在别处读过的任何交谈都不同。……那是什么？她想按住书页看个究竟；可沙托夫先生一直读啊读，稳稳地翻动书页。她跳着读，咬住摊开那页她这一侧的一块块对话，来回地读，扫一眼中间大段的实体部分，抢一个背景的概观。那神秘的差异是什么？为什么她觉得自己听得见说话的声口、听得见谈话之间的停顿；那种空气里有东西在移动、在变化的感觉，一切交谈里都有的那种感觉？她的兴奋高涨起来，把她拽得直起身来，将她的疑问瞪进灯光之外的幽暗里。

===Original===
“Well?” demanded Mr. Shatov.

“It’s fascinating.”

“What have I told you? That is Tols*toy*,” he said proudly; “but this is a most vile translation. All these *nu* and *da*. Why not simply *well* and *yes*; and *boszhe moi* is quite simply, my God. But this preliminary part is not so interesting as later. There is in this book the self-history of Tolstoy. He is Layvin, and Kitty is the Countess Tolstoy. That is all most wonderful. When we see her in the early morning; and the picture of this wedding. There is only Tolstoy for those marvellous touches. I shall show you.”

“Why does he call it Anna Karaynina?” asked Miriam anxiously.

“Certainly. It is a most masterly study of a certain type of woman.”

The fascination of the book still flickered brightly; but far away, retreated into the lonely incommunicable distance of her mind. It seemed always to be useless and dangerous to talk about books. They were always about something else.﻿ ﻿… If she had not asked she would have read the book without finding out it was a masterly study of Anna. Why must a book be a masterly study of some single thing? Everybody wisely raving about it.﻿ ﻿… But if one never found out what a book was a masterly study of, it meant being ignorant of things everyone knew and agreed about; a kind of hopeless personal ignorance and unintelligence; reading whole books through and through, and only finding out what they were about by accident, when people happened to talk about them, and even then, reading them again, and finding principally quite other things, which stayed, after one had forgotten what people had explained.

“I see,” she said intelligently. The readers on either side were glancing angrily. Miriam guiltily recalled her own anger with people who sat together murmuring and hissing. But it felt so different when you were one of the people. The next time she felt angry in this way she would realise how interested the talkers were, and try to forget them. Still it was wrong. “We must not talk,” she breathed. He glanced about and returned to his shuffling of pages.

“Heere it is!” he exclaimed in a guttural whisper far more distinct than his mutterings; “I shall show you this wonderful passage.”

“Ssh, yes,” murmured Miriam firmly, peering at the indicated phrase. The large warm gloom of the library, with its green-capped pools of happy light, was stricken into desolation as she read. She swung back to her world of English books and glanced for comfort at the forms of Englishmen seated in various attitudes of reading about the far edges of her circle of vision. But the passage was inexorably there; poison dropping from the book into the world; foreign poison, but translated and therefore read by at least some Englishmen. The sense of being in arms against an onslaught already achieved, filled her with despair. The enemy was far away, inaccessibly gone forward spreading more poison. She turned furiously on Mr. Shatov. She could not disprove the lie; but at least he should not sit there near her, holding it unconcerned.

“I can’t see anything wonderful. It isn’t true,” she said.

“Ah that is very English,” beamed Mr. Shatov.

“It is. *Any* English person would know that it is not true.”

Mr. Shatov gurgled his laughter. “Ah that is very naive.”

“It may be. That doesn’t make any difference.”

“It makes the difference that you are inexperienced,” he growled gently. That was true. She had no experience. She only *knew* it was not true. Perhaps it was true. Then life grew bleak again.﻿ ﻿… It was not true. But it was true for men. Skimmed off the surface, which was all they could see, and set up neatly in forcible quotable words. The rest could not be shown in these clever, neat phrases.

===Chinese===
“怎么样？”沙托夫先生追问。

“太迷人了。”

“我怎么跟您说的？这就是托尔斯*泰*，”他骄傲地说；“不过这是个最恶劣的译本。所有这些 nu（喏）和 da（是）。为什么不干脆用 well 和 yes；boszhe moi 干脆就是‘我的上帝’。不过这开头部分不如后面有趣。这本书里有托尔斯泰的自我历史。他就是列文，吉蒂就是托尔斯泰伯爵夫人。这一切最是美妙。当我们看见她在清晨；还有那婚礼的画面。唯有托尔斯泰才有那些神来之笔。我要指给您看。”

“他为什么管它叫《安娜·卡列尼娜》？”米丽亚姆焦急地问。

“当然。这是对某一类型女性的最为精湛的研究。”

那书的魅力仍在明晃晃地闪；却退远了，退进她心里那片孤独的、无从传达的远方。谈书似乎总是既无用又危险。它们谈的永远是别的东西。……她若不问，本可以读完这本书而不知道它是对安娜的精湛研究。为什么一本书必须是对某一样东西的精湛研究？人人都在明智地为它如痴如狂。……可若是一个人永远发现不了一本书是对什么的精湛研究，那就意味着对人人皆知、人人公认的事情无知；一种无望的、属于个人的无知与不敏；整本整本的书从头读到尾，只有碰巧别人谈起时才偶然发现它们讲的是什么，甚至那时重读一遍，发现的又主要是全然另外的东西，而且等别人讲解的都忘光了，这些倒留了下来。

“我明白了，”她一副心领神会的样子说。两侧的读者都怒目而视。米丽亚姆心虚地想起自己从前对那些凑在一起嘀嘀咕咕、嘶嘶作响的人的恼怒。可当你是嘀咕的那一个时，感觉多么不同。下回她再这样冒火，她就会想起谈话的人有多投入了，试着忘掉他们。不过这终归是不对的。“我们不该说话，”她轻声道。他环顾一下，回到他的翻页中去。

“在*这儿*呢（Heere it is）！”他用一种比他的咕哝清楚得多的喉音低语喊道；“我要把这精彩的一段指给您看。”

“嘘，好，”米丽亚姆坚定地低声说，凑近去看他指的那个短语。图书馆那巨大而温暖的幽暗，连同它一汪汪绿罩的快活灯光，随着她读下去，被击成一片荒芜。她荡回她的英语书的世界，朝视野圆缘四周以种种读书姿态坐着的英国人身影投去求慰的一瞥。可那段文字铁面无私地立在那里；毒液从书里滴进世界；异邦的毒，可译成了英文，因而至少有一些英国人读到。一种迎战一场已然得手的突袭的感觉，让她充满绝望。敌人远在天边，够不着地一路向前撒着更多的毒。她狂怒地转向沙托夫先生。她驳不倒那个谎言；可至少别让他若无其事地捧着它坐在她旁边。

“我看不出有什么精彩的。这不是真的，”她说。

“啊，这很英国，”沙托夫先生容光焕发地说。

“是英国。*随便哪个*英国人都知道这不是真的。”

沙托夫先生咕噜咕噜地笑。“啊，这真是天真。”

“也许是。那也改变不了什么。”

“它改变的是：您没有经验，”他温柔地低吼道。那是真的。她没有阅历。她只是*知道*它不真。也许它是真的。那么生活又黯淡下去。……它不真。可对男人是真的。从他们唯一看得见的表层撇下来，整整齐齐立成铿锵可引的字句。其余的部分，这些聪明漂亮的短语显不出来。

===Original===
“But I find the air here is most-evil. Let us rather go have tea.”

===Chinese===
“不过我觉得这里的空气最是恶劣。我们还是去喝茶吧。”

===Original===
Astonishment melted into her pride in leading him down through the great hall and along the beloved corridor of her solitary pacings, out into the gigantic granite smile of the Egyptian gallery, to the always sudden door of the refreshment room.

===Chinese===
惊愕融化了，化作她领他下行的骄傲：穿过恢弘的大厅，走过她独自踱步时心爱的那条走廊，出去，走进埃及展廊那花岗岩的巨大微笑，走向茶室那扇永远出其不意的门。

===Original===
“If I got locked into the Museum at night I should stay in this gallery,” she said unable to bear companionship in her sanctuary without extorting some recognition of its never-failing quality.

===Chinese===
“要是晚上被锁在博物馆里，我就待在这个展廊，”她说，受不了圣所里有人作伴，非要逼对方承认它这份从不失效的品性不可。

===Original===
“It is certainly impressive, in a crude way,” admitted Mr. Shatov.

===Chinese===
“它确实有感染力，以一种粗粝的方式，”沙托夫先生承认道。

===Original===
“They are so absolutely peaceful,” said Miriam struggling on behalf of her friends with her fury at this extraordinary judgment. It had not before occurred to her that they were peaceful and that was not enough. She gazed down the vista to discover the nature of the spell they cast. “You can see them in clear light in the desert!” she exclaimed in a moment. The charm grew as she spoke. She looked forward to being alone with them again in the light of this discovery. The chill of Mr. Shatov’s indifferent response to her explanation was buried in her private acknowledgment that it was he who had forced her to discover something of the reason of her enchantment. He forced her to think. She reflected that solitude was too easy. It was necessary for certainties. Nothing could be known except in solitude. But the struggle to communicate certainties gave them new life; even if the explanation were only a small piece of the truth.﻿ ﻿… “Excuse me I leave you a moment,” he said, turning off through the maze of little figures near the door. The extraordinary new thing was that she *could* think, untroubled, in his company. She gratefully blessed his disappearing form.

===Chinese===
“它们是那样彻底地安详，”米丽亚姆说，一边为她这些朋友抗争，一边压着对这非凡判词的怒火。她此前从未想到它们是安详的、而安详还不够。她顺着展廊的纵深望去，要找出它们施下的魔咒的性质。“在沙漠里你能看见它们沐浴在清澈的光里！”过了一会儿她喊道。她一边说，魅力一边增长。她期盼着带着这个发现的光，独自同它们重聚。沙托夫先生对她这番解释的冷淡回应，其寒意被她私下的一个承认埋掉了：正是他逼她发现了她着魔的缘由的一部分。他逼她思考。她想，独处太容易了。独处对于确定性是必要的。唯有在独处中才能有所知。可是为传达确定性而作的挣扎，给了它们新的生命；哪怕解释只是真理的一小片。……“请原谅，我离开您一会儿，”他说，转身穿过门边那些小雕像的迷宫。那件新的不寻常之事是：同他在一起，她*居然能*思考，不受搅扰。她感激地祝福他远去的背影。

===Original===
“I’m going to have toast and jam,” she announced expansively when the waitress appeared.

“Bring me just a large pot of tea and some kind of sweetmeat,” said Mr. Shatov reproachfully.

“Pastries,” murmured Miriam.

“What is *pastries*” he asked mournfully.

“*Pâtisseries*” beamed Miriam.

“Ah no,” he explained patiently, “it is not that at all; I will have simply some small things in sugar.”

“No pastries; cake,” said the waitress, watching herself in the mirror.

“Ach bring me just *tea*,” bellowed Mr. Shatov.

===Chinese===
“我要烤面包片加果酱，”女侍出现时她豪爽地宣布。

“给我来一大壶茶，再随便来点什么甜食，”沙托夫先生委屈地说。

“Pâtisseries（法式糕点），”米丽亚姆轻声提点。

“*pastries* 是什么”他愁苦地问。

“*Pâtisseries*，”米丽亚姆容光焕发地说。

“啊不，”他耐心地解释，“完全不是那个；我只要一些小个的糖点心。”

“没有 pastries；有蛋糕，”女侍说，一边照着镜子端详自己。

“啊，就给我上*茶*吧，”沙托夫先生吼道。

===Original===
Several people looked round, but he did not appear to notice them and sat hunched, his overcoat coming up behind beyond his collar, his arms thrust out over the table, ending in grubby clasped hands. In a moment he was talking. Miriam sat taking in the change in the feeling of the familiar place under the influence of his unconcerned presence. There were the usual strangers strayed in from the galleries, little parties, sitting exposed at the central tables near the door; not quite at home, their eyes still filled with the puzzled preoccupation with which they had wandered and gazed, the relief of their customary conversation held back until they should have paid, out of their weary bewilderment some tribute of suitable comment; looking about the room, watching in separate uneasiness for material to carry them past the insoluble problem. They were unchanged. But the readers stood out anew; the world they had made for her was broken up. Those who came in twos and sat at the more sequestered tables, maddening her with endless conversations at cross purposes from unconsidered assumptions, were defeated. Their voices were covered by Mr. Shatov’s fluent monologue, and though her own voice, sounding startlingly in the room, seemed at once only an exclamatory unpractised reproduction of these accustomed voices, changing already their aspect and making her judgment of them rock insecurely in her mind, it was threaded into his unconcerned reality and would presently be real.

===Chinese===
好几个人回头看，他却似乎毫无觉察，弓着背坐着，大衣在身后越过领口高高耸起，两条胳膊伸过桌面，末端是一双肮脏的交握的手。转眼他又谈开了。米丽亚姆坐着，感受着这个熟悉的地方在他那毫不在乎的在场的影响下起了什么变化。照例有从展馆里误进来的生客，一小拨一小拨，暴露在门边中央的桌子旁；不那么自在，眼睛里还装着漫游张望时那种困惑的心不在焉，他们惯常的谈资被按捺着，等付过账、从疲惫的茫然里缴出几句得体的点评之后才放出来；打量着这间屋子，各自不安地搜寻着什么材料，好载着他们渡过这道无解的难题。他们没有变。可那些读者们焕然一新地凸现出来；他们为她造的世界被打散了。那些成双而来、坐在更幽僻的桌旁、凭着未经思量的臆断自说自话、把她逼得发疯的人，败下阵来。他们的声音被沙托夫先生流利的独白盖住了，而她自己的声音，在这间屋子里响得吓人，一时间竟像是这些熟悉腔调的生涩的、感叹式的复述，已经改变了它们的模样，令她对它们的评判在脑海里摇晃不稳，可它被织进了他那份毫不在意的真实里，转眼就会变成真的。

===Original===
But the solitary readers, sitting in corners over books, or perched, thoughtfully munching and sipping, with their backs to the room, on the high stools at the refreshment counter, and presently getting down to escape untouched and free, through the swing door, their unlifted eyes recovering already, through its long glass panels, the living dream of the hugely moving galleries, reproached her for her lost state.

===Chinese===
可是那些独自的读者，坐在角落里对着书，或者高踞在茶点柜台的高凳上，若有所思地咀嚼、啜饮，背对着整间屋子，过一会儿便下来，原封不动、自由自在地从弹簧门里逃出去，他们未抬起的眼睛已经透过门上长长的玻璃嵌板，重新拾起那宏大运转的展馆里流动的活梦，他们却在责备她这失落的境地。

===Original===
Mr. Shatov’s dreaming face woke to prevent her adding milk to his tea, and settled again, dwelling with his far off theme. She began listening in detail to screen her base interest in her extravagant fare. “It is a remarkable fact,” he was saying and she looked up, astonished at the sudden indistinctness of his voice. His eyes met hers severely, above the rim of his cup, “but of almost universal application,” he proceeded thickly, and paused to produce between his lips a saturated lump of sugar. She stared, horrified. Very gravely, unattained by her disgust, he drew in his tea in neat noiseless sips till the sugar disappeared﻿ ﻿… when he deftly extracted another lump from the basin and went on with his story.

===Chinese===
沙托夫先生那出神的脸醒过来，拦住她往他的茶里加奶，随即又定住，沉浸在他辽远的主题里。她开始细听，好遮掩自己对这顿奢侈茶点的卑下关心。“一个值得注意的事实是，”他正说着，她抬起头，惊异于他嗓音突然含混起来。他的目光在杯沿上方严肃地迎住她的，“而且在几乎一切场合都适用，”他含混地说下去，顿了顿，从唇间顶出一块吸饱了的糖。她瞪着，骇住了。他不慌不忙、全然不受她恶心的影响，极郑重地小口小口利落地啜着茶，直到那块糖消失……然后又灵巧地从糖钵里拈出一块，继续讲他的故事。

===Original===
The series of lumps, passing one by one without accident through their shocking task, softened in some remarkable way the history of Tourgainyeff and Madame Viardot. The protest that struggled in her to rise and express itself was held in check by his peculiar serenity. The frequent filling of his cup and the selection of his long series of lumps brought no break in his concentration.﻿ ﻿… Above the propped elbows and the cup held always at the level of his lips, his talking face was turned to hers. Expressions moved untroubled through his eyes.

===Chinese===
那一串糖块，一块接一块毫发无损地通过了它们骇人的任务，以某种了不起的方式，软化了屠格涅夫（Tourgainyeff）与维亚尔多夫人的这段掌故。在她胸中挣扎着要站起来表达的那声抗议，被他那独有的安详按住了。他一次次续杯、挑选他那长长一串糖块，也不曾中断他的专注。……在支起的双肘和永远举在唇边高度的茶杯上方，他那张谈讲的脸转向她。种种表情不受搅扰地从他的眼中流过。

===Original===
When they left the tearoom he plunged rapidly along as if unaware of his surroundings. The whole Museum was there, unexplored, and this was his first visit.
