He assented indifferently to her suggestion that they should just look at the Elgin Marbles, and stood unmoved before the groups, presently saying with some impatience that here, too, the air was oppressive and he would like to go into the freshness.

===Chinese===
离开茶室时他快步向前冲，仿佛对周遭浑然不觉。整座博物馆都在这里，未曾勘探，而这才是他头一回来。对她说“就看看埃尔金大理石雕（Elgin Marbles）吧”的提议，他淡漠地应了，在那群雕像前无动于衷地站着，不多会儿便有些不耐烦地说，这里，也是，空气憋闷，他想进到新鲜空气里去。

===Original===
Out in the street he walked quickly along brumming to himself. She felt they had been long acquainted; the afternoon had abolished embarrassments, but he was a stranger. She had nothing to say to him; perhaps there would be no more communications. She looked forward with uneasiness to the evening’s lesson. They were both tired; it would be an irretrievable failure to try to extend their afternoon’s achievement; and she would have to pass the intervening time alone with her growing incapability, while he recovered his tone at the dinner-table. The thought of him there, socially alive while she froze in her room, was intolerable. She too would go in to dinner﻿ ﻿… their present association was too painful to part upon. She bent their steps cheerfully in the direction of home. Excuse me, he said suddenly, I will take here fruits, and he disappeared into a greengrocer’s shop emerging presently munching from an open bag of grapes.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
到了街上，他快步走着，独自哼着什么。她觉得他们相识已久了；这个下午消灭了一切局促，可他仍是个陌生人。她对他无话可说；也许不会再有交流了。她不安地期待着晚上的课。两人都累了；硬要把下午的成果再延展下去，那将是无可挽回的失败；而她得独自捱过中间这段时光，守着自己愈长愈大的无能，他却在餐桌上恢复他的气色。想到他坐在那里，社交场上生气勃勃，而她在自己房间里冻僵，难以忍受。她也要进去吃晚饭……眼前这份交情，就这样散了未免太难受。她兴冲冲地把两人的脚步引向家的方向。失陪一下，他忽然说，我要在这儿买点水果，说着便钻进一家果蔬店，出来时已经对着一袋敞开的葡萄大嚼起来。……

---

===Original===
Supposing books had no names.﻿ ﻿… Villette had meant nothing for years; a magic name until somebody said it was Brussels﻿ ﻿… she was impressed by St. Paul’s dome in the morning because it was St. Paul’s. That spoilt the part about the journey; waking you up with a start like the end of a dream. St. Paul’s sticking out through the text; someone suddenly introduced to you at a gathering, standing in front of you, blocking out the general sense of things; until you began to dance, when it came back until you stopped, when the person became a person again, with a name, and special things had to be said. St. Paul’s could not be got into the general sense of the journey; it was a quotation from another world; a smaller world than Lucy Snowe and her journey.

===Chinese===
假如书没有名字。……《维莱特》（Villette）多少年里对她毫无意义；一个有魔力的名字，直到有人说那写的是布鲁塞尔……她清晨对圣保罗大教堂的穹顶心生感动，只因为它是圣保罗。这就糟蹋了关于旅程的那部分；它像一场梦的结尾那样猛地把你惊醒。圣保罗大教堂从文本里戳出来；像聚会上有人突然被引见给你，站在你面前，挡掉了事物的一般意义；直到你跳起舞来，它退回去，等你停下，那人重新变回一个人，有了名字，必须说些特别的应酬话。圣保罗大教堂没法被收进旅程的一般意义里；它是从另一个世界来的引文；一个比露西·斯诺（Lucy Snowe）和她的旅程更小的世界。

===Original===
Yet it *would* be wonderful to wake up at a little inn in the city and suddenly see St. Paul’s for the first time. Perhaps it was one of those journey moments of suddenly seeing something celebrated, and missing the impression through fear of not being impressed enough; and trying to impress your impression by telling of the thing by name﻿ ﻿… everybody had that difficulty. The vague shimmer of gaslit people round the table all felt things without being able to express them﻿ ﻿… she glowed towards the assembled group; towards everyone in the world. For a moment she looked about in detail, wanting to communicate her thought and share a moment of general agreement. Everybody was talking, looking spruce and neat and finished, in the transforming gaslight. Each one something that would never be expressed, all thinking they were expressing things and not knowing the lonely look visible behind the eyes they turned upon the world, of their actual selves as they were when they were alone. But they were all saying things they wanted to say﻿ ﻿… they *did* express themselves, in relation to each other; they grew in knowledge of each other, in approval or disapproval, tested each other and knew, behind their strange immovable positive conversations about things that were all matters of opinion perpetually shifting, in a marvellous way each others’ characters. They also knew after the first pleasant moment of meeting eyes and sounding voices when one tried to talk in their way, that one was playing them false. The glow could live for awhile when one had not met them for some time; but before the end of the meeting one was again condemned, living in heavy silence, whilst one’s mind whirled with the sense of their clear visions and the tantalising inclination to take, for life, the mould of one or other point of view.

===Chinese===
可要是能在城里一家小客栈醒来，第一次突然看见圣保罗大教堂，那*确会*妙不可言。也许那正是旅程中的时刻之一：突然看见某样名闻遐迩的东西，却因为怕自己感动得不够而错过了印象；于是靠说出那东西的名字、向人讲述，来给自己的印象加盖印记……人人都有这个难处。煤气灯下围着餐桌的人影影绰绰，人人都感觉到了什么，却说不出来……她为围坐的众人心里发热；为世上的每一个人。有一刻她细细环顾，想把自己的想法说给人听，分享一刻普遍的赞同。人人都在谈话，在幻化的煤气灯光里显得光鲜、齐整、功德圆满。每一个人都是某种永远表达不出来的东西，人人都以为自己正在表达，却不知他们转向世界的那双眼睛背后，露着各自独处时真实面目的孤独相。可他们都在说自己想说的话……他们*确实*表达了自己，相对于彼此而言；他们在或赞许或反对中加深对彼此的了解，互相试探、彼此了然，在他们那一场场奇怪的、雷打不动的、斩钉截铁的、全属见仁见智、永远漂移的交谈背后，奇妙地摸清了彼此的性情。他们在最初那个愉快的瞬间之后也明白——当四目相接、声音相闻，而你也试着用他们的方式说话时——你在对他们弄虚作假。这团暖意可以维持一阵子，当你有一段时间没同他们见面；可是聚会结束之前，你再度被判了刑，活在沉重的沉默里，而你的头脑晕眩着，满是他们那些清明的见识，还有那撩人的倾向：想把这样或那样一个观点的模子，翻铸成自己一生的。

===Original===
How *obliviously* they all talked on. She thanked them. With their talk flowing across the table, giving the central golden glow of light a feeling of permanence, her failures in life, strident about the room, were visible and audible only to herself. If she could remain silent, they would die down, and the stream of her unworthy life would merge, before he appeared, into a semblance of oneness with these other lives.﻿ ﻿… She caught the dark Russian eyes of Mr. Rodkin sitting opposite. He smiled through his glasses, his dry, sweet, large-eyed smile, his head turned listening to his neighbour. She beamed her response, relieved, as if they had had a long satisfactory conversation. He would have understood﻿ ﻿… in spite of his commercial city-life. He accepted everybody. He was the central kindliness of the room. No wonder Mrs. Bailey was so fond of him and leant upon his presence, in spite of his yawning hatred of Sundays. He was illuminated; she had his secret at last given her by Mr. Shatov. *Russian* kindliness.﻿ ﻿… Russians understand silence and are not afraid of it? Kindly silence comes out of their speech, and lies behind it, leaving things the same whatever has been said? This would be truer of him than of Mr. Shatov﻿ ﻿… moy word. Shatov at the station with his father. You never saw such a thing. Talking to the old boy as if he was a porter; snapping his head off whenever he spoke.﻿ ﻿… She pulled up sharply. If she thought of him, the fact that she was only passing the time would become visible﻿ ﻿… what was that just now, opening; about silence?

===Chinese===
他们全都谈得多么*浑然忘我*啊。她感谢他们。他们的谈话流过餐桌，给中央那团金色的光晕添上一种恒久之感，而她人生中的种种失败，在屋子四下里尖声作响，却只有她自己看得见、听得着。只要她保持沉默，它们便会平息，她那条不配的人生之流，就会在他露面之前，同这些别的人生汇成一片貌似的浑然一体。……她接住了坐在对面的罗德金先生那双俄罗斯人的黑眼睛。他隔着镜片微笑，那干爽、甜润、眼睛睁得大大的微笑，头侧着在听邻座说话。她满面春风地回应，松了口气，仿佛他们刚进行完一场漫长而尽兴的交谈。他会懂的……尽管他有他那商业化的大都市生活。他接纳每一个人。他是这间屋子中央的善良。难怪贝利太太那么喜欢他、倚仗他的存在，尽管他对星期天恨得哈欠连天。他浑身发亮；他的秘密终于由沙托夫先生交到了她手里。*俄罗斯人的*善良。……俄国人懂沉默并且不害怕沉默？善良的沉默从他们的话语里生出来，又躺在话语背后，无论说过什么，事情还是原样？这话用在他身上比用在沙托夫先生身上更真……乖乖（moy word）。沙托夫在车站上跟他父亲。你从没见过那样的场面。跟那老头子说话就像跟一个搬行李的；他一开口就被他抢白得狗血淋头。……她猛地收住。若是想起他，她这只是在打发时间的事就会露馅……方才那是什么，正要敞开的；关于沉默的什么来着？

---

===Original===
There is no need to go out into the world. Everything is there without anything; the world is added. And always whatever happens there is everything to return to. The pattern round her plate was life, alive, everything﻿ ﻿… what was that idea I used to have? Enough for one person in the world would be enough for everybody﻿ ﻿… how did it go? It was so clear, while the voice corneted out spoiling the sunshine,﻿ ﻿… “oh yes we were *very* jolly; very jolly party, talking all the time. Miss Hood’s song sounding out at intervals, *Halcyon* weather.”﻿ ﻿… “Do you ever feel how much there is everywhere?” “Nachah’s abundance?” “No. I don’t mean that. I mean that nearly everything is wasted. Not things, like soap; but the meanings of things. If there is enough for one person there is enough for everybody.” “You mean that one happy man makes the whole universe glad?” “He does. But I don’t mean that. I mean﻿—everything is wasted all the time, while people are looking about and arranging for more things.” “You would like to simplify life? You feel man needs but little here below?” “He doesn’t need *anything*. People go on from everything as if it were nothing and never seem to know there is anything.” “But isn’t it just the stimulus of his needs that keeps him going?” “Why need he keep going? that is just my point.” “*Je ne vois pas la nécessité*, you would say with Voltaire?” “The necessity of living? Then why didn’t he hang himself.” “I suppose because he taught in song what he learned in sorrow.”﻿ ﻿… How many people knew that Maeterlinck had explained in words what life was like inside? Seek ye first the Kingdom﻿ ﻿… the test is if people want you at their deathbeds. None of these people would want me at their deathbeds. Yet they all ask deliberate questions, shattering the universe. Maeterlinck would call them innocent questions about the weather and the crops, behind which they gently greet each other.﻿ ﻿… Women always know their questions are insincere, a treachery towards their silent knowledge.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
没有必要走进世界去。一切都在这里，不费一物；世界是额外奉送的。而且无论那里发生什么，永远有一切可以归来。她餐盘四周的纹样就是生活，活的，就是一切……我从前常有的那个念头是什么来着？够一个人在这世上用的，就够所有人用……它是怎么说的来着？它曾那么清楚，而那声音正像短号一样刺出来，糟蹋着这片阳光，……“哦是啊我们*特别*快活；特别快活的一场聚会，一直说个不停。胡德小姐的歌不时响起，《翠鸟晴日》（Halcyon weather）。”……“你可曾感觉到到处都是那么多东西？”“纳查（Nachah）的丰饶？”“不。我不是那个意思。我是说，几乎一切都被浪费了。不是东西，像肥皂那样的东西；是事物的意义。够一个人用的，就够所有人用。”“你是说，一个快乐的人让整个宇宙都高兴？”“是。可我不是那个意思。我是说——一切时时刻刻都被浪费着，而人们还在东张西望、忙着张罗更多的东西。”“你是想简化生活？你觉得人在这个尘世上需要的很少？”“他*什么*都不需要。人们把一切都当作无所谓地一路抛在身后，似乎从不知道有什么东西存在。”“可不正是他那些需求的刺激在推着他走吗？”“他干嘛非要走下去？这正是我要说的。”“‘我看不出有什么必要’（Je ne vois pas la nécessité），你会像伏尔泰那样这么说？”“活着的必要？那他干吗不把自己吊死。”“我想是因为他把悲伤中学到的东西，用歌唱教给了人。”……有多少人知道，梅特克林已经用语言把生命内里是什么样讲明白了？你们要先求他的国（Seek ye first the Kingdom）……试金石是，人临终时要不要你在床前。这些人谁也不会要我守在他们床前。可他们个个都在提出深思熟虑的问题，把宇宙震得粉碎。梅特克林会把这些问题称作关于天气和收成的无辜问题，他们借此温文地互致问候。……女人们永远知道自己的问题不真诚，是对她们沉默的知识的一种背叛。……

===Original===
He must read the chapter on silence and then the piece about the old man by his lamp. That would make everything clear﻿ ﻿… where was he all this time? Dinner was nearly over. Perhaps he was going out. She contemplated her blank evening. His voice sounded in the hall. How inconvenient for people with very long eyelashes to have to wear glasses she thought, engrossing herself in a sudden vision of her neighbour’s profile. He was coming through the hall from seeing somebody out of the front door. If she could be talking to someone she would feel less huge. She tried to catch Mr. Rodkin’s eye to ask him if he had read Tolstoy. Mr. Shatov had come in, bowing his deep-voiced greeting, and begun talking to Mr. Rodkin before he was in his chair, as if they were in the middle of a conversation. Mr. Rodkin answered at once without looking at him, and they went on in abrupt sentences one against the other, the sentences growing longer as they talked.

===Chinese===
他一定要读论沉默的那一章，然后读老人坐在灯旁的那一篇。那会让一切清楚起来……这一晌他都在哪儿？晚饭快吃完了。他也许要出门。她盘算着自己空白的夜晚。他的声音在门厅里响起。睫毛那么长的人还非得戴眼镜，多不方便啊，她想，一头扎进对邻座侧影的突然的凝视里。他正穿过门厅，从送客的前门回来。要是能正跟谁说上话，她就不会觉得自己这么庞大。她试着去接罗德金先生的目光，想问问他对过托尔斯泰没有。沙托夫先生进来了，深深一躬，压着嗓门问了好，人还没坐进椅子就已经对罗德金先生开了腔，仿佛他们正处在一场谈话的中段。罗德金先生头也不抬地立刻应答，两人便用一句顶着一句的短句谈下去，谈着谈着句子越来越长。

===Original===
Sissie did not hear the remark about the weather because she too was attending to the rapid Russian sentences. She was engrossed in them, her pale blue eyes speculative and serene. Miriam watched in swift glances. The brilliant colour that Mr. Shatov had seemed to distribute when he sat down, had shrunk to himself. He sat there warm and rich, with easy movements and easily moving thoughts, his mind far away, his features animated under his raised carelessly singing eyebrows, by his irascible comments on Mr. Rodkin’s rapped-out statements. The room grew cold, every object stiff with lifeless memory, as they sat talking Mr. Rodkin’s business. Everyone sitting round the table was clean-cut, eaten into by the raw edge of the winter night, gathered for a moment in the passing gaslit warmth, to separate presently and face an everlastingly renewed nothingness.﻿ ﻿… The charm of the Russian words, the fascination of grasping the gist of the theme broke in vain against the prevailing chill. If the two should turn away from each other and bend their glowing faces, their strangely secure foreign independence towards the general bleakness, its dreadful qualities would swell to a more active torment, all meanings lost in empty voices uttering words that no one would watch or explain. There was a lull. Their conversation was changing. Mr. Shatov had sat back in his chair with a Russian word that hung in the air and spread music. His brows had come down and he was glancing thoughtfully about the table. She met Mr. Rodkin’s eyes and smiled and turned again to Sissie with her remark about the weather. Sissie’s face came round surprised. She disagreed, making a perfect comment on the change that left Miriam marvelling at her steady ease of mind. She agreed in an enthusiastic paraphrase, her mind busy on the hidden source of her random emphasis. It could rest, everything could rest for awhile, for a little time to come, for some weeks perhaps.﻿ ﻿… But he would bring all those books; with special meanings in them that everyone seemed to understand and agree about; real at the beginning and then going off into things and never coming back. Why could she not understand them? Finding things without following the story was like being interested in a lesson without mastering what you were supposed to master and not knowing anything about it afterwards that you could pass on or explain. Yet there *was* something, or why did school which had left no knowledge and no facts seem so alive? Why did everything seem alive in a way it was impossible to explain? Perhaps part of the wrong of being a lazy idiot was being happy in a way no one else seemed to be happy.

===Chinese===
西西没有听见那句关于天气的评语，因为她也在听那些飞快的俄语句子。她全神听入，淡蓝的眼睛若有所思而安详。米丽亚姆用迅疾的一瞥一瞥看着。沙托夫先生坐下时仿佛播撒开的那片灿烂色彩，此刻收拢回他自身。他坐在那里，温暖而丰饶，举止从容，思绪也从容，心神远远的，抬起的眉毛漫不经心地唱着歌，眉下的五官因对罗德金先生甩出来的断语所发的那些火爆评论而生动。屋子里冷下来，每一件物件都僵满了无生命的记忆，他们坐着谈的是罗德金先生的生意。围桌而坐的每一个人轮廓分明，被冬夜生冷的边刃啃噬着，在这片流动的煤气灯光的暖意里聚上一刻，转眼就要散去，去面对一场永远翻新的虚无。……俄语的魅力，抓住题旨要义的迷醉，徒劳地撞在四下弥漫的寒气上。这两个人若是转过脸不再相对，把他们发着光的容颜、他们那种奇异的安稳的外邦人的独立，弯向这片普遍的荒凉，那些可怖的性质就会胀成一种更主动的折磨，一切意义都散失在空空洞洞的嗓音里，吐着没人要看、没人要解释的字句。谈话出现一个间歇。他们的交谈正在换向。沙托夫先生已仰回椅背，扔出一个悬在空中、荡开乐音的俄语词。他的眉毛落了下来，他若有所思地环视餐桌。她遇上罗德金先生的眼睛，笑了笑，重新转向西西，把那句关于天气的话接上。西西的脸惊讶地转过来。她表示不同意，对这变化作了一句漂亮的点评，让米丽亚姆惊叹于她心境的稳定安闲。她用一番热烈的同义转述表示同意，心里忙着琢磨她那随口强调底下藏着的来由。可以歇歇了，一切都可以歇上一阵子，歇一小段来日，也许几个星期。……可他会把那些书统统搬来；书里带着人人都似懂非懂、人人点头称是的特殊意义；开头是真的，随后就岔进各种东西里去，再也回不来。她为什么就懂不了它们呢？不跟着故事走而有所发现，就像对一堂课起了兴致却没掌握你本该掌握的东西，过后又拿不出一点能转述、能讲解的。然而那里头*确实*有东西，要不然，为什么没留下任何知识、任何事实的学校生活，回想起来竟那么活？为什么一切都以一种没法讲清楚的方式显得活？也许，做一个懒散的呆子的错处之一，就是享一种别人似乎都不曾享过的快乐。

===Original===
If one was an idiot, people like Mr. Shatov would not.﻿ ﻿… He looked straight across, a swift observant glance. She turned once more towards Sissie making herself smilingly one with the conversation that was going on between her and her further neighbour and listened eagerly across the table; “*Gracieuse*” Mr. Shatov was saying at the end of a sentence, dropping from objection to restatement. Mr. Rodkin had asked him if he did not think her pretty. That would be his word. He would have no other word. Mr. Shatov had looked considering the matter for the first time. “*Gracieuse.*” Surely that was the very last thing she could be. But he thought it.

===Chinese===
一个人若是呆子，沙托夫先生这样的人是不会……的。他径直望过来，迅疾而察鉴的一瞥。她再次转向西西，笑吟吟地把自己织进她同她另一位邻座之间的谈话里，隔着餐桌热切地听；“*风姿曼妙*（gracieuse）”沙托夫先生在一句话的收尾处说道，从反驳降为重述。罗德金先生方才问他，是否不觉得她标致。那该是他的词。他不会有别的词。沙托夫先生当时一副头一回考虑这个问题的样子。“*风姿曼妙。*”那无疑是她最当不成的一样东西。可他偏这么想。

===Original===
Grace was a quality, not an appearance. Strong-minded and plain. That, she knew, was the secret verdict of women; or, doesn’t know how to make the best of herself. She pondered, seeking in vain for any source of grace. Grace was delicacy, refinement, little willowy cattish movements of the head, the inner mind fixed always on the proprieties, making all the improprieties visible,﻿ ﻿… streaming from the back-view of their unconscious hair.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
风姿是一种品质，不是一种外表。个性太强、其貌不扬。她知道，那便是女人们心照不宣的判词；或者：不会把自己拾掇到最好。她思忖着，徒劳地搜寻风姿的任何来源。风姿是纤巧，是精致，是脑袋那一系列柔韧的、猫一般的摆动，是内心永远钉死在礼数上，从而把一切失礼照得通明，……从她们浑然不觉的后脑发丝里流淌出来。……

===Original===
A *gracieuse* effect means always deliberate *behaving*. Madame de Something. But people who keep it up can never let thoughts take their course. They must behave to their thoughts as they behave to people. When they are by themselves they can only go on mincing quietly, waiting for their next public appearance. When they are not talking they wait in an attitude, as if they were talking; ready to behave. Always on guard. Perhaps that was what Mr. Wilson meant when he said it was the business of women to be the custodians of manners.﻿ ﻿… Their “sense of good form, and their critical and selective faculties.” Then he had no right to be contemptuous of them.﻿ ﻿… “Donald Braden﻿ ﻿… *lying* across the dinner table﻿ ﻿… a drink sodden hull, *swearing* that he would never again go to a dinner-party where there were no ladies”﻿ ﻿… “Good *talk* and particularly good *stories* are not expected of women, at dinner tables. It’s their business to *steer* the conversation and head it off if it gets out of bounds.”﻿ ﻿… To simper and watch, while the men were free to be themselves, and then step in if they went beyond bounds. In other words to head the men off if they talked “improperly”; thus showing their knowledge of improprieties﻿ ﻿… “tactfully” ignoring them and leading on to something else with a gracious pose. Those were the moments when the improprieties streamed from their hair.﻿ ﻿… Somebody saying ssh, superior people talking together, modern friends-in-council, a week end in a beautiful house, *subjects* on the menu, are you high church or low church, the gleam of a woman’s body through water. “Ssh.” *Why?*﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
一种*风姿曼妙*的效果，永远意味着存心的*做派*。某某夫人（Madame de Something）。可是把这做派维持得住的人，永远没法让思想顺其自然。他们必须像对人一样对他们的思想做派。独处的时候，他们只能继续安安静静地小口咀嚼，等着下一次公开露面。不说话的时候，他们以一种姿态等待，仿佛正在说话；随时准备做派登场。永远戒备着。也许威尔逊先生说女人的天职是当礼数的守护者时，就是这个意思。……她们的“得体感，她们的批判力与鉴别力”。那他就无权轻蔑她们。……“唐纳德·布雷登……*瘫*在餐桌上……一具被酒泡酥了的空壳，*赌咒*发誓说有女士在座的晚宴他再也不去了”……“好*谈吐*，尤其是好*段子*，在餐桌上本就不该指望女人。她们的职分是*掌舵*，谈话出了界就把船头调回来。”……一味傻笑着旁观，男人们倒自由自在地当自己，等他们出了格再插手。换句话说，男人们说话“不得体”时要把他们引开；这样便显出了她们对种种失礼的知根知底……“得体地”视而不见，再摆出一个和婉的姿势引去别处。失礼正是从她们的发丝里倾泻而出的那些时刻。……什么人嘘了一声，体面人物们聚谈，现代的“议事之友”，在一所漂亮宅子里过周末，菜单上排着*议题*，你信高教会还是低教会，一个女人的身体在水中隐约放光。“嘘。”*为什么？*……

===Original===
But her impression to himself was good. A *French* impression; that was the extraordinary thing. Without any consideration that was the impression she had made. Perhaps everyone had a sort of style, and people who liked you could see it. The style of one’s family would show, to strangers as an unknown strange outside effect. Everyone had an effect.﻿ ﻿… She had an *effect*, a stamp, independent of anything she thought or felt. It ought to give one confidence. Because there would certainly be some people who would not dislike it. But perhaps he had not observed her at all until that moment and had been misled by her assumption of animation.

===Chinese===
可她给他留下的印象是好的。一种*法国式的*印象；这才是奇事。不加任何考虑，她留下的便是这样一个印象。也许人人都有一种笔致，喜欢你的人便看得见。一个人的家风笔致，落到外人眼里，便成了一种陌生的、奇特的外在效果。人人都有效果。……她有一种*效果*，一枚戳记，独立于她所想所感的一切。这本该给人信心。因为肯定会有那么一些人，不至于讨厌它。可也许他根本没留意过她，直到那一刻，是被她那副活泛的做态带偏了。

===Original===
If I tried to be gracious, I could never keep it up, because I always forget that I am visible. She called in her eyes, which must have been staring all the time blankly about the table, so many impressions had she gathered of the various groups, animated now in their unconscious relief at the approaching end of the long sitting. Here again was one of those moments of being conscious of the strange fact of her incurable illusion, and realising its effects in the past and the effects it must always have if she did not get away from it. Nearly always she must appear both imbecile and rude, staring, probably with her mouth half open, lost. Well-brought-up children were *trained* out of it. No one had dared to try and train her for long. They had been frightened, or offended, by her scorn of their brisk cheerful pose of polite interest in the *surface* of everything that was said. It was not worth doing. Polite society was not worth having. Every time one tried for awhile, holding oneself in, thinking of oneself sitting there as others were sitting, consciousness came to an end. It meant having opinions. Taking sides. It presently narrowed life down to a restive discomfort.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
我要是费心去做个优雅的人，我也维持不住，因为我总是忘记自己是看得见的。她唤回自己的眼睛——方才它们准是一直直愣愣地满桌乱扫，各色人等她已收了那么多印象，此刻他们正为这长坐将散而无意识地把劲头松了下来。这里又来了那种时刻，让她意识到自己那不治之症式的错觉这个奇怪的事实，意识到它过去的影响、以及她若摆脱不了它便注定会有的影响。她多半总是显得又痴又鲁莽，直瞪着眼，多半还半张着嘴，失魂落魄。教养好的孩子都被*训练*出来了。没有人敢长时间试着训练她。他们对她的轻蔑都吓退了，或是触怒了——她蔑视他们那种对一切所言之事的*表面*装出的轻快殷勤。不值得做。客套的社交圈不值得要。每回试着凑合一阵，拘着自己，想着自己像别人那样坐在那里，意识便到了头。那就意味着要有观点。要站队。过不了多久，生活就被挤窄成一种躁动的别扭。……

===Original===
Jan went about the streets thinking she was invisible﻿ ﻿… “and then quite suddenly I saw myself in a shop mirror. *My dear.* I got straight into an omnibus and went home. I could not stand the sight of my hips.” But with people, in a room, she never forgot she was there.

===Chinese===
简（Jan）满街走，以为自己是隐形的……“后来冷不丁地，我在一面商店的镜子里看见了自己。*亲爱的。*我立刻跳上一辆公共马车回家了。我那两条胯，我一眼也看不下去。”可是当着人、在屋子里，她从不忘记自己在场。

===Original===
The sight of Mr. Shatov waiting for her under the gas in the drawing-room gathered all her thoughts together, struggling for simultaneous expression. She came slowly across the room, with eyes downcast to avoid the dimly-lit corner where he stood, and sought rapidly amongst the competing threads of thought for some fragment that could be shaped into speech before he should make the communication she had seen waiting in his face. The sympathetic form must listen and make some understanding response. She felt herself stiffening in angry refusal to face the banishment of her tangled mass of thought by some calmly oblivious statement, beginning nowhere and leading them on into baseless discussion, impeded on her part by the pain of unstated vanishing things. They began speaking together and he halted before her formal harsh-voiced words.

===Chinese===
看见沙托夫先生在客厅煤气灯下等她，把她全部的思绪都聚拢起来，争先恐后要同时出口。她慢慢穿过房间，眼睛垂着，避开他站的那处昏暗的角落，在那几股争抢的思路里飞快地翻拣，想抢在他把那桩她已看见悬在他脸上的传达讲出来之前，捡出哪一片能先塑成话。那个善解人意的外形必须听着，并且作出某种会心的回应。她感到自己在愤怒地僵住，拒绝面对她那团乱麻般的思绪被一句心平气和的、全然忘形的话放逐——那话不知从何处起头，领着他们陷进一场无根的讨论，而她这边，被那些说不出口、正在消逝的东西硌得生疼。两人一同开口，他在她那串板着腔调、嗓音发硬的话前面停住了。

===Original===
“There is always a bad light on Saturday evenings because nearly everyone goes out,” she said and looked her demand for his recognition of the undischarged burden of her mind impatiently about the room.

“I had not observed this,” he said gently, “but now I see the light is indeed very bad.” She watched him as he spoke, waiting, counting each syllable. He paused, gravely consulting her face; she made no effort to withhold the wave of anger flowing out over the words that stood mocking her on the desolate air, a bridge, carrying them up over the stream of her mind and forward, leaving her communications behind forever. She waited, watching cynically for whatever he might offer to her dumbness, wondering whether it surprised him, rebuked as she regarded him, by his unchanged gentle lustre.

“Oh *please*” he said hurriedly, his downcast inturned smile suddenly irradiating his forehead, bringing down the eyebrows that must have gone singing thoughtfully up as he spoke about the light﻿ ﻿… a request of some kind; one of his extraordinary unashamed demands.﻿ ﻿… “You must help me. I must immediately pawn my watch. Where is a pawning shop?”

Miriam stared her consternation.

“Ah, no,” he said, his features working with embarrassment “it is not for myself. It is my friend, the Polish Doctor, who was only now here,” Miriam gazed, plunging on through relief into a chaos of bewildered admiration.

“But you hardly know him!” she exclaimed, sitting down for more leisurely contemplation.

“That is not the point,” he said seriously, taking the chair on the other side of the little table. “Poor fellow, he is not long in London, and has almost no friends. He is working in abstruse researchings, needing much spendings on materials, and is threatened by his landlady to leave his apartments.”

“Did he tell you this?” said Miriam sceptically recalling the Polish head, its smooth cold perfect beauty and indifference.

“Most certainly he told me. He must immediately have ten pounds.”

“Perhaps you would not get so much,” persisted Miriam. “And suppose he does not pay it back?”

“You are mistaken. The watch, with the chain, is worth more than the double this sum.” His face expressed a grave simple finality.

“But it is a *shame*,” she cried, jealously eyeing the decoration that seemed now to have been an essential part of their many meetings. Without this mark of opulence, he would not be quite the same.﻿ ﻿…

“Why a shame?” demanded Mr. Shatov, with his little abrupt snorting chuckle. “I shall again have my watch when my father shall send me the next portion of my allowance.” He was not counting on the return of the money! Next month, with his allowance, he would have the watch and forget the incident.﻿ ﻿… Wealth made life safe for him. People could be people to him; even strangers; not threats or problems. But even a wealthy Englishman would not calmly give ten pounds to a disreputable stranger﻿ ﻿… he would suspect him even if he were not disreputable. It *might* be true that the Pole was in honest difficulties. But it was impossible to imagine him really working at anything. Mr. Shatov did not feel this at all.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
“星期六晚上灯光总是不好，因为差不多人人都出门了，”她说，一边把她要对方承认的心头未卸之担，不耐烦地满屋子瞪过去。

“我倒没有留意过这个，”他温和地说，“不过现在我看到了，灯光确实很糟。”她看着他说话，等着，数着每一个音节。他顿住了，郑重地征询她的脸色；她不作半分努力去拦阻那股涌出的怒火，任它漫过那些冷冷地立在荒凉空气里嘲弄她的话，一座桥，把它们载过她心念的湍流、载向前方，把她的传达永远留在身后。她等着，冷眼旁观他会对她的哑口拿出什么来，纳闷这是否令他惊讶，一面这样苛评着他，一面是他那份不曾改变的、温和的光泽。

“哦*求您了*”他急忙说，他那低垂内敛的微笑突然照亮了他的额头，把那两道刚才谈灯光时想必还若有所思地一路唱着扬起的眉毛拉了下来……某种请求；他那不识惭愧的非凡索取中的一桩。……“您一定要帮我。我必须立刻把我的表当掉。当铺在哪儿？”

米丽亚姆瞪出了她的惊愕。

“啊，不，”他说，五官因窘迫而抽动着“不是为我自己。是我的朋友，那位波兰医生，方才还在这里来着，”米丽亚姆凝视着，从如释重负一头扎进一片慌乱的钦佩里。

“可你几乎不认识他呀！”她喊道，坐下来好从从容容地打量这件事。

“问题不在这里，”他正色说，在小桌另一侧的椅子上坐下。“可怜的人，他来伦敦不久，几乎没有朋友。他正做着深奥的研究，需要许多花费买材料，还受着房东太太的威胁，要被撵出寓所。”

“这些是他告诉你的？”米丽亚姆将信将疑地问，心里想起那颗波兰人的头颅，它那光洁、冰冷、完美无瑕的美和漠然。

“千真万确是他告诉我的。他必须立刻弄到十英镑。”

“兴许你当不出那么多，”米丽亚姆不依道。“再说，万一他不还呢？”

“您错了。这块表，连同链子，值这个数目的两倍还多。”他的脸上是一种郑重、简净的定论。

“可这*太不像话了*，”她喊道，嫉羡地盯着那件饰物，此刻它俨然已是他俩多次会面中不可分的一部分。少了这枚富足的标记，他就不再全然是他了。……

“为什么不像话？”沙托夫先生质问道，带着他那种短促的哼笑。“等我父亲给我寄来下一笔津贴，我就又会有一块表了。”他根本没指望那笔钱还回来！下个月，津贴一到，他就会把表赎回来，把这件事忘个干净。……财富让生活于他是安稳的。人们于他是人；连陌生人也是；不是威胁，也不是难题。可哪怕一个有钱的英国人，也不会心平气和地给一个不体面的陌生人十英镑……哪怕那人体面，他也要疑他三分。波兰人当真陷在诚实的窘境里，这*也许*是真的。可要想象他真正在做点什么研究，不可能。沙托夫先生对此毫无感觉。……

===Original===
“I’m afraid I don’t know any pawn shops,” she said, shrinking even from the pronunciation of the word. She scanned her London. They had always been there﻿ ﻿… but she had never noticed or thought of them﻿ ﻿… “I don’t remember ever having seen one; but I know you are supposed to recognise them,” here was strange useful knowledge, something picturesque floating in from somewhere﻿ ﻿… “by three gold balls hanging outside.﻿ ﻿… I *have* seen one” they were talking now, the Polish Doctor was fading away. “Yes﻿ ﻿… on a bus” his wide child’s eyes were set impersonally on what she saw, “somewhere down by Ludgate Circus.”

“I will at once go there,” he said sitting leisurely back with dreaming eyes and his hands thrust into his pockets.

“Oh no,” she cried, thrusting off the disaster, “it would be *closed*.”

“That is bad,” he reflected. “Ach, no matter. I will write to him that I come on Monday.”

“He would not get your letter until Monday.”

“That is true. I did not think of this.”

===Chinese===
“恐怕我一家当铺都不知道，”她说，连这个词的发音都让她畏缩。她把她那座伦敦扫视了一遍。它们一直在那儿……可她从未留意过、也从未想过它们……“我不记得见过一家；可我知道人应该认得出它们，”这真是新奇有用的知识，一点画意不知从哪儿漂了过来……“看门外挂着的三颗金球。……我*见*过一家”此刻他们谈开了，波兰医生渐渐淡去。“对……在公共汽车上”他那双孩子气的、睁大的眼睛非关个人地停在她看见的东西上，“就在卢德盖特广场那一带。”

“我这就到那儿去，”他说着，悠然往后一靠，眼神梦着，双手插进口袋。

“哦不行，”她喊道，一把推开那场灾祸，“那样*就关门了*。”

“这就糟了，”他寻思道。“啊，不要紧。我写信给他，说我星期一去。”

“他星期一才能收到你的信。”

“这倒是真的。我没想到这个。”

===Original===
There must be pawn shops quite near; in the Tottenham Court Road. They would still be open. Not to suggest this would be to be responsible if anything happened to the Pole.﻿ ﻿… Thrusting down through the numbed mass of her forgotten thoughts to the quick of her nature came the realisation that she was being tested and found wanting﻿ ﻿… another of those moments had come round.﻿ ﻿… She glanced into the open abyss at her own form staring up from its depths, and through her brain flew, in clear record, decisive moments of the past; her self, clearly visible, clothed as she had been clothed, her poise and bearing as she had flinched and fled. Here she was, unchanged, not caring what happened to the man, so long as her evening was not disturbed﻿ ﻿… she was a murderess. This was the hidden truth of her life. Above it her false face turned from thing to thing, happy and forgetful for years, until a moment came again to show her that she could face and let slip the risk of anything to anyone, anywhere, rather than the pain of renouncing personal realisation. Already she was moving away. A second suggestion was in her mind and she was not going to make it. She glanced enviously at the unconscious kindliness lolling in the opposite chair. It was clear to its depths; unburdened by spectres of remembered cruelty.﻿ ﻿… But there was also something else that was different﻿ ﻿… easy circumstances; the certainty, from the beginning, of self-realisation.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
附近就该有当铺；就在托特纳姆宫路上。这会儿多半还开着。不把这个说出来，波兰人若出了什么事，她就算有一份责任。……从她那团麻木的、被遗忘的思绪底下，直扎到她天性的痛处，浮起一个认识：她正在受试，而且被试出了亏欠……那样的时刻又转回来了。……她朝敞开的深渊里瞥了一眼，看见自己的身影正从渊底向上瞪视，一段段决断的往事清清楚楚掠过她的脑际；她自己，清晰可见，穿着她当时的衣着，摆着她退缩逃开时的仪态。她在这里，分毫未变，不在乎那人会怎样，只要她的夜晚不受打扰……她是个杀人凶手。这便是她生命里藏着的真相。在这真相之上，她那张假脸从一件事转向另一件事，快活而健忘地过了多年，直到又一个时刻来临，向她证明：为了不承受放弃个人实现的痛，她可以对任何人、在任何地方、面对任何风险，都放得开手。她已经在抽身了。第二个建议就在她心里，而她不打算提。她嫉妒地望了望对面椅子里那毫无知觉的善良。它清澈见底；不曾被记忆中残忍的幽灵压着。……可是还有另一样不同的东西……优裕的境遇；自呱呱坠地起，对自我实现的笃定。……

===Original===
“Perhaps someone in the house could tell you.” Oh *stupidity*; blurting out *anything* to hide behind the sound of voices.

“Possibly. But it is a delicate matter. I could not for instance mention this matter to Mrs. Bailey.”

“Do you like him? Didn’t you find him amongst those dreadful men looking like monkeys?”

“At this Vienna café. Ah indeed it is dreadful there upstairs.”

“He is very handsome.”

“The Poles are perhaps the most beautiful of European peoples. They have also *immense* courage”﻿ ﻿… unsuspicious thoughtfully talking face, lifting her up and out again into light and air.﻿ ﻿… “But the Pole is undoubtedly the most *treacherous* fellow in Europe.” Grave live eyes flashed across at her, easily, moulding the lounging form into shapeliness. “He is at the same time of the most distinguished mentality.” Why should anyone help a distinguished mentality to go on being treacherous? “And in particular is this true of the Polish *Jew*. There are in all European universities amongst the very most distinguished professors and students very many Polish Jews.” Le Juif Polonais﻿ ﻿… *The Bells*. It was strange to think of Polish Jews going on in modern everyday life.﻿ ﻿… But if Poles were so evil.﻿ ﻿… That was Dr. Veslovsky’s expression. Cold evil.

===Chinese===
“兴许这屋里什么人能告诉你。”哦*愚蠢*；信口*胡乱*抛出一句话，好躲进人声后面。

“也许吧。可这是一件微妙的事。比如，我就不能把这件事对贝利太太提。”

“你喜欢他吗？你不是在那群长得像猴子的可怕男人中间发现他的吗？”

“在那家维也纳咖啡馆。啊，楼上那地方确实糟糕。”

“他非常英俊。”

“波兰人也许是欧洲诸民族里最美的。他们还有*非凡*的勇气”……那不疑不忌的、边想边说的面容，把她重新托举起来，送回光与空气里。……“可波兰人也无疑是欧洲最*奸诈*的家伙。”一双严肃的活眼睛轻巧地朝她一闪，把那懒散的身影捏出了形。“他同时又具有最出众的心智。”凭什么要帮一个出众的心智继续奸诈下去？“这一点在波兰*犹太人*身上尤其真确。在所有欧洲的大学里，最杰出的教授和学生当中，有许许多多波兰犹太人。”《波兰犹太人》（Le Juif Polonais）……《钟声》（The Bells）。想到波兰犹太人在现代日常生活里照常过日子，真奇怪。……可要是波兰人这么邪恶。……那是维斯洛夫斯基医生（Dr. Veslovsky）的说法。冷冰冰的恶。

===Original===
“There was an awful thing last week in Woburn Place.”

“Yes?”

“Mrs. Bailey told me about it. There was a girl who owed her landlady twenty-five shillings. She threw herself out of her bedroom window on the top floor because her landlady spoke to her about it.”

“That is *terrible*” whispered Mr. Shatov. His eyes were dark with pain; his face shrunk as if with cold. “That could *never* happen in Russia,” he said reproachfully.

“Why not?”

“No. In Russia such a thing is impossible. And in student circles most particularly. This young girl living in this neighbourhood without salary was probably some sort of student.”

“Why? She might have been a governess out of work or a poor clerk. Besides I thought people were always committing suicide in Russia.”

“That is of course a gross exaggeration. There are certainly suicides in Russia as everywhere. But in Russia suicide, which does certainly occur in abnormally high frequency amongst the young intelligentsia, arises from trouble of *spirit*. They are psychopath. There comes some spiritual crisis and﻿—phwtt﻿—.﻿ ﻿… It is characteristic of the educated Slav mind to lose itself in face of abstractive insolubilities. But for need of *twenty-five shillings*. I find in this something peculiarly horrible. In midst of your English civilisation it is pure-barbaric.”

“There has not been any civilisation in the world yet. We are still all living in caves.” The quotation sounded less convincing than at Wimpole Street.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
“上礼拜沃本广场出了件可怕的事。”

“是吗？”

“贝利太太跟我说的。有个姑娘欠了房东太太二十五先令。就因为房东太太说了她几句，她从顶楼自己房间的窗户跳了下去。”

“这*太可怕了*”沙托夫先生低声说。他的眼睛因痛苦而发黑；他的脸缩着，仿佛受了冻。“这种事在俄国*永远*不可能发生，”他责备道。

“为什么不可能？”

“不可能。在俄国这种事是不可能的。在学生圈子里尤其不可能。这个住在这附近的年轻姑娘，没有薪水，多半是某种学生。”

“为什么？她也可能是个失业的家庭女教师，或者一个穷职员。再说我还以为俄国人老是自杀呢。”

“那当然是言过其实。俄国同任何地方一样，当然有自杀。可在俄国，自杀——它在年轻的知识界里发生的频率确实高得反常——源自*精神*的烦恼。他们是心理病态者。来一场精神的危机，然后——噗——。……受过教育的斯拉夫头脑在抽象的无解难题面前迷失自己，这才是它的特性。可是为着*二十五先令*的短缺。我在这里面觉出一种特别的恐怖。在你们英国文明的正中央，这是纯然的野蛮。”

“世界上至今还不曾有过任何文明。我们都还住在洞穴里。”这句引文，在温波尔街说出口时可比现在有说服力。……

===Original===
“That is too superficial. Pardon me, but it implies a too slight knowledge of what has been in the past and what still persists in various developmental stages.” Miriam felt about among the statements which occurred to her in rapid succession, all contradicting each other. Yet somebody in the world believed each one of them.﻿ ﻿… Mr. Shatov was gravely waiting, as if for her agreement with what he had just said. Far away below her clashing thoughts was something she wanted to express, something he did not know, and that yet she felt he might be able to shape for her if only she could present it. But between her and this reality was the embarrassment of a mind that could produce nothing but quotations. She had no mind of her own. It seemed to be there when she was alone; only because there was no need to express anything. In speech she could produce only things other people had said and with which she did not agree. None of them expressed the underlying thing.﻿ ﻿… Why had she not brought down Maeterlinck?

===Chinese===
“这就太浮浅了。恕我直言，这意味着对往昔曾经存在的东西、对至今仍以各种发育阶段存续着的东西，了解得太少。”米丽亚姆在她脑中飞快接连冒出的那些彼此抵牾的论断里翻摸。可世界上每一种论断都有人信。……沙托夫先生郑重地等着，仿佛在等她同意他刚说过的话。在她那些相击的念头下面，远远的，有一样她想表达的东西，一样他不知道、而她又觉得他或许能替她塑形的东西——只要她能把它端出来。可横在她与这个真实之间的，是一颗只能生产引文的心灵的窘迫。她没有自己的头脑。独自一人时它似乎在；只因为无需表达什么。一开口，她只能搬出别人说过、而她并不同意的话。没有一句够得着底下那个东西。……她怎么没把梅特克林带下来？

===Original===
Mr. Shatov’s quiet waiting had ended in a flow of eager talk. She turned unwillingly. Even he could go on, leaving things unfinished, talking about something else.﻿ ﻿… But his mind was steady. The things that were there would not drop away. She would be able to consider them﻿ ﻿… watching the effect of the light of other minds upon the things that floated in her own mind; so dreadfully *few* now that he was beginning to look at them; and all ending with the images of people who had said them, or the bindings of books where she had found them set down﻿ ﻿… yet she felt familiar with all points of view. Every generalisation gave her the clue to the speaker’s mind﻿ ﻿… wanting to hear no more, only to criticise what was said by pointing out, whether she agreed with it or no, the opposite point of view.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
沙托夫先生安静的等待，化成了一股热切的谈锋。她不情愿地转回来。连他也能这样继续下去，把事情撂在半截，谈起别的来。……可他的头脑是稳的。那些在场的东西不会掉落。她将能慢慢细想……看着别的心灵的光，照在她自己心里漂浮的那些东西上；如今他开始注视它们了，它们显得那么*少得可怜*；而且全都结束在说过它们的人的身影里，或在她发现它们被记下的那些书的封皮里……然而她对一切观点都觉亲切。每一个概括都给她一条通往言说者内心的线索……不想再听了，只想批评所说的话，办法是不论同意与否，都把相反的观点指出来。……

===Original===
She smiled encouragingly towards his talk, hurriedly summoning an appearance of attention into her absent eyes while she contemplated his glowing pallor and the gaze of unconscious wide intelligence, shining not only towards her own, but also with such undisturbed intentness upon what he was describing. She could think later on, next year, when he had gone away leaving her to confront her world with a fresh armoury. As long as he stayed, he would be there, without effort or encouragement from her, filling her spare hours with his untired beauty, drawing her along his carefully spun English phrases, away from personal experiences, into a world going on independently of them; unaware of the many scattered interests waiting for her beyond this shabby room, and yet making them shine as he talked, newly alight with rich superfluous impersonal fascination, no longer isolated, but vivid parts of a whole, growing more and more intelligible as he carried her further and further into a life he saw so distinctly, that he made it hers, too quickly for her to keep account of the inpouring wealth.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
她朝着他的谈锋鼓励地微笑，匆匆召来一副专注的面容安进自己那双走神的眼睛里，一边端详他发亮的苍白，和那双无自觉的、宽阔的智性的凝望——那凝望不只朝向她，也以那样不受打扰的专注，落在他所描绘的东西上。她可以往后再想，明年，等他离去、留她带着一副崭新的甲胄去面对她的世界。只要他还在，他就会在那里，不用她费一分力、一分心，用他不倦的美好充满她零碎的时辰，牵着她沿他精心纺出的英语句子走，离开切身的经验，走进一个不依赖他们而运转的世界；浑然不觉这间寒酸的房间之外，还有多少零散的兴味在等她，却偏借着他的谈讲让它们发亮，重新燃起一种丰饶的、多到溢出的、非个人的魅力，不再孤立，而是一个整体的鲜活部分，随着他把她越带越远、带进一种他看得如此分明的生活，变得越来越可解——他把它变作她的，快得她来不及清点这源源注入的财富。……

===Original===
She beamed in spacious self-congratulation and plunged into the midst of his theme in holiday mood. She was in a theatre, without walls, her known world and all her memories spread, fanwise about her, all intent on what she saw, changing, retreating to their original form, coming forward, changing again, obliterated, and in some deep difficult way challenged to renewal. The scenes she watched opened out one behind the other in clear perspective, the earlier ones remaining visible, drawn aside into bright light as further backgrounds opened. The momentary sound of her own voice in the room encouraging his narrative, made no break; she dropped her remarks at random into his parentheses, carefully screening the bright centres as they turned one by one into living memories.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
她满面春风，带着宽绰的自得，以假日的心境一头扎进他谈锋的中心。她在一座没有墙的剧院里，她那个已知的世界连同她全部的记忆，如扇面在她四周铺开，全都专注于她之所见，变化着，退回原本的形貌，又上前来，再度变化，抹除，又以某种深而艰难的方式被召唤着更新。她观看的一幕幕场景在清晰的透视里一幕向一幕后方展开，较早的那些仍看得见，被拉开到明亮的光里，作更远处再开新背景的衬幕。她自己的嗓音在房间里偶尔一响、给他的叙述打气，并不造成中断；她把她的点评随手丢进他的括号里，小心地遮挡着那些明亮的中心，看它们一个接一个变成活的记忆。……

===Original===
Suddenly she was back withering in the cold shabby room before the shock of his breaking off to suggest with a swift personal smile that she herself should go to Russia. For a moment she stared at him. He waited, smiling gently. It did not matter that he thought her worthy.﻿ ﻿… The conviction that she had already been to Russia, that his suggestion was foolish in its recommendation of a vast superfluous undertaking, hung like a veil between her and the experiences she now passed through in imagining herself there. The very things in the Russian student circles that had most appealed to her, would test and find her out. She would be one of those who would be mistrusted for not being sufficiently careless about her dress and hair. It would not suit her to catch up her hair with one hairpin. She would not be strong enough to study all day and half the night on bread and tea. She was sure she could not associate perpetually with men students, even living and sharing rooms with them, without the smallest flirtation. If she were wealthy like he, she would not so calmly accept having all things in common; poor she would be uneasy in dependence on other students. She sat judged. There was a quality behind all the scenes, something in the Russians that she did not possess. It was the thing that made him what he was.﻿ ﻿… It answered to a call that was being made all the time to everyone, everywhere. Yet why did so many of them *drink*?

===Chinese===
突然间她回来了，在这间寒冷、寒酸的房间里萎缩着，承受着他突然收住话头的震动——他换上一抹飞快的、个人的微笑，建议她本人到俄国去。有一刻她直瞪着他。他等着，温和地笑着。他以为她配得上，这不要紧。……那种她早已去过俄国的确信，那种他的建议愚蠢在怂恿一桩浩大而无谓之举的判语，像一面纱，垂在她与她此刻想象自己身处其间的那种种经验之间。俄国学生圈子里最打动她的那些东西，恰恰会考验她、试出她的原形。她会属于那种因为对衣着和头发不够漫不经意而遭疑心的人。用一根发针把头发拢上去，不合她的性子。整天加半夜靠面包和茶水用功，她撑不住。她敢肯定，自己没法同男学生没完没了地交往，哪怕同住、合用房间，连最小的一点调情都没有。她若像他那样有钱，也不会那么心平气和地接受一切共有；穷，则靠别的同学过活，她会不安。她坐在那里受审。所有那些场景背后有一种品质，某种俄罗斯人有而她没有的东西。正是那东西把他造就成他这样。……它应和着一种时时刻刻向每一个人、在每一处发出的召唤。可他们当中为什么那么多人*酗酒*？

===Original===
“Well?” said Mr. Shatov. The light was going down. “*What* is this?” he asked staring up impatiently at the lessening flame. “Ah it is simply *stupid*.” He hurried away and Miriam heard his voice shouting down to Mrs. Bailey from the staircase as he went, and presently in polite loud-toned remonstrance from the top of the basement stairs. The gas went up, higher than it had been before. It must be eleven. It was not fair to keep the gas going for two people. She must wind up the sitting and send him away.

“What a piece of English stupidity,” he bellowed gently, coming back across the room.

“I suppose she is obliged to do it,” said Miriam feeling incriminated by her failure to resent the proceeding in the past.

“How *obliged*?”

“She has had an awful time. She was left penniless in Weymouth.”

“That is bad; but it is no cause for *stupidity*.”

“I know. She doesn’t understand. She managed quite well with lodgers; she will never make boarders pay. It’s no use giving her hints. The house is full of people who don’t pay their bills. There are people here who have paid nothing for eighteen months. She has even lent them money.”

“Is it possible?” he said gravely.

“And the Irish journalist *can’t* pay. He is a home-ruler.”

“He is a most distinguished-looking man. Ah but she is *stupid*.”

“She can’t *see*” said Miriam﻿—he was *interested*; even in *these* things. She dropped eagerly down amongst them. The whole evening and all their earlier interchange stood far off, shedding a relieving light over the dismal details and waiting to be resumed, enriched by this sudden excursion﻿—“that when better people come she ought to alter things. It isn’t that she would think it wrong, like the doctor who felt guilty when he bought a carriage to make people believe he had patients, though of course speculation *is* wrong”﻿—she felt herself moving swiftly along, her best memories with her in the cheerful ring of her voice, their quality discernible by him, a kind of reply to all he had told her﻿—“because she believes in keeping up appearances; but she doesn’t know how to make people comfortable.” She was creating a wrong impression but with the right voice. Without Miss Scott’s suggestions, the discomforts would never have occurred to her.

“Ah she is *stupid*. That is the whole thing.” He sat forward stretching and contracting his hands till the muscles cracked; his eyes, flashing their unconcerned contemptuous judgment, were all at once the brilliant misty eyes of a child about to be quenched by sudden sleep.

“*No*,” she said resentfully, “she wants good people, and when they come she has to make all she can out of them. If they stayed she would be able to afford to do things better. Of course they don’t come back or recommend her; and the house is always half empty. Her *best* plan would be to fill it with students at a fixed low figure.” Miss Scott again﻿ ﻿… his attention was wandering﻿ ﻿… “The dead *flowers*,” he was back again, “in dirty water in a cracked vase; Sissie rushing out, while breakfast is kept waiting, to buy just enough butter for one meal.”

“Really?” he giggled.

“She has been most awfully good to me.”

“Why not?” he chuckled.

“Do you think you will go and see your Polish friend tomorrow?” She watched anxiously.

“Yes,” he conceded blinking sleepily at the end of a long yawn. “I shall perhaps go.”

“He might be driven to desperation,” she muttered. Her accomplished evening was trembling in the balance. Its hours had frittered away the horrible stranger’s chance.

“Ah no,” said Mr. Shatov with a little laugh of sincere amusement, “Veslovski will not do foolish things.” She rose to her feet on the tide of her relief, meeting, as she garnered all the hours of her long day and turned with an out-spreading sheaf of questions towards the expanses of evening leisure so safely at her disposal in the oncrowding tomorrows, the rebuke of the brilliantly burning midnight gas.

===Chinese===
“怎么样？”沙托夫先生说。灯光在沉下去。“*这是*怎么搞的？”他不耐烦地抬头瞪着那愈来愈小的火苗。“啊，这简直*愚蠢*。”他匆匆走开，米丽亚姆听见他一路下楼对贝利太太喊，随即在地下室楼梯口客气地大声理论。煤气拧大了，比先前任何时候都亮。准是十一点了。为两个人点着煤气耗着，不公平。她得把这场坐谈收尾、送他走。

“好一出英国式的愚蠢，”他轻轻地吼着，穿过房间走回来。

“我想她是迫不得已，”米丽亚姆说，为自己从前从未对这做法生过气而觉得受了牵连。

“怎么个*不得已*法？”

“她吃过可怕的苦头。她在韦茅斯被抛下时身无分文。”

“那很糟；可这不是*愚蠢*的理由。”

“我知道。她不明白。招房客那阵子她经营得挺好；房饭全包的客人她永远收不上钱。给她递话也没用。这屋里满是欠账的人。有人十八个月一个子儿没付过。她甚至还借钱给他们。”

“竟有这种事？”他郑重地说。

“再说那位爱尔兰记者*付不起*。他是个地方自治派。”

“他是个相貌十分出众的人。啊，可她*蠢*。”

“她*看不见*”米丽亚姆说——他*感兴趣*了；连对这些事也感兴趣。她急切地一头扎进这些细节里去——整个晚上、他们先前的一切交流都退得远远的，把一层宽解的光洒在这些惨淡的细节上，等着被重新拾起，因这趟突发的岔道而更见丰饶——“等更好的人来了，她该把种种改一改。倒不是她会像那位买马车让人相信自己有病人、因而愧疚的医生那样，觉得这是错的，虽然投机取巧*当然*是错的”——她感到自己顺流疾行，最好的记忆都随着她清亮的嗓音在场，它们的品质他辨得出来，是对他讲给她听的一切的一种回答——“因为她信奉撑场面；可她不知道怎么让人舒服。”她在造成一个错误的印象，用的却是正确的嗓音。若没有斯科特小姐的种种提示，这些不舒心她根本不会想到。

“啊她*蠢*。症结全在这里。”他坐上前去，一伸一缩着双手，捏得指节噼啪作响；他的眼睛闪着那种满不在乎的轻蔑评断，忽然又变成一个孩子的那种亮晶晶、雾蒙蒙的眼睛，眼看要被突然降临的睡眠扑灭。

“*不*，”她忿忿地说，“她要的是好人，好人来了她就得从他们身上多挤出些。他们若住下，她就有钱把诸事办得像样些。他们自然不再回来，也不荐别人来；房子永远半空着。她*最好*的办法是用一个固定的低价住满学生。”又是斯科特小姐……他的注意力在游移……“那些枯死的花，”他又回来了，“插在裂了缝的花瓶的脏水里；西西赶着出门，早餐都晾在一旁，只买一顿饭刚够用的黄油。”

“真的吗？”他咯咯笑道。

“她对我一直好得不得了。”

“那不是应该的吗？”他呵呵笑道。

“你觉得你明天会去看你的波兰朋友吗？”她不安地注视着。

“会，”他让步道，在长长的哈欠末尾睡意惺忪地眨着眼。“我也许去。”

“他兴许会被逼上绝路的，”她喃喃道。她那圆满的夜晚悬在天平上颤动。它的几个钟头，已经把那可怕陌生人的一线机会磨掉了。

“啊不会，”沙托夫先生发出一声真心觉得有趣的轻笑，“维斯洛夫斯基不会做蠢事。”她在如释重负的潮水上站起身，正当她把她漫长一天的时辰尽数收割、抱起一捧铺展开的问题，转向纷至沓来的明日里稳稳听凭她处置的夜晚闲暇的旷野时，迎面撞上午夜煤气灯雪亮燃烧的责难。

===Original===
“But tell me; how has Mrs. Bailey been so good?” He sat conversationally forward as if it were the beginning of the evening.

“Oh well.” She sought about distastefully amongst the phrases she had collected in descriptions given to her friends, conveying nothing. Mr. Shatov knowing the framework, would see the detail alive and enhance her own sense of it. She glanced over the picture. Any single selection would be misleading. There was enough material for days of conversation. He was waiting eagerly, *not* impatient after all of personal experiences. Yet nothing could be told.﻿ ﻿…

“You see she lets me be amphibious.” Her voice smote her. Mrs. Bailey’s kindliness was in the room. She was squandering Mrs. Bailey’s gas in an effort that was swiftly transforming itself under the influence of her desire to present an adequate picture of her own separate life. His quickening interest drove her on. She turned her eyes from the gas and stared at the carpet, her picture broken up and vanishing before the pathos of its threadbare faded patterns.

“I’m neither a lodger nor a boarder,” she recited hurriedly. “I have all the advantages of a boarder; the use of the whole house. I’ve had this room and the piano to myself for years, on Sunday mornings until dinner time, and when there are interesting people I can go down to dinner. I do for weeks on end sometimes, and it is so convenient to be able to have meals on Sundays.”

“It is really a most *admirable* arrangement,” he said heartily.

“And last year I had a bicycle accident. I was brought back here with a very showy arm; in a cab. Poor Mrs. Bailey fainted. It was not at all serious. But they gave me their best room, the one behind this, for weeks and waited upon me most beautifully, and mind you they did not expect any compensation, they knew I could not afford it.”

“An injury that should disable for so many weeks shall not have been a light one.”

===Chinese===
“不过告诉我；贝利太太是怎么对你好的？”他朝前坐成谈话的姿势，仿佛夜晚才刚开始。

“哦，这个嘛。”她没滋味地在那些她攒下的、讲给朋友们听的描述里翻拣着，全都辞不达意。沙托夫先生认得这里的框架，会看见鲜活的细节，还会加深她自己对它的感受。她扫了一眼这幅图景。单挑哪一样都会误导。这里的材料够谈上几天。他急切地等着，对切身的经验，终究*不是*不耐烦。可是什么也没法讲。……

“您瞧，她让我过着两栖的日子。”她的声音刺了她自己一下。贝利太太的善良就在这间屋子里。她正挥霍着贝利太太的煤气，卖着一把力气，而这力气正在她想把自己独立生活的图景描画周全的愿望之下，飞快地变了性质。他那越烧越旺的兴趣推着她走。她把眼睛从煤气灯上挪开，瞪着地毯，她的图景在那磨秃褪色的花纹的哀婉面前，碎掉、消散了。

“我既不算房客也不算包饭的客人，”她急急地背诵道。“包饭客人的一切便利我都有；整栋房子随我用。这间屋子连那架钢琴，多少年来星期天早上一直到开饭都归我一个人，遇上有趣的人我还可以下楼吃饭。有时我一连几个星期天天如此，星期天能在这儿用饭，实在太方便了。”

“这真是一种最*可钦佩*的安排，”他由衷地说。

“去年我出了次自行车事故。人家用一辆马车把我送回来，一条胳膊煞是好看。可怜的贝利太太晕了过去。伤一点也不重。可他们把最好的房间给了我，就在这间后面那间，一连几个星期，伺候得我美极了，而且您听着，他们不图任何报偿，他们知道我出不起。”

“能让您这么多星期动弹不得的伤，就不会是轻伤。”

===Original===
“That was the doctor. You see it was Saturday. It was more than an hour before they could find anyone at all, and then they found a small surgeon in Gower Street. He stitched up my arm with a rusty darning needle taken from Mrs. Bailey’s workbasket just as it was. I told him I had some carbolic in my room; but he said Nevorr mind that. I’m not one of yrr faddists, and bound it all up and I came down to dinner. I had just come back from the first week of my holiday; bicycling in Buckinghamshire, perfect, I never felt so well in my life. I was going to Paris the next day.”

===Chinese===
“那是医生说的。您瞧，那天是星期六。过了一个多钟头他们才总算找到人，找到的是高尔街一个小外科。他用一根从贝利太太针线篮里现拿的生锈的织补针，就那么给我缝了胳膊。我告诉他我屋里有一点石炭酸；可他说甭介那个。俺可不是你们那号讲穷讲究的人，包扎完我就下楼吃饭去了。我那时刚休假回来一个星期；在白金汉郡骑车，再完美不过，我一辈子没那么健康过。我第二天就要去巴黎。”

===Original===
“That was indeed most unfortunate.”

===Chinese===
“那真是万分不幸了。”

===Original===
“Well I don’t know. I was going with a woman I did not really know. I meant to go, and she had been thinking of going and knew Paris and where to stay cheaply and suggested we should join forces. A sort of marriage of convenience. I was not really disappointed. I was relieved; though awfully sorry to fail her. But everyone was so kind I was simply astonished. I spent the evening on the sofa in the dining-room; and they all sat quietly about near me. One man, a Swede, who had only just arrived, sat on the end of the sofa and told Swedish folk stories in a quiet motherly voice, and turned out afterwards to be the noisiest, jolliest, most screamingly funny man we have ever had here. About eleven o’clock I felt faint and we discovered that my arm must have broken out again some time before. Two of the men rushed off to find a doctor and brought an extraordinary little old retired surgeon with white hair and trembling hands. He wheezed and puffed and bound me up afresh and went away refusing a fee. I wanted some milk, and the Swede went out at midnight and found some somewhere.﻿ ﻿… I come back with at least one cow or I come not at all.﻿ ﻿… Of course a week later I had stitch abscesses.”

===Chinese===
“嗯，也难说。同行的是个我并不真认识的女人。我本要去，她也一直想去，又熟巴黎，知道住哪儿便宜，就提议我们搭伙。一种权宜的联姻。我谈不上失望。是松了口气；虽然爽约了她，心里难过得要命。可人人都那么好，我简直惊呆了。那天晚上我躺在饭厅的沙发上；他们都安安静静坐在我近旁。有个人，一个瑞典人，刚到没多久，坐在沙发一头，用一种安静的、慈母般的嗓音讲瑞典民间故事，后来才知道，他竟是这栋房子里住过的最吵闹、最快活、最笑死人的男人。十一点来钟我觉得发晕，我们才发现我的胳膊准是早些时候又裂开了。两个男人飞奔出去找医生，领来一位不同寻常的、白发手颤的退休小个子老外科。他喘着气、鼓着劲给我重新包好，分文不取地走了。我想喝点牛奶，那瑞典人半夜跑出去，不知从哪儿弄来了。……‘俺要是不至少牵回头奶牛来，就干脆不回来。’……当然，一星期后我发了缝线脓肿。”

===Original===
“But this man was a *criminal*.”

“Yes wasn’t it abominable. Poor man. The two doctors who saw my arm later said that many limbs have been lost for less. He counted on my being in such good health. He told Mrs. Bailey I was in splendid health. But he sent in a big bill.”

“I sincerely trust you did not pay this.”

“I sent him a description of his operation, told him the result and said that my friends considered that I ought to prosecute him.”

“Certainly it was your duty.”

“I don’t know. I hate cornering people. It would not have made him different and I am no better than he is.”

“That is a most extraordinary point of view.”

“I was sorry afterwards that I had written like that.”

“Why?”

“Because he threw himself into Dublin Harbour a year later. He must have been in *fearful* difficulties.”

“No excuse for criminal neglect.”

===Chinese===
“可这个人是个*罪犯*。”

“可不是嘛，恶劣透了。可怜的人。后来看过我胳膊的两位医生说，多少条胳膊腿都是为比这轻的伤没的。他全指望我身子骨棒。他告诉贝利太太我身体好极了。可他寄来了一张大账单。”

“我真诚地希望您没有付这笔钱。”

“我把他那台手术的经过给他描写了一遍，告诉了他结果，还说我的朋友们认为我该去告他。”

“这当然是您的义务。”

“我不知道。我恨把人逼到墙角。那不会让他变好，我也并不比他好。”

“这是一种最不寻常的观点。”

“后来我就后悔那样写信了。”

“为什么？”

“因为一年之后他跳了都柏林港。他准是陷在*可怕*的困境里。”

“这不能开脱玩忽人命。”

===Original===
“The most wonderful thing in the accident itself,” pursued Miriam firmly, grasping her midnight freedom and gazing into the pattern her determination that for another few minutes no one should come up to interrupt, “was being so near to death.” She glanced up to gauge the effect of her improvisation. The moment she was now intent upon had not been “wonderful.” She would not be able to substantiate it; she had never thought it through. It lay ahead now for exploration if he wished, ready to reveal its quality to her for the first time﻿ ﻿… he was sitting hunched against the wall with his hands driven into his pockets, waiting without resistance, with an intentness equal to her own﻿ ﻿… she returned gratefully to her carpet. “It was a skid,” she said feeling the oily slither of her front tyre. “I fell with my elbow and head between the horses’ heels and the wheel of a dray. The back-thrown hoof of the near horse caught the inner side of my arm, and for a long long time I saw the grey steel rim of the huge wheel approaching my head. It was strained back with all my force, my elbow pressing the ground, but I thought it could not miss me. There was a moment of absolute calm; *indifference* almost. It came after a feeling of hatred and yet pity for the wheel. It was so awful, wet glittering grey, and relentless; and stupid, it could not help going on.”

===Chinese===
“事故本身最奇妙的地方，”米丽亚姆坚定地往下讲，攥紧她这份午夜的自由，凝望着地毯的纹样，拿定了主意再有几分钟谁也不许上楼来打扰，“是离死亡那么近。”她抬眼估量一下她这番即兴发挥的效果。她此刻卖力铺陈的那一刻，本来并不“奇妙”。她论证不出它来；她从未把它想透过。此刻它摊在前头，随他来勘探，准备头一回向她显出它的品性……他缩着背靠墙坐着，双手深深插在口袋里，不加抗拒地等着，那份专注不输于她……她感激地回到她的地毯上。“是打了个滑，”她说，感觉到前胎那油滑的一溜。我摔下去，胳膊肘和脑袋正夹在马蹄和一辆运货马车的轮子中间。左边那匹马向后甩的马蹄擦着我胳膊的内侧，好长好长一段时间，我眼看着那只巨大车轮的灰色钢圈朝我的脑袋压过来。我拼尽全力把脑袋往后仰，胳膊肘抵着地，可我想它躲不过去了。有那么一刻绝对的平静；几乎是*无动于衷*。它来在一种恨意、继而又是对车轮的怜悯之后。它那么可怕，湿漉漉闪着灰光，毫不留情；而且蠢，它不由自主地只能往前走。”

===Original===
“This was indeed a most *remarkable* psychological experience. It happens rarely to be so near death with full consciousness. But this absence of fear must be in you a personal idiosyncracy.”

“But I *was* afraid. The thing is that you don’t go *on* feeling afraid. Do you see?”

“I hear what you say. But while there is the chance of life the instinct of self-preservation is so strong”﻿ ﻿…

“But that is the surprise; the tumult in your body, something surging up and doing things without thinking.”

“Instinctive nervous reaction.”

“But there is something else. In the moment you are *sure* you are going to be killed, death changes. You wait, for the moment after.”

“That is an illusion, the strength of life in you that cannot, midst good health, accept death. But tell me; your arm was certainly broken?” His gently breathed question took away the sting of his statement.

“No. The wheel went over it just above the bend of the elbow. I did not feel it, and got up feeling only a little dizzy just for a moment and horribly annoyed at the crowd round me. But the two men who were riding with me told me afterwards that my face was grey and my eyes quite *black*.”

“That was shock.” He rose and stood facing her, in shadow; dark and frock-coated, like a doctor.

“Yes; but I mean it shows that things look worse than they are.”

“That is most certainly a deduction that might be drawn. Nevertheless you suffered a most formidable *shock*.”

===Chinese===
“这真是一次最*不寻常*的心理体验。意识完全清醒地离死亡这么近，是难得碰上的。不过这种无所畏惧，在您身上想必是一种个人的特质。”

“可我*是*怕的。要紧的是你不会*一直*怕下去。您明白吗？”

“我听见您的话了。可只要还有一线生机，自我保存的本能就会强烈到如此地步”……

“可奇怪就在这儿；你身体里那场骚乱，有什么东西涌上来，不假思索地就把事情办了。”

“本能的神经反应。”

“可还有别的。在你*确信*自己就要被轧死的那一刻，死亡变了样。你等着，等之后的那一刻。”

“那是幻觉，是您体内生命的强力，它健康无恙时接受不了死亡。不过告诉我；您的胳膊肯定断了？”

“没有。轮子从肘弯上方压了过去。我没什么感觉，站起来时只晕了一小会儿，更多的是被围着的人群烦得要命。可同我一起骑车的两个人后来说，我的脸是灰的，眼睛*黑*得吓人。”

“那是休克。”他站起来，面对着她立在阴影里；一身黑衣长礼服，像个医生。

“是；可我的意思是，这说明事情看起来总比实际的糟。”

“这确实可以推出这样一个结论。不过您经受的是一次极其可怕的*休克*。”

===Original===
She moved towards the gas looking decisively up at it; and felt herself standing unexpressed, under the wide arch of all they had said. He must be told to remember to put out the gas before he went. That said, there was nothing in the world but a reluctant departure.

===Chinese===
她朝煤气灯走去，毅然抬头看它；只觉得自己站在他们所说的一切架起的宽阔拱顶之下，未曾把什么表达出来。必须告诉他，走之前记得把煤气关掉。这话一出，世上便只剩一场不情愿的告别了。

## III / III

===Original===
Three months ago the Christmas had been a goal for which she could hardly wait. It had offered her, this time, more than its usual safe deep firelit seclusion beyond which no future was visible. It was to pay her in full for having missed the beginning of Eve’s venture, taking her down into the midst of it when everything was in order and the beginnings still near enough to be remembered. But having remained during the engrossing months, forgotten, at the same far-distant point, Christmas now suddenly reared itself up a few days off, offering nothing but the shadow of an unavoidable interruption. For the first time she could see life going on beyond it. She would go down into its irrelevance, taking part in everything with absentminded animation, looking towards her return to town. It would not be Christmas, and the long days of forced absence threatened the features of the year that rose, far away and uncertain, beyond the obstruction.
===Chinese===
三个月前，圣诞节还是一个让她几乎等不及的目标。这一次，它许给她的，比往常那种安稳深沉、炉火融融的隐居更多——在那隐居之外，本来看不见任何将来。它要连本带利地补偿她错过伊芙事业开头的亏欠，等一切就绪、开端仍近得可以记起时，把她带到事情的中央。可是在那些全神贯注的月份里，她被忘在原来那个遥远的点上，如今圣诞节突然在几天之外赫然耸起，许给她的只剩一场无法回避的中断的影子。她第一次能够看见生活在它那一边继续进行。她将沉入那不相干的日子里，心不在焉却兴致勃勃地样样参加，眼睛望着自己回城的那一天。那将不是圣诞节，而这段被迫离去的漫长时日，威胁着一年中那些在阻碍之外远远升起、尚不确定的面貌。

===Original===
But the afternoon she came home with four days holiday in her hand, past and future were swept from her path. Tomorrow’s journey was a far-off appointment, her London friends remote shadows, banished from the endless continuance of life. She wandered about between Wimpole Street and St. Pancras, holding in imagination wordless converse with a stranger whose whole experience had melted and vanished like her own, into the flow of light down the streets; into the unending joy of the way the angles of buildings cut themselves out against the sky, glorious if she paused to survey them; and almost unendurably wonderful, keeping her hurrying on pressing, through insufficient silent outcries, towards something, anything, even instant death, if only they could be expressed when they moved with her movement, a maze of shapes, flowing, tilting into each other, in endless patterns, sharp against the light; sharing her joy in the changing same same song of the London traffic; the bliss of post-offices and railway stations, cabs going on and on towards unknown space; omnibuses rumbling securely from point to point, always within the magic circle of London.
===Chinese===
可是那天下午，当她手里攥着四天假期回到家时，过去与未来都从她的路上扫除了。明天的旅程成了远在天边的约会，她的伦敦朋友们成了遥远的影子，被放逐出生活无尽的绵延。她在温波尔街和圣潘克拉斯之间徘徊，在想象中与一个陌生人作无言的交谈——那人全部的经历也像她自己的一样，融化、消失在街道上倾泻的光流里；消失在那无休无止的欢愉中：建筑物的棱角把自身的轮廓剪刻在天幕上，她若驻足瞻望，它们便辉煌壮丽；而那几乎无法承受的美妙，又催她不断赶路、催迫着她，通过那些不充分的、无声的呐喊，奔向什么、奔向任何东西、甚至奔向即刻的死亡——只要它们，那一团迷宫般的形状，流动着、相互倾侧着，在无穷的图案里，逆着光棱角分明，能在随她一同移动时被表达出来；它们同她一起分享着伦敦车流那变化不息又永远同一的歌；分享着邮局与火车站的至福，一辆辆出租马车朝着未知的空间一路驶去；公共马车稳稳当当地从一点隆隆驶向另一点，永远在伦敦的魔圈之内。

===Original===
Her meal was a crowded dinner-party, all the people in the restaurant its guests, plunging with her, released from experience, unhaunted by hope or regret, into the endless beginning. Into the wrapped contemplation of the gathering, the thought of her visit flashed like a star, dropping towards her, and when she was gathering things together for her packing, her eagerness flamed up and lit her room.
===Chinese===
她的一餐饭成了一场宾客满座的晚宴，餐馆里的所有人都是它的来宾，同她一起，摆脱了经历、不受希望或悔恨的纠缠，纵身跃入那无尽的开始。在这场聚会全然的凝神之中，此行的念头像一颗星一闪，向她坠落下来；而当她把东西聚拢来收拾行装时，她的热望腾地燃起，照亮了她的房间。

===Original===
… The many Christmasses with the Brooms had been part of her long run of escape from the pain-shadowed family life; their house at first a dream-house in the unbroken dream of her own life in London, a shelter where agony was unknown, and lately a forgetfulness, for the long days of the holiday, of the challenge that lived in the walls of her room. For so long the walls had ceased to be the thrilled companions of her freedom, they had seen her endless evening hours of waiting for the next day to entangle her in its odious revolution. They had watched her in bleak daylight listening to life going on obliviously all round her, and scornfully sped her desperate excursions into other lives, greeting her empty glad return with the reminder that relief would fade, leaving her alone again with their unanswered challenge. They knew the recurring picture of a form, drifting, grey face upwards, under a featureless grey sky, in shallows, “unreached by the human tide” and had seen its realisation in her vain prayer that life should not pass her by; mocking the echoes of her cry, and waiting indifferent, serene with the years they knew before she came, for those that would follow her meaningless impermanence. When she lost the sense of herself in moments of gladness, or in the long intervals of thought that encircled her intermittent reading, they were all round her, waiting, ready to remind her, undeceived by her daily busy passing in and out, relentlessly counting its secret accumulating shame.
===Chinese===
……在布鲁姆家（the Brooms）度过的那许多个圣诞节，原是她从痛苦笼罩的家庭生活里长长逃亡的一部分；他们的房子起初是她伦敦生活未破之梦中的一座梦中之屋，一个不知痛苦为何物的庇护所，而近来则成了节日漫长白昼里的一剂忘忧散，让她忘掉她房间四壁里栖居的那个质问。有那么久了，这些墙壁早已不再是她自由的热切伴侣；它们看着她无尽的一个个夜晚，等待着第二天来把她缠进它可憎的轮回。它们看着她在惨淡的日光里，听着生活在她四周浑然忘我地进行，又轻蔑地目送她孤注一掷地远足到别人的生活里去，在她空落落、兴冲冲归来时提醒她：那点宽慰终会消散，她将再度独自面对它们那无人应答的质问。它们认得那幅反复出现的图景：一个身影在浅水里漂流，灰白的脸朝天，头顶是一片没有面貌的灰天，「人潮不及之处」（unreached by the human tide）；也亲眼看着它应验在她那徒劳的祷告里——求生活别从她身边一晃而过；它们讥笑她呼喊的回声，冷漠地等着，以她来之前便已熟识的岁月安然自处，等着那将接续她无谓之朝生暮死的岁月。每当她在欢愉的片刻里失去自我的知觉，或在环绕她断续阅读的漫长思想间隙里，它们就在她四周，等着，随时准备提醒她，不被她每日忙碌的进进出出所蒙蔽，无情地数着那秘密累积的羞耻。

===Original===
During the last three months they had not troubled her. They had become transparent, while the influence of her summer still had them at bay, to the glow shed up from the hours she had spent downstairs with Mrs. Bailey, and before there was time for them to close round her once more, the figure of Michael Shatov, with Europe stretching wide behind him, had forced them into companionship with all the walls in the world. She had been conscious that they waited for his departure; but it was far away out of sight, and when she should be once more alone with them, their attack would find her surrounded; lives lived alone within the vanquished walls of single poor bare rooms in every town in Europe would come visibly to her aid, driving her own walls back into dependence.
===Chinese===
这三个月里它们没有来搅扰她。它们变得透明了——在夏日余威的震慑下，又蒙着她在楼下与贝利太太共度的那些时辰向上映出的暖光；还没等它们重新围拢过来，迈克尔·沙托夫的身影，身后铺展着辽阔的欧洲，已经迫着它们与世上所有的墙壁结成了伙伴。她一直察觉它们在等他离开；可那还远得没有影儿，等到她再独自与它们相对时，它们的进攻会发现自己已被包围——欧洲每一座城镇里，那些在孤身一人、贫穷光秃的小房间里被驯服的墙壁之内度过的生活，都会现形来援助她，把她自己的墙壁逼回从属的地位。

===Original===
But tonight they were radiant. On no walls in the world could there be a brighter light. Streaming from their gaslit spaces, wherever she turned, was the wide brilliance that had been on everything in the days standing behind the shadow that had driven her into their enclosure. Eve and Harriett, waiting for her together, in a new sunlit life, were the full answer to their challenge. She was going *home*. The walls were traveller’s walls. That had been their first fascination; but they had known her only as a traveller; now as she dipped into the unbroken life that would flow round her with the sound of her sisters’ blended voices, they knew whence she came and what had been left behind. They saw her years of travel contract to a few easily afforded moments, lit though she had not known it, by light instreaming from the past and flowing now visibly ahead across the farther years.
===Chinese===
可是今晚它们光彩焕发。世上再没有哪里的墙壁能比它们更亮。无论她转向哪边，从它们煤气灯照明的空间里倾泻而出的，是那片宽阔的光辉——在把她驱进这四壁之中的阴影背后，那些日子里它曾罩在万事万物上。伊芙和哈丽特正一起在一种新的阳光生活里等她，这就是对它们那个质问的完满回答。她要*回家*了。这些墙壁是旅人的墙壁。那本是它们最初的迷人之处；可它们只认得作为旅人的她；如今她即将浸入那不曾中断的生活，它将带着姐妹们交融的语声在她周身流淌，它们于是知道了她从何处来、身后留下了什么。它们眼看她多年的旅居收缩成几个轻而易举便能承担的时刻，被从过去涌入、此刻又分明流向未来更远岁月的光照亮着——虽然她自己并不知道。

===Original===
The distant forgotten forms of the friends of her London life, turning away slighted, filled her, watching them, with a half-repenting solicitude. But they had their mysterious secret life, incomprehensible, but their own; they turned away towards each other and their own affairs, all of them set, at varying angles, unquestioningly towards a prospect she did not wish to share.
===Chinese===
她伦敦生活的那些朋友，遥远而被忘却的身影，正因受了冷落而转过身去；她望着他们，心里充满半是悔意的牵挂。可他们有自己神秘的隐秘生活，费解，却属于他们自己；他们转向彼此、转向各自的事务，全都以不同的角度、毫不迟疑地朝着一片她不想分享的前景。

===Original===
She went eagerly to sleep and woke in a few moments in a morning whose sounds coming through the open window, called to her as she leapt out towards them, for responsive demonstrations. Her desire to shout, thrilled to her feet, winged them.
===Chinese===
她带着急切沉入睡眠，片刻后便醒在一个清晨里；敞开的窗外传来的种种声响呼唤着她，她朝它们一跃而起，它们正要她作出呼应的表示。她那想呼喊的欲望直颤到脚底，给双脚插上了翅膀。

===Original===
Sitting decorously at the breakfast-table, she felt in equal relationship to all the bright assembly, holding off Mr. Shatov’s efforts to engage her in direct conversation, that she might hear, thoughtless and uncomprehending, the general sound of interwoven bright inflections echoing quietly out into the vast morning. She ran out into it, sending off her needless telegram for the joy of skimming over the well-known flags with endless time to spare. The echoing London sky poured down upon them the light of all the world. Within it her share gleamed dancing, given to her by the London years, the London life, shining now, far away, in multitudinous detail, the contemplated enviable life of a stranger.
===Chinese===
她端端正正坐在早餐桌旁，觉得自己与这一桌明亮的人群处于平等的关系里，挡开沙托夫先生想同她直接攀谈的努力，好让她能不假思索、不求甚解地听那交织的明亮语调汇成的总体声响，静静回荡进辽阔的清晨里去。她跑进这清晨，把那封本不必要的电报发了出去，只为享受踏着熟悉的石板轻快掠过的快乐，身后留着用不完的时间。回声阵阵的伦敦天空把全世界的光倾泻在他们身上。在这光里，她的那一份熠熠跃动，是伦敦的岁月、伦敦的生活赠给她的，此刻在远处闪亮，千头万绪，是一个被注视着的、令人艳羡的陌生人的生活。

===Original===
The third-class carriage was stuffy and cold, crowded with excited travellers whose separate eyes strove in vain to reach the heart of the occasion through a ceaseless exclamatory interchange about what lay just behind them and ahead at the end of the journey.﻿ ﻿… At some time, for some moments during the ensuing days, each one of them would be alone.﻿ ﻿… Consulting the many pairs of eyes, so different yet so strangely alike in their method of contemplation, so hindered and distracted, she felt, with a stifling pang of conviction, that their days would pass and bring no solitude, no single touch of realisation, and leave them going on, with eyes still quenched and glazed, striving outwards, now here now there, to reach some unapprehended goal.
===Chinese===
三等车厢又闷又冷，挤满了兴奋的旅人，一双双各怀心思的眼睛徒劳地想透过没完没了、大惊小怪的交谈——谈的尽是他们身后刚刚过去的事和旅程尽头的种种——触及这个日子的核心。……在往后的日子里，总会有某个时候，有那么片刻，他们中的每一个人都将独自一人。……她请教着这一双双眼睛，那么各不相同，凝视的方式却又那么出奇地一致，那么受阻、那么分神；她带着一种透不过气来的刺痛确信：他们的日子将一天天过去，不会带来独处，不会带来一丝一毫的领悟，只留下他们继续走下去，眼睛依旧黯淡无光，一味向外使劲，忽而这里忽而那里，想去够某个永不能领会的目标。

===Original===
Immersing herself in her corner she saw nothing more until Eve’s face appeared in the crowd waiting upon the seaside platform. Eve beamed welcome and eager wordless communications and turned at once to lead the way through the throng. They hurried, separated by Miriam’s hand-luggage, silenced by the din of the traffic rattling over the cobblestones, meeting and parting amongst the thronging pedestrians, down the steep slope of the narrow street until Eve turned, with a piloting backward glance, and led the way along the cobbled pavement of a side-street, still narrower and sloping even more steeply downhill. It was deserted, and as they went single-file along the narrow pavement, Miriam caught in the distance, the unwonted sound of the winter sea. She had not thought of the sea as part of her visit, and lost herself in the faint familiar roll and flump of the south-coast tide. It was enough. The holiday came and passed in the imagined sight of the waves tumbling in over the grey beach, and the breaking of the brilliant seaside light upon the varying house-fronts behind the promenade; she returned restored; the prize of far-off London renewed already, keenly, within her hands, to find Eve standing still just ahead, turned towards her; smiling too breathlessly for speech. They were in front of a tiny shopfront, slanting with the steep slant of the little road. The window was full of things set close to the panes on narrow shelves. Miriam stood back, pouring out her appreciation. It *was* perfect; just as she had imagined it; exactly the little shop she had dreamed of keeping when she was a child. She felt a pang of envy.
===Chinese===
她把自己浸进车厢一角，直到伊芙的脸出现在海边月台上等候的人群里，才又看见了什么。伊芙满面笑容地表示欢迎，热切地传递着无声的话语，随即转身领路，穿过人群。她们匆匆走着，被米丽亚姆的手提行李隔开，被鹅卵石路上辚辚的车马声搅得无法交谈，在摩肩接踵的行人里聚了又散，顺着窄街陡峭的坡道一路向下，直到伊芙带着领路人的一瞥回望，拐进一条小巷的鹅卵石人行道，路更窄，下坡更陡。巷子里空无一人，当她们沿着窄窄的人行道鱼贯而行时，米丽亚姆远远捕捉到冬日大海不同寻常的声音。她原本没想到此行会有大海，此刻不禁迷失在南岸潮水那微弱而熟悉的翻滚与扑落里。这就够了。假期来而复去，都在想象的望见里：海浪翻卷着涌上灰色的海滩，明艳的海滨之光折在滨海步道后面高高低低的房屋正面；她休整归来；遥远伦敦的那份奖赏已然鲜亮地重新握在手中——却发现伊芙停在前面不远，转过身朝着她，笑得喘不过气来说不出话。她们站在一间小小的店面跟前，店面随着小路的陡坡倾斜着。橱窗里满满当当地摆着东西，紧贴玻璃，搁在窄窄的架子上。米丽亚姆退后一步，把她的赞叹倾倒出来。这*真是*完美；正如她想象的那样；正是她小时候梦想着要开的那间小店。她感到一阵艳羡的刺痛。

===Original===
“*Mine*” said Eve blissfully “my own.” Eve had property; fragile delicate *Eve*, the problem of the family. This was her triumph. Miriam hurried, lest her thoughts should become visible, to glance up and down the street and exclaim the perfection of the situation.

“I know,” said Eve with dreamy tenderness, “and it’s all my own; the shop and the house; all mine.” Miriam’s eyes rose fearfully. Above the shop, a narrow strip of bright white plaster house shot up, two storeys high; charming, in the way it was complete, a house, and yet the whole of it, with a strip of sky above, and the small neat pavement below, in your eye at once, and beside it right and left, the irregular heights and widths of the small houses, close-built and flush with the edge of the little pavement, up and down the hill. But the thought of the number of rooms inside the little building brought, together with her longing to see them, a sense of the burden of possessions, and her envy disappeared. While she cried you’ve got a *house*, she wondered, scanning Eve’s radiant slender form, whence she drew with all her apparent helplessness, the strength to face such formidable things.
===Chinese===
“*我的*，”伊芙喜滋滋地说，“我自己的。”伊芙有了产业；纤弱娇贵的*伊芙*，家里的难题。这是她的凯旋。米丽亚姆赶紧抬眼把街上下打量一遭、赞叹这位置再好不过，生怕自己的心思被人看穿。

“我知道，”伊芙带着朦胧的温柔说，“而且全是我自己的；店和房子；全是我的。”米丽亚姆的眼睛怯怯地抬起来。店铺上方，一条窄窄的亮白灰泥房子拔地而起，两层高；迷人，在于它的完整，是一幢房子，却又整个儿——头顶一线天，脚下小巧齐整的人行道——一览无余；左右两旁，小房子高高低低、宽宽窄窄，鳞次栉比，与窄人行道的边缘齐平，顺着山坡上上下下。可一想到这小楼里房间的数目，连同想看看它们的渴望，一并涌来的是一份产业之累的感觉，她的艳羡便消失了。她喊着你有了一幢*房子*呀，一面打量伊芙容光焕发的苗条身影，暗自纳罕：她这样一副全然无助的样子，是从哪里得来面对这般可怕事物的力气。

===Original===
“I’ve let the two rooms over the shop. I live at the top.” As she exclaimed on the implied wealth, Miriam found her envy wandering back in the thought of the two rooms under the sky, well away from the shop in another world, the rest of the house securely cared for by other people. She moved to the window. “All the right things,” she murmured, from her shocked survey of the rows of light green bottles filled with sweets, the boxes of soap, cigarettes, clay pipes, bootlaces, jewellery pinned to cards, crackers and tightly packed pink and white muslin Christmas stockings. Between the shelves she saw the crowded interior of the little shop, a strip of counter, a man with rolled up shirt sleeves, busily twisting a small screw of paper.﻿ ﻿… *Gerald*.
===Chinese===
“店面楼上那两间我租出去了。我住在顶上。”她为这话里暗含的富足惊叹时，米丽亚姆的艳羡却又回到那两间天空底下的房间里去了——离铺子远远的，在另一个世界里，房子的其余部分由别人稳妥地照管着。她走到橱窗前。“样样都是对路的货，”她低声说——她那受了惊的目光扫过一排排浅绿色糖果瓶、成盒的肥皂、香烟、陶土烟斗、鞋带、别在纸卡上的首饰、彩炮，还有塞得紧紧的粉白平纹纱圣诞袜。货架之间，她望见小店里拥挤的内容，一条窄窄的柜台，一个卷着衬衫袖口的男人正忙着拧一小纸包。……*杰拉尔德*（Gerald）。

===Original===
“Come inside,” said Eve from the door.

“Hullo, Mirry, what d’you think of the emporium?” Gerald, his old easy manner, his smooth polished gentle voice, his neat, iron handshake across the mean little counter, gave Eve’s enterprise the approval of all the world. “I’ve done up enough screws of tea to last you the whole blessed evening,” he went on from the midst of Miriam’s exclamations “and at least twenty people have been in since you left.” A little door flew open in the wall just behind him and Harriett, in an overall, stood at the top of a short flight of stairs, leaping up and down in the doorway. Miriam ran round behind the counter, freely, Eve’s shop, their shop, behind her. “Hulloh old silly,” beamed Harriett kissing and shaking her “I just rushed down, can’t stay a minute, I’m in the middle of nine dinners, they’re all leaving tomorrow and you’re to come and sleep with *us*.” She fled down the steps, out through the shop and away up the hill, with a rousing attack on Gerald as she passed him leaning with Eve over the till. Miriam was welcomed. The fact of her visit was more to Harriett than her lodgers. She collected her belongings and carried them up the steps past a small dark flight of stairs into a dark little room. A small fire was burning in a tiny kitchen range; a candle guttered on the mantelpiece in the draught from the shop; there was no window and the air of the room was close with the combined odours of the things crowded into the small space. She went back into the bright familiar shop. Gerald was leaving; see you tomorrow he called from the door with his smile.
===Chinese===
“进来呀，”伊芙在门口说。

“哈罗，米丽（Mirry），你觉得我这百货商场怎么样？”杰拉尔德，还是那副从容的老样子，那圆润文雅、打磨得光光的嗓音，隔着寒酸的小柜台那干净利落、铁一般坚定的一握，给了伊芙的事业全世界的认可。“我包好了一包包茶叶，够你们快快活活用一晚上，”他在米丽亚姆的连声惊叹中继续说，“你走了以后至少进来过二十个人。”他身后墙上一扇小门倏地开了，哈丽特系着罩衫站在一小截楼梯顶上，在门口一蹦一跳。米丽亚姆绕到柜台后面，自由自在地——伊芙的店，她们的店——把它留在身后。“哈罗，傻东西，”哈丽特满面春风地亲她、摇她，“我刚冲下来的，一分钟也待不了，正做着九份晚饭呢，他们明天全走，你来跟*我们*睡。”她顺着楼梯飞奔下去，穿过店面跑上山坡去了，路过正和伊芙俯身对着钱柜的杰拉尔德身边时，还响亮地抢白了他一句。米丽亚姆受到了欢迎。她来这件事，在哈丽特心里比她的房客们要紧。她收拾好自己的东西，提上台阶，经过一段黑黢黢的小楼梯，走进一间黑黑的小屋。小小的厨炉里燃着一小炉火；一支蜡烛在壁炉台上被店堂的穿堂风吹得泪痕斑斑；屋里没有窗，挤在小空间里的种种东西，气味混作一团，闷在空气里。她回到亮堂堂的熟识的店里。杰拉尔德正要走；明儿见，他在门口带着他那种微笑喊道。

===Original===
“*Now*; I’ll light the lamp and we’ll be cosy,” said Eve leading the way back into the little room. Miriam waited impatiently for the lamp to make a live centre in the crowded gloom. The little black kitchen fire was intolerable as president of Eve’s leisure. But the dim lamp, standing low on a little table, made the room gloomier and Eve was back in the shop with a customer. Only the dingy little table, a battered tray bearing the remains of a hasty, shabby tea, the fall below it of a faded ugly fringed tablecloth and a patch of threadbare carpet, were clearly visible.﻿ ﻿… She could not remove her attention from them.
===Chinese===
“*这下*好了；我点起灯，咱们就舒服了，”伊芙说着领路回到小屋里。米丽亚姆不耐烦地等着灯光在这拥挤的幽暗里燃起一个活生生的中心。那小小的黑色厨炉，作为伊芙闲暇的主宰，叫人无法忍受。可是那盏暗淡的灯低低地立在一张小桌上，反倒把屋子照得更暗了，而伊芙已经回店里招呼一位顾客去了。只有那张邋遢的小桌还看得清楚：一只碰得坑坑洼洼的托盘，盛着一顿匆忙寒酸的茶点剩下的残局，桌子下面垂着一幅褪了色的、难看的流苏桌布，还有一块磨秃了绒的地毯。……她的注意力没法从这些东西上挪开。

===Original===
Lying sleepless by Eve’s side late that night, she watched the pictures that crowded the darkness. Her first moments in the little back room were far away. The small dark bedroom was full of the last picture of Eve, in her nightgown, quietly relentless after explaining that she always kept the window shut because plenty of air came in, taking a heavy string of large blue beads out of her top drawer, to put them in readiness with tomorrow’s dress. No; I don’t think that a bit; and if I *were* a savage, I should hang myself all over with beads and *love* it. She had spoken with such *conviction*﻿ ﻿… Up here, with her things arranged round her as she had had them at home and in her bedroom at the Greens’, she kept her life as it had always been. She was still her unchanged self, but her freedom was giving her the strength to be sure of her opinions. It was as if she had been saying all the evening with long accumulating preparedness, holding her poise throughout the interruptions of customers and down into the details of the story of her adventures, *Yes* I know your opinions, I have heard them all my life, and now I’m out in the world myself and can meet everybody as an equal, and say what I think, without wondering whether it suits my part as the Greens’ governess. She had got her strength from the things she had done. It was amazing to think of her summoning courage to break again with the Greens and borrowing from them to start in business, Mr. Green “setting his heart” on the success of the little shop and meaning to come down and see how it was getting on. How awful it would be if it did not get on.﻿ ﻿… But it was getting on.﻿ ﻿… How terrifying it must have been at first not knowing the price of anything in the shop or what to buy for it﻿ ﻿… and then, customers telling her the prices of things and where they were kept, and travellers being *kind*; respectful and friendly and ready to go out of their way to do *anything*﻿ ﻿… that was the other side of Maupassant’s “*hourrah pour la petite difference*” *commis voyageurs*﻿ ﻿… and well-to-do people in the neighbourhood rushing in for some little thing, taken aback to find a lady behind the counter, and coming again for all sorts of things.﻿ ﻿… Eve would become like one of those middle-aged women shopkeepers in books, in the country, with a kind heart and a sarcastic tongue, seeing through everybody and having the same manner for the vicar and a ploughman, or a rather nicer manner for a ploughman. *No.* Eve was still sentimental.﻿ ﻿…
===Chinese===
夜深了，她躺在伊芙身边睡不着，看着黑暗里涌挤上来的幅幅图景。她初进那间后屋小房的片刻已远在天边。这间黑黑的小卧室里，满是伊芙最后的形象：她穿着睡衣，刚解释完自己总是关着窗、因为空气照样进得来，那份安静的斩钉截铁还没散，便从最上层的抽屉里取出一大串沉甸甸的蓝色大珠子，预备着搭配明天的衣裳。不；我一点也不这么想；我要是*个野蛮人*，我就把浑身上下挂满珠子，还*爱*得不行。她说话时那份*笃定*……在这上面，她的东西在四下里摆好，如同在家里、在格林家（the Greens）她卧房里那样，她把自己的生活保持着原样。她还是那个没有变的她，可她的自由正给她底气，让她敢确信自己的见解。她仿佛整晚都在说，带着久经积蓄的从容有备，任凭顾客的打岔也不失分寸，一路说到她种种奇遇的细节：*是的*，我知道你们的见解，我听了一辈子了，如今我自己也到世上来闯了，能同人人平起平坐，想到什么就说什么，不必再琢磨它合不合我格林家女教师的身份。她的力气是从她做过的事情里得来的。想想真叫人惊叹：她鼓起勇气再同格林家分了手，还向他们借钱做本钱开店，格林先生「一心」盼着小店兴隆，还打算下来看看经营得怎么样。若是经营不下去，那该多可怕。……可它正蒸蒸日上。……起初一定吓人，店里样样东西的价钱都不知道，也不知道该进什么货……后来，顾客们把东西的价钱、搁在哪里一五一十告诉她，跑外的推销员们又*和气*；恭敬、友好，甘愿特地多绕些路来*帮忙*……这是莫泊桑那句「hourrah pour la petite difference」（为那小小的差异三呼万岁）的另一面——*commis voyageurs*（旅行推销员）们……还有附近的有钱人急着进来买点小东西，猛然发现柜台后站着一位淑女，怔了一怔，后来便什么都要来买了。……伊芙会变成书里写的那种乡村中年女店主，心肠好，嘴巴刻薄，把人人看穿，对牧师和对庄稼人一副腔调——或者对庄稼人还要客气些。*不。*伊芙还是多愁善感的。……

===Original===
Those wonderful letters were a bridge; a promise for the future.﻿ ﻿… They were the letters of a boy; *that* was the struggling impression she had not been able to convey. She could start the day well by telling Eve that in the morning. They were the letters of a youth in love for the first time in his life﻿ ﻿… and he had *fifteen* grandchildren. “So wonderful when you think of that old, old man” had not expressed it at all. They were wonderful for *anybody*. Page after page, all breathing out the way things shine when the sense of someone who is not there, is there all the time. Eve knew what it had meant to him; “age makes no difference.” Then might life suddenly shine like that at any moment, right up to the end.﻿ ﻿… And it made Eve so wonderful; having no idea, all those years, and thinking him just a very kind old man to come, driving, almost from his deathbed, with a little rose-tree in the carriage for her. It was so perfect that he wrote only after she had gone, and he knew he was dying; a youth in love for the first time. If there were a future life he would be watching, for Eve to walk gently in crowned with song and making everything sing all round her.﻿ ﻿… But what of the wife, and of Eve’s future husband?
